**Summary.** This paper details the theoretical control of collective quantum states in arrays of emitters coupled to a waveguide. By applying precise frequency shifts, the authors demonstrate how to engineer perfect single-excitation dark states that are immune to decay. This technique is crucial for building robust quantum information storage and networking devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses controlling dissipation in open quantum systems, which is central to quantum memory and quantum networking using solid-state emitters.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is stabilizing perfect single-excitation and near-perfect multi-excitation dark states in arrays of quantum emitters coupled to a waveguide.

**Main result.** Optimal frequency shifts allow for the deterministic preparation of single-excitation dark states, and near-perfect multi-excitation dark states can be achieved by treating the system as coupled subsystems.

**Method.** The analysis uses the master equation and Hamiltonian diagonalization to find states that decouple from the dissipative environment, requiring precise tuning of emitter detunings.

**Model / system.** The system consists of N two-level emitters (qubits) coupled to a one-dimensional photonic waveguide terminated by a mirror. Dynamics are governed by a Lindbladian evolution incorporating coherent and dissipative couplings.

**Key observables.** Dark state population ($p_D$), decay rate ($\gamma$), and the error ($\epsilon$) in state preparation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Optimal relative detuning ($\Delta_{opt}$), number of emitters ($N$), and the strength of external driving fields ($\Omega$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The Markov approximation is used, and for some analyses, adiabatic elimination is employed under the assumption that driving fields are weak compared to decay rates.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the array setup, the decay paths in the Lindbladian spectrum, and the error ($\epsilon$) in photon emission for two-emitter systems under noise.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the general theory of dark states in coupled emitters, detailing the stabilization mechanism for single excitations via detuning, generalizing to multi-excitations, and finally analyzing the robustness of the preparation protocol against noise.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The radiative properties of quantum emitters are profoundly influenced by their electromagnetic environment. When coupled to a waveguide terminated at one end by a mirror, distant emitters interact strongly via virtual photon exchange, leading to collective superradiant and subradiant states with enhanced or suppressed decay rates. We demonstrate that applying optimal frequency shifts to each emitter enables the formation of perfect single-excitation dark states (i.e. states with zero decay rate) and near-perfect multi-excitation dark states in small ensembles. These collective states can be deterministically prepared with high fidelity using classical driving fields or few-photon pulses propagating along the waveguide. These results, readily implementable in superconducting qubit platforms, open new avenues for quantum information storage, networking and control of light.

</details>

**Summary.** This work presents a novel approach to nanoscale magnetometry using strongly interacting electron spins in diamond NV centers. By coherently controlling collective many-body dynamics via Overhauser-Antiferromagnetic Tunneling (OAT), the authors achieve significant signal amplification and demonstrate the ability to map spatially correlated magnetic noise down to 50 nm. This opens pathways for advanced quantum sensing in biological and material characterization.

**Why it may be interesting.** The use of collective dynamics and engineered spin interactions in a solid-state platform like NV centers provides a concrete, experimentally realized example of how many-body physics can enhance quantum sensing beyond the standard quantum limit.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of achieving practical, high-gain nanoscale magnetic sensing by leveraging the collective many-body dynamics of interacting quantum spins.

**Main result.** They successfully demonstrate metrological gains up to 8.8 dB for noise sensing and achieve momentum-space-resolved detection of spatially correlated magnetic noise down to 50 nm.

**Method.** The core method involves engineering Overhauser-Antiferromagnetic Tunneling (OAT) dynamics using pulsed magnetic field gradients to coherently control and amplify the sensing signal.

**Model / system.** The system is a high-density ensemble of Nitrogen-Vacancy (NV) centers in diamond, whose dynamics are governed by magnetic dipole-dipole interactions, modeled via collective spin-wave dynamics.

**Key observables.** Metrological gain (dB), spatial resolution (nm), spin polarization dynamics, and the response function $F(\vec{k}_n, \vec{K})$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The sensing gain (e.g., 7.9 dB for signal, 8.8 dB for noise), the minimum detectable length scale (50 nm), and the interaction strength parameter $J_0$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis accounts for experimental overheads, and theoretical derivations rely on mean-field approximations and specific symmetries in the spin-wave model.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental setup, the multi-stage signal amplification protocol (OAT), and the theoretical prediction of momentum-space response functions $F(\vec{k}_n, \vec{K})$ showing wavevector selectivity.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from demonstrating the physical system and the need for interaction enhancement, detailing the OAT protocol for signal amplification, and culminating in a theoretical analysis of the momentum-space response function to characterize spatially correlated noise.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Many-body dynamics constitutes a promising approach for creating correlations between quantum particles which can be used for applications in sensing and metrology. However, utilizing this potential for substantial gains in practical settings is a challenging task with only a very few applications realized to date. Here, we demonstrate an approach to nanoscale magnetic sensing enabled by strongly interacting electronic spins in a room temperature solid. By coherently controlling collective many-body dynamics of a dipolar ensemble of $\sim 10^4$ nitrogen-vacancy (NV) centres in diamond with pulsed magnetic field gradients, we demonstrate practical metrological gain up to $7.9(2)\,\mathrm{dB}$ for magnetic signal detection and $8.8(3)\,\mathrm{dB}$ for magnetic noise sensing, fully accounting for experimental overheads. Finally, we combine these methods to demonstrate a momentum-space-resolved sensing modality that enables detection of spatially correlated magnetic noise at continuously tunable length scales down to 50 nanometers. These observations open the door toward practical applications of interaction-enhanced quantum sensors for nanoscale biological imaging and material characterization.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper shows that Boundary Time Crystals (BTCs) can be robustly realized in coupled atomic ensembles even when the driving is weak and the system is detuned from resonance. This is achieved by exploiting how shared dissipation organizes the system's dynamics into low-dissipation channels. The theoretical analysis uses semiclassical and spectral methods, demonstrating the feasibility of these exotic quantum states in physical platforms like waveguide-QED.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful theoretical framework for realizing non-equilibrium quantum phenomena like BTCs using realistic, weakly driven, and detuned systems, which is highly relevant for quantum optics and open quantum systems research.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to establish conditions for robust Boundary Time Crystals (BTCs) in driven-dissipative systems, overcoming the strict requirements of strong driving and exact resonance found in conventional models.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that coupling two atomic ensembles allows BTCs to persist under weak driving and finite detuning by organizing dissipation into dynamically accessible low-dissipation channels.

**Method.** The analysis employs mean-field approximations, semiclassical WKB methods, and the study of the Liouvillian spectrum to track the system's dynamics in the thermodynamic limit.

**Model / system.** The system consists of two atomic ensembles (spin-1/2 particles) coupled to a common Markovian reservoir, modeled by a master equation. The dynamics are analyzed on an effective lattice structure, which is later mapped to a waveguide-QED platform.

**Key observables.** Order parameters ($m_z$, $o$), the two-time correlation function $	ext{Re}[C(t)]$, and the low-lying Liouvillian spectrum.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Driving strength ($\eta$), detuning ($\Delta$), dissipation rate ($\gamma$), and the system size ($N_a$ or $S$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the Born–Markov approximation and the mean-field approximation, which are valid in the thermodynamic limit ($N_a 	o \infty$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate mean-field evolution of order parameters under varying driving/detuning, semiclassical phase space limit cycles, and the low-lying Liouvillian spectrum showing spectral gaps.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from analyzing the single-ensemble case to the two-ensemble model, deriving semiclassical equations of motion, and finally confirming the results using the full Liouvillian spectrum analysis and mapping the model to a waveguide-QED setup.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Spontaneous breaking of continuous time-translation symmetry in driven-dissipative systems gives rise to boundary time crystals (BTCs), characterized by persistent oscillations sustained by coherent driving and collective dissipation. Conventional BTCs, however, typically require strong driving and exact atom-drive resonance, imposing stringent constraints on their realization. Here we consider two atomic ensembles coupled to a common Markovian reservoir and show that shared dissipation organizes dissipation-free and low-dissipation modes into dynamically accessible low-dissipation channels, enabling BTCs under weak driving and finite detuning. Finite detuning further selects a unique stable limit cycle from an initial-state-dependent family of oscillatory trajectories. Our results establish low-dissipation dynamical channels as a route to robust BTCs under relaxed driving and resonance conditions.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a gauge-preserving framework to study thermalization and string dynamics in open non-Abelian lattice gauge theories. By coupling the SU(2) LGT to a thermal environment, the authors find that dissipation causes string melting and that the thermalization time depends non-monotonically on the coupling strength. These results establish a key theoretical understanding of non-Abelian plasma dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, non-Abelian example of open quantum system dynamics, bridging concepts from quantum information (open systems) with high-energy physics (QCD/LGTs).

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how the non-Abelian gauge structure modifies dissipative, real-time dynamics in open lattice gauge theories.

**Main result.** The thermalization time exhibits non-monotonic behavior with environment coupling, and the non-Abelian structure yields a mesonic bias in the steady state, contrasting with Abelian models.

**Method.** The dynamics are solved using tensor networks applied to a Lindblad master equation derived in the Quantum Brownian Motion limit.

**Model / system.** The system is a 1+1D SU(2) Yang-Mills Lattice Gauge Theory coupled to dynamical matter and an external thermal scalar environment via a gauge-preserving Lindblad evolution.

**Key observables.** String melting, thermalization time, mesonic bias, and chromoelectric flux screening.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Environment coupling strength, temperature, and the specific non-Abelian gauge group SU(2).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The environment is treated via a Lindblad evolution, and the analysis assumes the QBM limit and a color-singlet coupling to maintain gauge invariance.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the open-system dynamics, showing the interaction between the SU(2) LGT subsystem and the thermal environment, and detailing the use of Tensor Network methods for simulation.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the model (subsystem, environment, interaction), derives the Lindblad master equation in the QBM limit, and then presents results concerning string melting, thermalization times, and matter composition.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Open-system lattice gauge theory (LGT) has so far been developed predominantly in Abelian settings, leaving open how genuinely non-Abelian gauge structure reshapes dissipative real-time dynamics. Here, we study a $1+1$D SU(2) Yang--Mills LGT with dynamical matter coupled to a thermal scalar environment through a gauge-preserving Lindblad evolution, which we solve using tensor networks. Starting from a quark--antiquark pair connected by a chromoelectric flux string, we find that the thermal medium melts the string by delocalizing the color charges and screening the flux; on resonance, this dissipative melting competes with and delays coherent string breaking. The thermalization time is non-monotonic in the environment coupling, decreasing through environment-assisted transport at weak dissipation before increasing in a quantum-Zeno regime. In the strong-dephasing limit, a Schrieffer--Wolff expansion maps the dynamics to a classical exclusion process and yields the Liouvillian thermalization time analytically. Beyond these generic open-system effects, the non-Abelian matter structure produces a systematic mesonic bias in the steady state, while the thermalization time decreases with temperature, in contrast to the Abelian Schwinger model trend and in qualitative agreement with pNRQCD studies of the quark--gluon plasma. These results establish a gauge-preserving framework for thermalization and string dynamics in open non-Abelian lattice gauge theories.

</details>

**Summary.** This research explores discrete time-crystalline phases in disordered Heisenberg spin chains driven periodically. Using advanced MPS simulations, the authors confirm the DTC signature but show that increasing the driving error suppresses this phase. The study maps out an intermediate dynamical regime characterized by localization signatures, providing a detailed diagnostic toolkit for non-equilibrium quantum matter.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses non-equilibrium quantum phases (time crystals) in the presence of disorder, bridging concepts from many-body localization, Floquet engineering, and quantum criticality, which are highly relevant to modern AMO and condensed matter research.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the existence and stability of discrete time-crystalline (DTC) behavior in strongly disordered anisotropic Heisenberg spin chains subjected to periodic driving.

**Main result.** DTC behavior is observed at zero driving error, characterized by period-doubled oscillations, but this response is suppressed by increasing the rotation error, leading to a regime reminiscent of Anderson localization.

**Method.** The study employs extensive numerical simulations using Matrix Product States (MPS) and Matrix Product Operators (MPO) to analyze time evolution.

**Model / system.** The system is a one-dimensional disordered spin-1/2 XXZ Heisenberg chain driven by global $\pi$-kicks. The Hamiltonian includes nearest-neighbor interactions and random on-site fields.

**Key observables.** Two-time imbalance correlation function ($G_2(0, t)$), Entanglement entropy, Quantum Fisher information, and Restricted-control ergotropy.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The rotation-angle error ($\epsilon$), disorder strength ($W$), and the ratio of interaction couplings ($J_z/J_{\perp}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the system can be accurately described using MPS techniques and the two-site time-dependent variational principle (2TDVP).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the time evolution of the imbalance correlation function $G_2(0, t)$ and its dependence on the driving cycle $n$, comparing DTC signatures against localization signatures across varying error parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the theoretical framework of spontaneous symmetry breaking in Floquet systems to numerical simulations on the disordered spin chain, analyzing the robustness of the DTC phase against controlled errors and comparing diagnostics across different physical regimes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A discrete time crystal is an out-of-equilibrium phase of matter characterized by the spontaneous breaking of discrete time-translation symmetry. Using extensive numerical simulations based on matrix-product-state methods, we provide evidence for discrete time-crystalline behavior in strongly disordered spin chains with Heisenberg interactions, including the isotropic point, subject to periodic driving. Starting from a many-body localized regime, we observe that rotations induced by delta kicks produce a pronounced subharmonic response at half the drive frequency in spin observables. We investigate the stability of this response against rotation-angle errors through entanglement entropy, quantum Fisher information, short-range spin correlations, and restricted-control ergotropy. Increasing the rotation error reveals an intermediate dynamical regime separating the time-crystalline and Floquet-localized responses. In this regime, most observables exhibit signatures of weakly correlated dynamics reminiscent of Anderson localization.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes the effective Hamiltonian for a monitored Majorana chain, revealing that the dynamics lead to long-range hoppings with non-trivial correlations. The key finding is that these correlations are responsible for an enhanced, $[\ln(L)]^2$ scaling of the entanglement entropy, a signature rarely seen in conventional isolated quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete example of how non-trivial correlations in the effective Hamiltonian, arising from measurement processes, can fundamentally alter entanglement scaling beyond what is predicted by standard uncorrelated disorder models.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the effective Hamiltonians governing the monitored dynamics of a 1D Majorana chain, focusing on unconventional entanglement scaling in the gapless phase.

**Main result.** The entanglement entropy scales as $[\ln(L)]^2$ when non-trivial correlations are present in the long-range hopping hoppings, contrasting with the $\ln(L)$ scaling found when these correlations are absent.

**Method.** The analysis employs Lyapunov spectral analysis to derive the effective Hamiltonian from measurement-induced dynamics, followed by constructing correlated random power-law hopping models for comparison.

**Model / system.** The system is a one-dimensional Majorana chain undergoing alternating unitary evolution and parity measurements. The dynamics are captured by an effective Hamiltonian derived via Lyapunov spectral analysis.

**Key observables.** Spectral gap closing ($	ext{faster than } 1/L 	ext{ but slower than } 1/L^2$) and entanglement entropy scaling ($[\ln(L)]^2$ vs. $\ln(L)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** System size $L$, measurement frequency $\omega_t$, and the strength of hopping correlations.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses on a gapless phase, and the effective Hamiltonian average is zero, preventing simple weak disorder treatment.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the gap closing behavior, the algebraic decay of hopping magnitudes, and the Pearson correlation coefficients between different hopping terms.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by deriving the effective Hamiltonian using Lyapunov analysis, characterizing its structure (random, long-range, correlated), and then comparing the entanglement scaling predicted by models with and without hopping correlations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the structures of effective Hamiltonians governing monitored dynamics of a one-dimensional Majorana chain through the Lyapunov spectral analysis. We focus on a gapless phase characterized by finite-size scalings different from those in conventional critical and/or frustration-free systems; the spectral gap closing faster than $1/L$ but slower than $1/L^2$ and the entanglement entropy growing as $[\ln(L)]^2$ with $L$ being the system size. We find that the corresponding effective Hamiltonians have random long-range power-law hoppings with nontrivial magnitude correlations, rather than being independently and identically distributed. To elucidate the role of these non-Gaussian correlations, we construct random power-law hopping models that capture the essential features of the effective Hamiltonians. The spectral gaps of the constructed models decay faster than $1/L$ but slower than $1/L^2$. We find that, in the absence of hopping correlations, the ground-state entanglement exhibits $\ln(L)$ scaling. In the presence of correlations, by contrast, the entanglement entropy is enhanced and its system-size dependence is consistent with $[\ln(L)]^2$ scaling over the system sizes studied. These results suggest that correlations among long-range hopping magnitudes are responsible for the entanglement scaling that seldom appears in ground states of conventional isolated quantum systems.

</details>

**Authors:** W. Callum Wareham, David A. Sivak  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.03217v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** 🔥 `methods for driven-dissipative` **4/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **3/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **3/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **2/5** · `quantum measurements` **2/5** · `Frenkel-Kontorova` **1/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5**

<sub>FIG. 1. Schematic of the simple models for a molecular ma- chine considered in this paper. **Summary.** This work extends optimal control theory to periodic, non-equilibrium mesoscopic systems, modeling molecular motors using Fokker-Planck dynamics. By employing automatic differentiation, the authors derive design principles showing that efficient operation requires minimizing mechanical heat by maintaining constant distribution rotation. This provides a quantitative tool for understanding energy efficiency in complex biological machines.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous application of optimal control theory and automatic differentiation to periodically driven, non-equilibrium stochastic systems provides a powerful computational framework applicable to open quantum systems where dissipation and driving are key.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Designing efficient, periodic, nonequilibrium control strategies for mesoscopic molecular machines that operate under strong fluctuations.

**Main result.** Optimal protocols minimize mechanical waste heat by ensuring the probability distribution rotates at a constant speed without changing its shape, and can reduce chemical heat loss.

**Method.** The study employs automatic differentiation applied to Fokker-Planck simulations to calculate gradients for optimizing control potentials $\lambda(t)$ over periodic time cycles.

**Model / system.** The model is a rotary molecular motor treated as a Brownian particle diffusing on a periodic angular coordinate, governed by a Langevin equation and analyzed via the Fokker-Planck equation.

**Key observables.** Total heat ($\langle Q angle = \langle Q_{	ext{mech}} angle + \langle Q_{	ext{chem}} angle$), output work ($\langle W_{	ext{out}} angle$), and the probability distribution $p_t(x)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The control protocol $\lambda(t)$ is assumed to be time-periodic, and the analysis focuses on the Periodic Steady State (PSS) in the long-time limit.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the control protocol is periodic and that the system can be effectively analyzed in the PSS regime.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates energy flows and potential structures; Figure 10 compares wasted energy across different control protocols (Fokker-Planck vs. Langevin); Figure 11 compares optimization strategies based on minimizing input work versus mechanical heat.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the model using Langevin/Fokker-Planck equations, develops the mathematical framework using coordinate transformations and the Floquet ansatz for periodic solutions, and then applies optimal control theory to derive and test design principles for minimizing heat dissipation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Biological molecular machines are mesoscopic systems that act repeatedly and periodically to perform important cellular tasks while contending with strong fluctuations and operating in an overdamped regime. Optimal control theory is a tool that can be used to understand the design principles behind efficient operation of these machines; however, most studies on optimal control of classical mesoscopic systems have focused on control problems that do not repeat periodically. Here, we automatically differentiate Fokker-Planck simulations to design efficient nonequilibrium control strategies for simple models of periodic molecular machines with and without explicit changes in the machine's chemical state. The designed protocols and theoretical analysis provide insight into the design principles governing efficient driving in these nonequilibrium systems. Designed control protocols should seek to reduce mechanical heat by rotating the entire angular probability distribution at a constant speed without changing its shape, and should reduce chemical heat by reducing the proportion of chemical transitions with large heat.

</details>

**Summary.** This work provides a unified theoretical framework for analyzing optimal control of stochastic systems, classifying the resulting problems into three mathematical types. It establishes a deep connection between the minimum cost required for optimal control and the rate function governing non-equilibrium relaxation after a quench. The theory is validated through experiments on colloidal particles, demonstrating access to rare-event physics via controlled trajectories.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection drawn between finite-time dynamical phase transitions (a non-equilibrium concept) and the cost of optimal control provides a powerful, measurable tool to probe rare-event physics in open quantum or mesoscopic systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a theory for optimally controlling stochastic systems in structured environments, focusing on identifying and characterizing sharp finite-time transitions.

**Main result.** The control problems are classified into three canonical equivalence classes (parabolic, hyperbolic, elliptic), and the optimal control cost is shown to map to the large-deviation rate function governing non-equilibrium relaxation for two of these classes.

**Method.** The authors use optimal control theory (Pontryagin's principle) applied to an overdamped Langevin equation, linearizing the dynamics around a fixed point to classify the resulting mathematical structure.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled by an overdamped Langevin equation, analyzed in the context of optimal control cost minimization and non-equilibrium relaxation following a potential quench. Experiments are validated using optically trapped colloidal particles.

**Key observables.** Optimal protocols, cost functions, critical times ($t_c$), and the large-deviation rate function.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Friction coefficient ($\gamma$), trap stiffness ($\kappa$), and the dimensionless noise parameter ($\epsilon$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on linearizing the dynamics around a zero-control fixed point, and the mapping between control cost and rate function fails for the elliptic class.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the control transition for mean stochastic work (parabolic class) and the finite-time dynamical phase transitions in free diffusion and harmonic relaxation.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the general theory by classifying control problems into three classes based on the control Hamiltonian determinant. It then derives explicit solutions for the optimal protocol and cost for each class, establishing the mapping to non-equilibrium relaxation theory, and finally validates these findings with three distinct experiments on colloidal particles.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a theory for the optimal control of stochastic systems in structured environments, represented by penalty terms in the cost functional. We show that such control problems generically feature sharp finite-time transitions associated with a qualitative change in the control strategy at a critical time. Starting from an overdamped Langevin equation and a quadratic cost functional, we show that all resulting problems fall into three canonical equivalence classes (parabolic, hyperbolic, and elliptic), distinguished by the sign of the determinant of the control Hamiltonian. For each class, we obtain the optimal protocol, the cost function, and the critical time in closed form, and show that the transition exhibits features of a continuous phase transition at mean-field level. We then establish a mapping between the optimal control cost and the large-deviation rate function governing non-equilibrium relaxation after a potential quench. The mapping covers the parabolic and hyperbolic classes, while the elliptic class has no simple relaxation counterpart. This correspondence implies that recently discovered finite-time dynamical phase transitions, which are exponentially costly to sample directly, are accessible through ordinary averages over optimally controlled trajectories. To validate our theoretical findings, we report three experiments with optically trapped colloidal particles: a control transition for the mean stochastic work, and the finite-time dynamical phase transitions in free diffusion and in harmonic relaxation.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work extends the description of impurities in a Fermi gas to finite temperatures by deriving a self-consistent equation for the effective mass gap $\Delta(T)$. The key finding is that the gap closes at a characteristic temperature $T^*$, signaling the thermal melting of quasiparticles. This thermodynamic crossover is mirrored dynamically by the disappearance of long-time quantum oscillations in the system's Ramsey response above $T^*$.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a unified, mean-field framework to connect the thermodynamic signature (gap closing) with the dynamic signature (loss of quantum beats) of quasiparticle thermal melting in a Fermi gas, which is highly relevant for understanding strongly interacting quantum gases.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper generalizes the mass-gap description of mobile impurities in a Fermi gas from zero temperature to finite temperature, and studies the resulting quench dynamics.

**Main result.** The effective mass gap closes at a characteristic temperature $T^*$, which is identified as the mean-field signature of the thermal melting of polaron and molecule quasiparticles. Furthermore, long-time quantum beats in the Ramsey response disappear precisely above $T^*$.

**Method.** The study combines the Lee–Low–Pines transformation with a self-consistent Hartree–Fock decoupling to derive the finite-temperature gap equation, and uses the functional-determinant approach to compute quench dynamics.

**Model / system.** The system consists of a single impurity of mass $M$ interacting via a contact interaction with a noninteracting Fermi sea of host fermions. The model is analyzed using a mean-field Hamiltonian derived from the full interaction.

**Key observables.** Effective mass gap $\Delta(T)$, Ramsey response $S(t)$, and the quasiparticle weight $Z$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Characteristic temperature $T^* = U_F / (2k_B)$, Fermi momentum $k_F$, and the impurity mass $M$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the Hartree–Fock decoupling, which is treated as a Gaussian (quasifree) approximation for the interacting system.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the mass-gap dispersion at different temperatures, showing the gap closing at $T^*$, and plot the Ramsey response $|S(t)|$ which exhibits oscillations for $T < T^*$ and decays monotonically for $T > T^*$.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the finite-temperature self-consistency equation for the mass gap $\Delta(T)$ and its relation to $T^*$. It then applies the functional-determinant approach to calculate the time evolution of the system overlap, $S(t)$, demonstrating the temperature dependence of the quantum beats.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recently, a mass-gap description of mobile impurities in a Fermi gas was introduced, which connects Anderson's orthogonality catastrophe for static impurities to the quasiparticle picture of Fermi polarons through a recoil-induced energy gap in the fermionic dispersion. That description, however, was restricted to zero temperature and did not address dynamics. Here we generalize the mass-gap model to finite temperature by combining the Lee--Low--Pines transformation with a self-consistent Hartree--Fock decoupling of the recoil-induced interaction, and we study the quench dynamics within this framework using the functional-determinant approach. At finite temperature the effective mass gap obeys the self-consistency equation $Δ(T)=2U_F\tanh[Δ(T)/4k_B T]$, with $U_F=k_F^2/2M$ and $M$ the impurity mass. This equation admits a nonzero solution below the characteristic temperature $T^*=U_F/(2k_B)$ and closes as $(T^*-T)^{1/2}$. We identify this closing as the mean-field signature of the thermal melting of the polaron and molecule quasiparticles. Computing the Ramsey response $S(t)$ after a sudden quench of the impurity--fermion interaction, we find that its long-time oscillations---quantum beats between the bound and in-gap states---disappear precisely above $T^*$. Our work ties the thermodynamic and dynamical fingerprints of polaron formation to a single temperature-dependent mean-field parameter.

</details>

**Summary.** This experimental work demonstrates a method to deterministically map the spin state of an electron in a quantum dot onto the polarization state of a reflected photon. By leveraging spin-induced Kerr rotation in a QD-cavity system, the authors achieve high-fidelity spin projection and show that the spin relaxation dynamics can be read out via subsequent photon polarization measurements. This paves the way for building quantum logic gates using solid-state emitters.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the realization of quantum logic gates by establishing a robust physical mechanism (spin-photon coupling) for deterministic quantum state transfer, which is central to quantum information processing.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to experimentally demonstrate a deterministic, one-to-one mapping (bijection) between the spin state of an embedded electron and the polarization state of a scattered photon.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated this spin-to-polarization mapping using a charged quantum dot-cavity device, showing that two orthogonal polarization states can be generated, each corresponding to a specific spin eigenstate.

**Method.** Time-resolved polarization measurements are used to project the electron spin onto its eigenstates and subsequently track spin relaxation dynamics via the detection of a second reflected photon.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a negatively-charged InGaAs Quantum Dot embedded in an AlGaAs micropillar cavity. The mechanism relies on the spin-induced Kerr rotation within this QD-cavity structure.

**Key observables.** Spin projection fidelity ($95\pm2\%$), the relative angle ($\alpha$) between Stokes vectors ($\vec{S}|\phi_1 \approx -\vec{S}|ar{\phi}_1$), and the conditional Stokes parameter $s_{\phiar{\phi}}^{|\phiangle}(	au)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The experiment is limited by the timescale competition between electron spin relaxation ($T_{	ext{spin}}^{-1} \approx 1.9$ ns) and the trion radiative lifetime ($T_{	ext{rad}}^{-1} \approx 200$ ps).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The initial model assumes the low-power limit and neglects decoherence for optical transitions or spin states, though the analysis tracks relaxation dynamics.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the energy levels and the dependence of the polarization overlap on detuning ($\Delta\omega$), and show the evolution of the conditional Stokes parameter $s_{\phiar{\phi}}^{|\phiangle}(	au)$ over time delay $	au$.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theoretical framework for the spin-photon mapping using Kerr rotation, details the measurement protocol involving conditional polarization tomography, presents the quantitative results showing spin projection fidelity, and discusses the limitations imposed by relaxation timescales.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Coherent light-matter interfaces controllably modifying the state of a photon upon interaction with a stationary qubit are a key resource for implementing deterministic entangling gates for optical quantum technologies. This requires a one-to-one mapping between the state of the scattered photon and that of the embedded qubit. Here, we present an experimental signature of such a bijection by leveraging the spin-induced Kerr rotation present in a low-noise charged quantum dot-micropillar cavity device. Through time-resolved polarization measurements, we project the electron spin to one of its eigenstates with $95\pm2\%$ fidelity with a single reflected photon detection, and follow the subsequent spin relaxation through the detection of a second reflected photon. We demonstrate that, after a transient regime governed by the trion radiative lifetime, two orthogonal polarization states can be produced, each associated to a given spin eigenstate. While the current results are limited by a timescale competition between electron spin relaxation and trion radiative lifetime, they could be improved using hole spins displaying increased relaxation times. Our work paves the way towards deterministic logic gates exploiting this one-to-one mapping between a spin and the polarization of a scattered photon.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work establishes a general design principle for understanding self-sustained oscillations in open quantum spin systems. It shows that nonlinear dissipation is crucial for stabilizing the necessary oscillatory background, while the specific symmetry-breaking mechanism determines whether the transition to oscillation is a standard Hopf bifurcation or a different type of singularity.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a general design principle for engineering non-equilibrium quantum dynamics, showing precisely how the interplay between background dissipation structure and symmetry-breaking terms dictates the type of emergent collective behavior (e.g., Hopf vs. non-Hopf).

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the fundamental mechanisms governing the emergence of self-sustained oscillations (SSOs) in open, driven-dissipative many-body quantum systems.

**Main result.** Nonlinear, U(1)-covariant dissipation is required to stabilize the SSO background via a supercritical Hopf bifurcation, whereas linear dissipation or simple symmetry-breaking channels fail to produce a standard Hopf instability.

**Method.** The analysis employs a bifurcation-based picture, linear stability analysis, and local bifurcation theory applied to the mean-field dynamics derived from the Lindblad master equation.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using driven-dissipative collective spins, analyzed in the thermodynamic limit using Bloch-sphere parametrization. The dynamics are governed by a Liouvillian superoperator incorporating both U(1)-covariant background evolution and explicit U(1)-breaking terms.

**Key observables.** Hopf frequency ($\omega_H$), Lyapunov coefficients ($l_1$), background attractor type (Polar Fixed Point vs. Self-Sustained Oscillator manifold), and the nature of the bifurcation (supercritical vs. subcritical).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dissipation imbalance ($\eta$), detuning ($\Delta$), and the structure of the U(1)-breaking terms (linear vs. nonlinear).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the existence of a U(1)-covariant background Liouvillian ($L_0$) and relies on mean-field approximations in the thermodynamic limit.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dependence of eigenvalues on dissipation imbalance and detuning, marking Hopf critical points. They also compare time traces and Lyapunov coefficient scans to distinguish between supercritical and subcritical Hopf transitions.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a bifurcation picture by first analyzing the structural requirements for the SSO background using linear and nonlinear dissipation. It then examines the transition dynamics under coherent U(1) breaking, contrasting resonance ($\Delta=0$) with finite detuning ($\Delta 
eq 0$), and finally contrasts these findings with the limitations imposed by simple linear dissipative symmetry-breaking jumps.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Self-sustained oscillations in open many-body systems can be organized by two distinct dynamical ingredients: the selection of a finite-amplitude background attractor and the subsequent control of its neutral phase. We develop this bifurcation-based picture for driven-dissipative collective spins and show how the microscopic dissipative structure determines the available background and its response to explicit U(1) breaking. In the thermodynamic-limit mean-field dynamics, a single linear U(1)-covariant jump produces only polar fixed-point backgrounds, whereas nonlinear covariant dissipation provides amplitude-dependent saturation and stabilizes a finite-latitude self-sustained-oscillator manifold through a supercritical Hopf bifurcation. Under coherent U(1) breaking, exact resonance leads to a reversible double-zero degeneracy with vanishing critical frequency rather than a standard Hopf onset. Finite detuning unfolds this singularity into a genuine finite-frequency Hopf boundary, which exists only on the self- sustained-oscillator side and can be either supercritical or subcritical. By contrast, a single linear dissipative U(1)-breaking jump cannot generate a standard Hopf instability: when its phase-pinning invariant vanishes the azimuthal direction remains neutral, whereas otherwise the phase-locked fixed points have a purely real Jacobian spectrum. These results establish a general design principle: nonlinear covariant dissipation selects the self-sustained background, while the structure of the symmetry-breaking channel determines whether the resulting local response is double-zero, genuinely Hopf, or non-Hopf.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors report the first demonstration of frequency-degenerate twin-photon generation and heralded single-photon emission using a single integrated silicon nitride microresonator. By measuring correlation functions like the CAR and $g^{(2)}_h(0)$, they prove the platform's capability to generate tailored quantum light sources on a scalable chip.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it demonstrates scalable, integrated quantum light sources (twin-photons and single photons) using established silicon photonics platforms, which is a core technology for quantum networking and computation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to demonstrate frequency-degenerate twin-photon generation and characterize a heralded single-photon source using a single integrated silicon nitride platform.

**Main result.** They successfully demonstrated both twin-photon generation (CAR up to 5.4) and heralded single-photon emission ($g^{(2)}_h(0) = 0.0042$) on the same chip.

**Method.** The experiments utilized time-correlated single-photon counting with SNSPDs to measure second-order correlation functions ($g^{(2)}$) for both coincidence and heralded measurements.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a silicon nitride (Si3N4) microring resonator integrated on a photonic chip. Quantum light generation occurs via Four-Wave Mixing (FWM) processes (both non-degenerate for twins and degenerate for single photons).

**Key observables.** Coincidence-to-Accidental Ratio (CAR), heralded anti-bunching ($g^{(2)}_h(0)$), and spectral purity ($P$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Telecom wavelengths, loaded quality factors ($Q \approx 10^5$), and pump powers up to $7 	ext{ dBm}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The single-photon purity calculation assumes residual deviation is due to incomplete spectral isolation, and quantitative results are derived independently from coincidence counts rather than fitting procedures.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic for inverse FWM (twin-photon source), the experimental setup using CDWDM, and plots showing $g^{(2)}$ cross-correlation vs. pump power, confirming the CAR dependence.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the realization of two distinct quantum light sources—twin-photon and single-photon—on one platform, characterized by measuring correlation functions ($g^{(2)}$) under different pumping conditions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Photonic chips with silicon nitride ($\mathrm{Si_3N_4}$) microring resonators are well established as heralded single-photon sources, but their operation as frequency-degenerate twin-photon sources has not previously been demonstrated. Here, we realise a twin-photon source at telecommunication wavelengths in a $\mathrm{Si_3N_4}$ ring microresonator via an inverse four-wave mixing (FWM) process, in which two photons from spectrally distinct pumps are converted into a pair of identical twin photons. The measurements show a maximum coincidence-to-accidental ratio (CAR) of $5.4\pm0.6$. In addition, the microresonator functions as a heralded single-photon source through pump-degenerate spontaneous four-wave mixing (SFWM), exhibiting a spectral purity of $P=0.67\pm0.05$ and a heralded anti-bunching of $g^{(2)}_h(0)=0.0042\pm0.0015$. Together, these results demonstrate both photon-generation schemes on a single integrated $\mathrm{Si_3N_4}$ platform, highlighting its potential for scalable, tailored quantum light generation.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors report on the generation and characterization of heralded multi-photon states using parametric down-conversion in a waveguide. They introduce and validate a resource-saving analysis method based on single-click probabilities and normalized factorial moments. The key finding is the quantitative demonstration of higher-order non-classicality by violating established classical light criteria.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to quantum optics and open quantum systems, providing robust experimental techniques to verify the non-classical nature of light sources, which is foundational for quantum communication and computation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to prepare and characterize heralded one-, two-, and three-photon number states using parametric down-conversion while demonstrating resource-saving quantum-optics analysis tools.

**Main result.** The measurements successfully characterized higher-order non-classicality by violating the classical condition $g^{(m+1)}_h \geq g^{(m)}_h \geq 1$, confirming the non-classical nature of the generated multi-photon states.

**Method.** The analysis utilizes manifold coincidences and singles counting, which is robust against experimental imperfections, to measure normalized factorial moments of photon number ($g^{(m)}_h$).

**Model / system.** The experiment uses cross-polarized twin beams generated via parametric down-conversion in a periodically-poled KTiOPO4 (PP-KTP) waveguide operating in the telecommunication wavelength range.

**Key observables.** Mean photon number $\langle\hat{n}angle^{(k)}_s$, normalized factorial moments $g^{(m)}_h(|kangle)$, Coincidences-to-Accidentals Ratio (CAR), and Klyshko's efficiencies.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Pumping powers ranged from $44 \mu	ext{W}$ to $214 \mu	ext{W}$; CAR values were measured between 5.9 and 29.3.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The calculation for $g^{(m)}_h$ assumes low detection efficiencies, and the theoretical treatment for $	ext{CAR} < 10$ is limited to single-mode PDC at low gains.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental setup, measured CAR vs. pump power, mean photon numbers vs. CAR, and the measured normalized factorial moments and the ratio $G = g^{(2)}_h / g^{(3)}_h$.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the resource-saving analysis tools using single-click probability. It then details the measurement of normalized factorial moments up to $m=n+1$ to test the violation of classical light conditions, culminating in quantitative results for multi-photon state characterization.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The manifold coincidences and singles counting provides a resource-saving quantum-optics analysis tool, since it is appropriate even in the presence of heavy experimental imperfections. Here, we prepare cross-polarized twin beams in the telecommunication wavelength range via parametric down-conversion in a periodically-poled KTiOPO$_4$ waveguide and herald photon-number states up to three photons. First, we show that the single-click probability is a versatile tool not only for extracting the state's mean photon number but also for sampling values of the moment generating function being the core behind any quantum optical state. Second, we measure values of the normalized factorial moments of photon number, $g^{(m)}_{\text{h}}$, up to the order $m = n+1$ for the heralded $n$-photon state. These normalized photon correlations provide an expedient method for examining the higher-order non-classicality of light by violating the condition $g^{(m+1)}_{\text{h}} \ge g^{(m)}_{\text{h}} \ge 1$.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper unifies optimal control theory with quantum measurement by showing that the control signal required to steer a quantum state between two distributions is mathematically equivalent to the imaginary part of a generalized weak value. It establishes that weak measurements act as a natural entropic regularization mechanism for quantum state transport, providing a concrete method for quantum feedback control synthesis.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a deep, quantitative link between fundamental concepts in quantum measurement theory (weak values) and advanced mathematical tools from optimal control (Schrödinger bridges), offering a powerful new diagnostic tool for open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a mathematical framework connecting optimal control theory, quantum Schrödinger bridges, and continuous quantum measurement for open quantum systems.

**Main result.** The imaginary part of a generalized weak value is shown to be the local control signal, directly corresponding to the directional derivative of the Doob/Sinkhorn bridge potential.

**Method.** The authors utilize the mathematical structure of conditional quantum trajectory theory, which induces a Fokker-Planck equation on the quantum state space, and apply optimal transport techniques (Schrödinger bridges).

**Model / system.** The system involves continuously monitored quantum systems, modeled by stochastic differential equations (Itô form) on the quantum state manifold. The dynamics are governed by a controllable Hamiltonian $H(u)$.

**Key observables.** Weak value ($	ext{Im}\langle A angle_{E,ho}^w$), Control Score ($S_A$), and the Schrödinger/Sinkhorn bridge potentials ($\hat{\varphi}, \varphi$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The control Hamiltonian $H(u) = H_0 + \sum u_\mu A_\mu$, and the terminal measurement effect $E$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis distinguishes its approach from other formulations, such as the Dressel–Jordan formula or the CDJ-Pontryagin framework, focusing on the classical diffusion induced by continuous measurement.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the Gaussian bridge solution for state transport and show numerical results for a two-detector qubit, comparing uncontrolled vs. weak-value/Sinkhorn-score-controlled processes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by introducing the Hamiltonian flow and boundary conditions, generalizing the classical bridge problem to continuous quantum measurement, and concluding with explicit examples demonstrating the equivalence between weak values and the bridge potential's directional derivative.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Schrödinger bridges and entropic optimal transport are usually formulated as stochastic interpolation problems between initial and final probability distributions. In their computational form, the bridge potentials are obtained by Sinkhorn or iterative proportional fitting, and are often regarded as auxiliary scaling functions. We show that for continuously monitored quantum systems, these potentials acquire a direct measurement-theoretic meaning. The mathematical structure of conditional quantum trajectory theory induces a Fokker--Planck equation on quantum state space. Conditioning this diffusion on a terminal distribution or a terminal measurement effect produces a Doob/Sinkhorn potential whose directional derivative along a unitary control vector field is the imaginary part of a generalized weak value. The same construction connects the Schrödinger-bridge viewpoint to the optimal-path framework for continuously monitored trajectories: the backward bridge potential plays the role of an effect-like costate, and its weak-value directional derivative gives the local control signal. By specifying the desired endpoint distribution, the induced drift produced by the Schrödinger bridge solution is the control solution that minimizes the quadratic cost feedback law, guiding the distribution to its desired endpoint. We quantify the control score of the available control Hamiltonian as a logarithmic directional derivative of the bridge potential, or an imaginary weak value. Three explicit examples identify weak measurement as a natural entropic regularization mechanism for quantum state transport and gives a route from Sinkhorn scaling to quantum feedback and Hamiltonian control synthesis for practical optimal control.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a robust protocol for engineering desired interactions in multi-subensemble quantum systems, such as atomic arrays. It tackles the theoretical difficulty of Hamiltonian control by providing necessary and sufficient conditions and efficient numerical design strategies. The results demonstrate that these robust sequences can successfully generate quantum states, like spin-squeezed states, even when subjected to realistic experimental control errors.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it provides concrete, robust protocols for controlling complex many-body interactions in atomic systems, moving beyond idealized theoretical models to address real-world experimental limitations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of robustly engineering target interactions (Hamiltonians) in multi-subensemble quantum systems using global control pulse sequences.

**Main result.** They provide a practical toolbox showing that target Hamiltonians can be engineered robustly against common control imperfections (Amplitude, Frequency, Pulse-Width errors) in systems like atomic ensembles.

**Method.** The methodology combines theoretical analysis of Hamiltonian engineering (using Floquet-Magnus expansion and completeness conditions) with numerical optimization techniques to design error-mitigating pulse sequences.

**Model / system.** The work applies to spin ensembles, modeled using generalized Gell-Mann operators, arising naturally in solid-state defects or multi-species atomic arrays. Specific applications include dual-species atomic ensembles and Rydberg atom arrays.

**Key observables.** Two-mode spin squeezing parameter ($\xi$), and the achievable effective Hamiltonian ($	ilde{H}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Error strengths ($\epsilon, \delta$), number of subensembles ($n$), and the length/design of the pulse sequence (e.g., unitary 4-design).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the completeness of toggling-frame unitaries forming a unitary 4-design to ensure full control over the interaction space.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison of spin squeezing achieved using bare vs. robust pulse sequences for both optical cavity and Rydberg atom settings under varying error strengths.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the general theory of Hamiltonian engineering and its computational hardness, to deriving necessary and sufficient conditions, and finally demonstrating robust implementation and application in specific atomic platforms.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a robust protocol to reshape interactions in a spin ensemble based on global control pulse sequences applied to multiple subensembles in parallel. This setting arises naturally from ensembles of solid-state defects or multi-species atomic arrays. We show that it is provably computationally hard to find pulse sequences that simultaneously engineer interactions both within and between subensembles. Despite its formal hardness, we identify a set of necessary or sufficient conditions under which one can synthesize a target Hamiltonian from the native one. Moreover, we introduce efficient numerical strategies for designing pulse sequences that engineer target Hamiltonians robust against common control imperfections. As a specific application, we discuss the robust generation of two-mode spin squeezing in dual-species atomic ensembles, which is a challenging task without subensemble control. Our results provide a practical toolbox to design novel quantum simulation and sensing experiments with minimal control overhead.

</details>

**Summary.** This research establishes a universal finite-time scaling theory for quantum entanglement entropy in driven quantum systems. By analyzing the corner entanglement entropy of Dirac fermions, the authors show that the scaling behavior robustly reveals the universal corner coefficient of the underlying critical theory. This extends critical dynamics concepts from local measurements to intrinsic, non-local quantum information measures.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, non-local diagnostic tool—entanglement entropy—to characterize quantum criticality in driven systems, which is highly relevant for understanding non-equilibrium dynamics in quantum simulators.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to extend the predictive power of Kibble-Zurek and finite-time scaling from local observables to non-local quantum information measures, specifically quantum entanglement entropy, in driven critical dynamics.

**Main result.** The corner entanglement entropy exhibits a universal finite-time scaling governed by the driving rate, allowing the robust extraction of the underlying conformal field theory's universal corner coefficient far from equilibrium.

**Method.** The authors use unbiased Quantum Monte Carlo simulations, employing an incremental algorithm to calculate the corner entanglement entropy ($\Delta S_2$) for various driving rates.

**Model / system.** The study investigates interacting Dirac-fermion models, including the Hubbard and spinless t-V models, on honeycomb and pi-flux square lattices, driven across quantum critical points (QCPs).

**Key observables.** Corner Entanglement Entropy ($\Delta S_2$), which is used to probe the universal scaling behavior.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The driving rate ($R$), system size ($L$), and the characteristic scaling variable ($RLr$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the system remains within the low-energy scaling window ($a \ll \xi_R \ll L$) for the asymptotic scaling function to hold.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the calculation of $\Delta S_2$ via lattice cuts and show data collapse when plotting $\Delta S_2$ against $\ln R$ or $\ln(RLr)$ for different system sizes.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical framework connecting FTS to entanglement, applies it to specific fermionic models, details the numerical implementation using incremental algorithms, and finally extracts the universal QCP corner coefficient by analyzing the scaling function's asymptotic behavior.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Kibble-Zurek mechanism (KZM) and finite-time scaling (FTS) provide a foundational framework for driven critical dynamics, yet their predictive power has been largely confined to local observables. Here, we establish a universal finite-time scaling theory for the nonequilibrium dynamics of quantum entanglement. Using unbiased quantum Monte Carlo simulations, we investigate the corner entanglement entropy of (2+1)-dimensional interacting Dirac fermions driven from ordered phases toward a quantum critical point. We find that the corner entanglement accurately obeys a universal driven scaling governed by the driving rate and system size, persisting whether the initial ordered state is fully gapped or hosts gapless Goldstone modes. Crucially, this dynamical entanglement exhibits a logarithmic dependence on the driving rate, from which the universal corner coefficient of the underlying conformal field theory can be robustly extracted far from equilibrium. These results generalize the KZM from local observables to the intrinsic nonlocal quantum information measures, offering a practical blueprint for characterizing quantum criticality and entanglement on programmable quantum simulators.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the initial decay of the Loschmidt echo after a small quench near a quantum critical point. It establishes that the initial decay rate is governed by the variance of the quench operator, which is directly related to static critical correlations. The work provides detailed scaling laws showing how this early-time response depends on the dimensionality and the scaling dimension of the quench operator.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous theoretical framework linking the non-equilibrium dynamics (Loschmidt echo) directly to the static properties (critical correlations) of the ground state, which is highly relevant for understanding quantum criticality in many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the initial growth rate of the Loschmidt rate function following a small sudden quench near a quantum critical point (QCP). It aims to connect this early-time dynamic response to static critical correlations.

**Main result.** The initial quadratic coefficient of the Loschmidt decay is exactly determined by the spatial sum of the equal-time connected two-point correlation function of the quench operator. The finite-size scaling of this coefficient depends critically on the scaling dimension of the quench operator relative to the system dimension.

**Method.** The analysis uses the short-time cumulant expansion of the Loschmidt amplitude, relating the quadratic coefficient to the variance of the quench operator and analyzing the scaling of this variance using critical correlation functions.

**Model / system.** The study applies to quantum many-body systems, specifically illustrating results using the transverse-field Ising chain model subjected to small quenches (e.g., changing the transverse or longitudinal field).

**Key observables.** Loschmidt rate function $f(t)$, initial quadratic coefficient $\chi_V$, and higher-order cumulants ($\kappa_4$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** System size ($L$), quench operator scaling dimension ($\Delta$), and the small quench parameter ($\delta\lambda$ or $\delta h$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the small-quench approximation and assumes power-law decay for critical correlations, $C(r) \sim |r|^{-2\Delta}$.

**Figures summary.** Not specified.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by establishing the connection between the short-time Loschmidt dynamics and the variance of the quench operator. It then derives the scaling laws for this variance based on critical exponents and analyzes the crossover behavior by including the fourth cumulant correction.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the short-time Loschmidt dynamics after a small sudden quench near a quantum critical point. We show that the initial quadratic growth of the Loschmidt rate function is governed by the variance of the quench operator per system size. For a local quench operator, this variance density is exactly equal to the spatial sum of the equal-time connected two-point correlation function in the initial ground state. This relation connects the early-time Loschmidt response to static critical correlations. Assuming power-law correlations at criticality, we classify the finite-size scaling of the short-time coefficient by the scaling dimension of the quench operator. In one dimension, the coefficient is finite, logarithmically enhanced, or algebraically enhanced with system size. We illustrate this operator dependence in the transverse-field Ising chain, where transverse-field and longitudinal-field quenches couple to different critical operators. We also discuss the first correction beyond the quadratic regime using the fourth cumulant of the post-quench Hamiltonian.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports on the efficient, low-noise conversion of quantum optical photons into mechanical phonons using an optomechanical crystal operating at millikelvin temperatures. By utilizing the OMIT mechanism with few-photon inputs, the researchers achieved a high conversion efficiency ($\eta=0.76$) and confirmed the quantum nature of the stored mechanical state. This work advances the field by providing a robust pathway toward deterministic single-quantum mechanical state preparation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the interface between quantum optics and mechanical oscillators, which is a cornerstone of modern AMO physics, particularly in quantum transducers and quantum memory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to achieve efficient, low-noise conversion of quantum optical states (photons) into quantum mechanical states (phonons) near the single-quantum level.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrated a record-level photon-phonon conversion efficiency of $\eta=0.76$ while maintaining low added thermal noise ($n_{th}=9.0$), establishing the platform for deterministic single-quantum mechanical state preparation.

**Method.** The conversion is achieved using Optomechanically Induced Transparency (OMIT) in a cavity optomechanical crystal, probing the system with weak, few-photon coherent optical pulses.

**Model / system.** The system utilizes a quasi-two-dimensional optomechanical crystal (OMC) operating at millikelvin temperatures. The interaction is modeled via an effective beam-splitter Hamiltonian, enabling coherent mapping between optical fields and mechanical modes.

**Key observables.** Conversion efficiency ($\eta$), mechanical storage lifetime ($T_1$), added thermal noise ($n_{th}$), and the second-order autocorrelation function $g^{(2)}(\Delta n)$ of the retrieved phononic state.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Millikelvin temperatures, few-photon input pulses, mechanical frequency ($\Omega_m/2\pi = 10.314$ GHz), and the coupling strength ($g_0/2\pi = 1.0$ MHz).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The process relies on operating in the resolved-sideband regime and requires low temperatures to suppress thermal noise, which was a limitation in previous room-temperature efforts.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the OMIT mechanism, the experimental setup involving two optical paths, and diagnostic measurements like the HBT autocorrelation function $g^{(2)}(\Delta n)$ to confirm the quantum nature of the converted state.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the theoretical framework using OMIT, presents the experimental setup and measurements of the OMIT window, and culminates in demonstrating the high-efficiency, low-noise quantum state transfer from photons to phonons.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Coherent interfaces between optical photons and mechanical excitations provide a promising route towards phonon-state engineering and hybrid quantum information processing. Cavity optomechanical systems enable such interfaces via optomechanically induced transparency (OMIT), allowing coherent mapping between traveling optical fields and localized mechanical modes. However, previous implementations of OMIT conversion protocols were limited to classical input signals with large coherent state photon numbers due to room temperature operation and corresponding thermal mechanical noise. Here, we demonstrate efficient low-noise photon-phonon state transfer close to the single-quantum regime in an optomechanical crystal operated at Millikelvin temperatures. Using weak coherent optical input pulses at the few-photon level, we achieve a record-level photon-phonon conversion efficiency of $η=0.76$, a mechanical storage lifetime of $T_\mathrm{1}=7.3~μs$, and a tunable conversion bandwidth exceeding 4.5 MHz. Hanbury Brown-Twiss measurements of the retrieved signal demonstrate the coherent nature of the converted phononic state, evidencing low added thermal noise in the conversion process ($n_\mathrm{th}=9.0$). These results establish optomechanical crystals as efficient optical interfaces to GHz mechanical modes and provide a pathway toward deterministic single-quantum-level mechanical state preparation.

</details>

The observed divergence is compatible with the power-law scaling associated with the critical exponent ν. The fitted value of the latter is ν = 0.65(8), compatible with the 3DXY critical exponent ν3DXY = 0.6718(1) obtained from conformal bootstrap [36]. Inset: same as main panel plotted in linear scale. The markers in light gray correspond to datasets excluded from the power-law fit (see SI). b. Rescaled momentum density distribution ˜n(˜k) as a function of rescaled momentum ˜k. The rescaled quantities are computed from the bare quantities...</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This work experimentally verifies the concept of two-scale-factor universality near a superfluid phase transition in ultracold lattice Bose gases. By collapsing measurements of order-parameter fluctuations and correlations using two scale factors, the authors confirm that multiple observables obey the same universal scaling laws. This provides strong evidence for the deep underlying principles governing critical behavior in quantum many-body systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous, quantitative experimental test of fundamental concepts in critical phenomena—specifically, the simultaneous universality of multiple observables—in a highly controllable quantum many-body platform.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To experimentally validate the prediction of 'two-scale-factor universality' for observables across a continuous phase transition, specifically the superfluid transition.

**Main result.** The study demonstrated that fluctuations (cumulants) and two-point correlations simultaneously collapse onto universal scaling functions using only two entropy-dependent scale factors.

**Method.** The authors measured order-parameter cumulants and two-point correlations in ultracold lattice Bose gases and collapsed the data using scaling theory concepts.

**Model / system.** The system is ultracold lattice Bose gases modeled by the Bose-Hubbard Hamiltonian, studied near the superfluid transition point.

**Key observables.** Order-parameter cumulants ($\kappa_n$), order-parameter probability distribution $P(|\psi|)$, and two-point correlation function $G^{(1)}(\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{x}', u)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The control parameter is $u = U/J$, and the analysis is performed along isentropic trajectories at varying entropies.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The results provide an experimental test of universality for a finite-size system, assuming corrections to classical scaling are negligible.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the concept of two-scale-factor universality, showing the collapse of bare cumulants onto universal curves using scale factors A and B, and displaying the scaling of the order parameter PDF and correlation length.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the concept of universal scaling, presents experimental measurements of cumulants and correlations across the superfluid transition, demonstrates the collapse onto universal functions using two scale factors, and finally determines critical exponents ($eta, \gamma, 
u$).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The critical behavior of a system near a continuous phase transition is commonly characterized by power laws and a set of universal critical exponents. Yet universality at phase transitions has even more fundamental manifestations. It dictates that all observables follow universal scaling functions, which may extend beyond simple power laws. Furthermore, microscopic details are captured entirely by only two system-specific scale factors, that rescale any measured quantity onto its universal form. In this work, we report the experimental validation of this prediction in ultracold lattice Bose gases undergoing the superfluid transition. We extract the scale factors by collapsing the order-parameter cumulants measured at varying entropies. We show that these factors specify scaling functions governing both the order-parameter distribution and two-point correlations. By measuring these scaling functions, we also determine three critical exponents $β$, $γ$ and $ν$. Our results provide an experimental demonstration that universal scaling is simultaneously obeyed by multiple observables in a quantum many-body system.

</details>
