**Summary.** This paper details a general protocol to simulate interacting bosonic and anyonic lattice models using superconducting transmon arrays. By precisely modulating the transmon frequencies, the authors can engineer the necessary on-site interactions and hopping terms for both the Bose-Hubbard and Anyon-Hubbard models. This work is significant because it demonstrates a scalable, controllable platform for studying strongly correlated quantum matter beyond current simulation limits.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, hardware-agnostic blueprint for simulating fundamental models of strongly correlated matter (like the Hubbard model) using superconducting circuits, bridging theoretical condensed matter physics with quantum hardware realization.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of simulating complex many-body phenomena, specifically bosonic and anyonic interactions, which are difficult to model using classical computational methods.

**Main result.** The authors propose a general, tunable protocol using superconducting transmon arrays to realize the dynamics of both the Bose-Hubbard and Anyon-Hubbard models.

**Method.** The simulation is achieved by resonantly driving the frequencies of capacitively coupled transmons in an alternating pattern, which effectively engineers the required interaction and hopping terms.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is a lattice of flux-tunable transmons. The target models are the Bose-Hubbard (BH) model and the Anyon-Hubbard (AH) model, which describe interacting bosons/anyons on a lattice.

**Key observables.** Interaction-dependent localization and the statistics-dependent asymmetry of the anyonic quantum walk.

**Important parameters / regimes.** On-site interaction strength (U), hopping amplitude (J), and the statistical phase ($	heta$) for anyons.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The protocol relies on resonant driving and uses approximations like the Rotating Wave Approximation (RWA) and a first-order Fourier expansion for the time-dependent frequency modulation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the quantum walk dynamics for the BH model across varying interaction strengths and show the total leakage probability in the AH model as a function of interaction strength.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of simulating interacting quantum systems, details the general protocol using transmon arrays to map the BH and AH Hamiltonians, presents numerical results demonstrating the capability to tune parameters and observe characteristic dynamics, and discusses limitations like leakage.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

When quantum particles interact, many-body phenomena that are hard to simulate classically emerge. Quantum analog simulation offers an alternative in which the target system's dynamics is directly realized in controllable quantum hardware. Here, we give a general protocol for simulating the Bose-Hubbard and anyon-Hubbard models using lattices of capacitively coupled flux-tunable transmons. By modulating the transmon frequencies in an alternating pattern, we resonantly drive multiple many-body transitions and can tune the on-site interaction and density-dependent hopping amplitudes for up to three bosons per site, with no additional restriction on the total particle number. By adding phases to the modulation, which renders the transition amplitudes complex-valued, we propose the first simulation protocol for the anyon-Hubbard model with transmons. Numerical simulations of the driven transmon arrays with experimentally realistic parameters reproduce the characteristic dynamics of the target models across a range of interaction strengths and statistical phases, including the interaction-dependent localization and the statistics-dependent asymmetry of the anyonic quantum walk.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper analyzes the weak momentum in quantum systems, showing that standard conditioned-diffusion models fail when the system's two-state amplitude develops nodes. The failure manifests as a divergence in the osmotic component of the weak momentum, which is a key diagnostic distinguishing the quantum reality from purely current-based classical descriptions.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous mathematical constraint on how quantum weak measurements can be modeled using classical diffusion processes, highlighting where the underlying stochastic assumptions break down.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the limitations of applying conditioned-diffusion representations, derived from quantum Schrödinger bridges, to the weak momentum, especially when the two-state amplitude exhibits nodes.

**Main result.** The positivity hypothesis required by these bridges fails on a definite locus (the nodal curve), causing the osmotic part of the weak momentum to diverge while the current part remains bounded.

**Method.** The analysis uses the Madelung decomposition of the weak momentum and compares its behavior to established quantum stochastic frameworks like Doob's $h$-transform.

**Model / system.** The system involves pre- and post-selected quantum states ($\psi$ and $\phi$) and analyzes the weak momentum in the context of a two-slit geometry with separable transverse fields.

**Key observables.** Weak momentum components (current and osmotic parts), nodal locus of $\phi^*\psi$, and fringe visibility/contrast.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The order of the zero ($k$) of the two-state amplitude, and the small parameter $	heta$ in related context analyses.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The results are derived for one-dimensional systems covering separable transverse fields, and the analysis distinguishes between the current velocity and the Itô drift.

**Figures summary.** Not specified (Notes focus on mathematical derivations and conceptual failures rather than figure descriptions).

**Paper structure.** The paper builds by first establishing the failure of the positivity hypothesis required by quantum Schrödinger bridges when dealing with the two-state amplitude. It then analyzes the divergence of the osmotic part at the node, contrasting this with the bounded current part, and finally relates these findings to experimental diagnostics like fringe visibility.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum Schrödinger bridge constructions establish the existence of a conditioned process under a positivity hypothesis on the endpoint data, and three independent ones adopt it. We show that hypothesis fails on a definite locus once the conditioned object is the two-state amplitude $φ^*ψ$ of a pre- and post-selected pair on configuration space, and that the failure is testable on recorded data. Whenever $φ^*ψ$ has a moving zero of order $k$ across which probability flows, no diffusion of constant diffusion coefficient can both carry the conditioned density and avoid the nodal curve: the current velocity it would need grows as the inverse $2k$-th power of the distance to that curve and points toward it on one side, placing the curve at finite scale distance, so it is reached and not merely approached. Identifying the weak momentum with a bridge drift fails independently: for a free particle post-selected in position the current velocity is exactly minus the drift of the bridge to the target. The positive Doob $h$-transform and the Bernstein conditioning built on it exist wherever $φ^*ψ$ is nodeless and fail on the nodal curve. There the osmotic part of the weak momentum diverges as the inverse distance to the zero and changes sign across it with a coefficient set by $k$, while the current part stays bounded; that boundedness rests on the signed factorization of the real-envelope class and hides the obstruction from any picture built on the current velocity alone. A measured fringe visibility fixes a single length, which puts a Lorentzian of order $10^{-3}$ into the current channel reconstructed in the two-slit trajectory experiment if the contrast is limited by an off-axis zero, and nothing there if by incoherence. The results are one-dimensional, covering the separable transverse field of a two-slit geometry, not general two-dimensional vortices.

</details>

**Summary.** This experimental work characterizes the low-frequency charge noise in Bilayer Graphene Quantum Dots using noise spectroscopy. The measured noise levels are competitive with established semiconductor platforms, and the noise proves robust against systematic electrostatic tuning. These results strongly validate BLG as a promising material for developing coherent solid-state qubits.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work directly addresses the primary decoherence mechanism (charge noise) limiting qubit coherence, providing quantitative metrics essential for assessing any solid-state platform for quantum computation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aimed to characterize and assess the low-frequency charge noise in Bilayer Graphene (BLG) Quantum Dots (QDs) to determine if the material is a viable platform for coherent quantum information processing.

**Main result.** The measured charge noise amplitude ($1.16 \mu	ext{eV}/\sqrt{	ext{Hz}}$) places BLG within the range of established semiconductor platforms, and the noise levels showed no systematic dependence on varying electrostatic tuning parameters.

**Method.** The noise was characterized using transport-based noise spectroscopy by observing current fluctuations at the flank of a Coulomb blockade resonance, with independent confirmation via superconducting resonator readout.

**Model / system.** The physical system is gate-defined BLG QDs, sometimes extended to incorporate Transition Metal Dichalcogenide (TMD) layers, fabricated in a van-der-Waals heterostructure.

**Key observables.** Median charge-noise amplitude ($S_{1/2\mu}(1 	ext{ Hz})$), Power Spectral Density (PSD) $S_\mu(f)$, and integrated charge noise ($\sigma_\mu$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Cryogenic temperature (20 mK), charge occupation, confinement potential, source-drain bias ($|V_{SD}|$), and sensor operating power.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The noise is attributed to Two-Level Fluctuators (TLFs) in dielectrics or interfaces, and the lack of systematic dependence suggests the noise is intrinsic to the environment rather than the gate-tunable parameters.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the device heterostructure and measurement procedure. Figure 2 compares the extracted charge noise amplitudes of BLG to other QD platforms. Figure 3 demonstrates the lack of systematic evolution of noise parameters when varying electrostatic tuning parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically characterizes charge noise by varying multiple operational parameters (occupation, bias, confinement) using transport spectroscopy, confirms results with an independent readout method, and extends the study to hybrid TMD/BLG systems.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Bilayer graphene (BLG) quantum dots (QDs) are a promising platform for semiconductor qubits. However, the low-frequency charge noise that may ultimately limit coherence has remained largely unexplored. Here, we systematically characterize charge noise in gate-defined BLG QDs using transport-based noise spectroscopy. We extract a median amplitude of $ S_μ^{1/2} (1~\text{Hz}) = 1.16~μ\text{eV}/\sqrt{\text{Hz}}$, placing BLG well within the range reported for established semiconductor quantum-dot platforms. Across variations in charge occupation, confinement, source-drain bias, and charge-sensor operating conditions, neither the noise amplitude nor the spectral dependence shows a reproducible trend in electrostatic tuning, indicating that we extracted the intrinsic semiconductor noise. Consistent noise levels are further observed in double QDs and confirmed using an independent superconducting resonator-based dispersive readout. Extending the study to BLG devices incorporating transition metal dichalcogenide layers reveals no measurable charge noise increase in weakly proximitized QDs. These results validate BLG as a viable platform for coherent quantum information processing.

</details>

The simulations use physical systems of 5 and 11 qubits, corresponding to N = 4 and N = 10 benchmark qubits, respectively. The swap-test implementation uses the remaining qubit as an ancilla, while the Bell-basis implementation does not require the additional qubit. Each point shows log(RMSEswap/RMSEBell) for the extracted effective polarization relative to a high-shot ideal reference computed using an error-free measurement circuit; positive values indicate lower estimation error for the Bell-basis implementation. (a) For N = 4, the Bell-basis measurement...</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Error Per Circuit Layer (EPCL), a novel overlap-based benchmark for characterizing noise in large quantum systems. EPCL measures the overlap between two independently evolved quantum registers, circumventing the need for computationally expensive classical simulations of ideal states. The method is validated on quantum hardware, providing a scalable metric for assessing aggregate register performance.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a crucial, hardware-agnostic diagnostic tool for assessing noise in large quantum circuits, which is vital for developing fault-tolerant quantum computation protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a robust, scalable circuit-level benchmark to characterize noise accumulation in large quantum systems without relying on classical simulation of ideal outputs.

**Main result.** The proposed Error Per Circuit Layer (EPCL) benchmark successfully measures aggregate register performance via output state overlap, showing clear decay on IBM hardware and remaining compatible with arbitrary gate sets.

**Method.** EPCL estimates effective layer polarization by measuring the overlap between the output states of two disjoint quantum registers after applying identical random circuits.

**Model / system.** The study analyzes quantum processors, using two copies of an $n$-qubit register to measure the overlap $	ext{Tr}(ho_A ho_B)$ as a function of circuit depth. The analysis is grounded in depolarizing channel models.

**Key observables.** Overlap between output states ($	ext{Tr}(ho_A ho_B)$), effective layer polarization ($p_{	ext{eff}}$), and the decay rate of this overlap with circuit depth.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Circuit depth ($d$), number of qubits ($n$), and the effective layer polarization parameter ($p$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes an ensemble-averaged depolarizing model for deriving the expected overlap decay, and it is designed to overcome the classical simulation bottleneck of prior methods.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the overlap measurement using techniques like the Swap Test or Pairwise Bell Measurements, and show the resulting decay curve as a function of circuit depth.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces EPCL as an overlap-based primitive, compares it to existing benchmarks (like XEB and EPLG), derives the expected decay under a depolarizing model, and validates the method through numerical simulations and experiments on quantum hardware.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum benchmarks provide compact measures of performance that are important for evaluating and comparing quantum systems. Circuit-level benchmarks are particularly valuable because they capture the accumulated effects of noise across interacting operations, but existing approaches may require structured gate sets and costly compilation, classical simulation of reference outputs, or subsystem decompositions that do not capture full-register behavior. We introduce Error Per Circuit Layer (EPCL), an overlap-based circuit-level benchmark that estimates an effective layer polarization by applying identical random circuits to two disjoint quantum registers and measuring the overlap between their output states as a function of circuit depth. EPCL avoids classical simulation of ideal output distributions and recovery to a known reference state, and is compatible with arbitrary gate sets, including non-Clifford gates.   We derive the expected overlap decay under an ensemble-averaged depolarizing model and identify the assumptions under which the fitted decay parameter represents an effective layer polarization. Numerical simulations show that EPCL recovers the predicted polarization under weak local stochastic noise and remains well described by a single-exponential decay at stronger stochastic noise levels. The simulations further show that coherent errors associated with fixed entangling layers may require Pauli twirling or randomized compiling to produce the expected decay, while inter-register correlations contribute an additional covariance term to the measured overlap. Finally, experiments on IBM quantum hardware demonstrate clear EPCL decay in 8- and 16-qubit implementations. These results support EPCL as a method for measuring aggregate register performance without requiring classical simulation of ideal circuit outputs or restriction to structured gate sets.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper experimentally validates a compact, fault-tolerant quantum computing architecture using the C4-Helix code on a trapped-ion quantum processor. The authors successfully demonstrated error suppression for memory, executed Clifford group operations with low logical error rates, and established a high-fidelity interface between two different quantum codes. These results confirm the architecture's viability for early-stage fault-tolerant quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** For a theoretical physicist, this provides concrete, hardware-validated benchmarks for resource estimation in fault-tolerant quantum computation, particularly regarding the overhead associated with implementing logical gates and state preparation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The challenge is to build a useful fault-tolerant quantum computation architecture that efficiently manages logical encoding, low-overhead logical operations, and access to non-Clifford resources.

**Main result.** The authors experimentally validated the C4-Helix code on a trapped-ion processor, demonstrating protected memory, low-overhead Clifford group operations, and a high-fidelity interface between two different quantum error-correcting codes.

**Method.** The work employs repeated Quantum Error Correction (QEC) cycles, randomized benchmarking (TQRB) for Clifford group testing, and benchmarking a heterogeneous interface to prepare entangled states between different codes.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is a trapped-ion quantum processor (Quantinuum Helios, 98-qubit). The primary theoretical tool is the C4-Helix code, which is tested for its ability to support fault-tolerant computation primitives.

**Key observables.** Logical error rates ($\epsilon_d$, $\epsilon_C$), GHZ state fidelity, and the efficiency of error suppression compared to physical measurements.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The target regime is the early fault-tolerant regime, aiming for logical error rates in the $10^{-6}$ to $10^{-8}$ range.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the assumption that the architecture can be validated by demonstrating protected memory, low-overhead Clifford control, and the ability to interface with other codes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the logical error rate scaling with code distance, the comparison of logical vs. physical randomized Clifford benchmarking, and the fidelity measurement for the three-logical-qubit GHZ state.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing protected memory using the C4-Helix code, then benchmarking the full Clifford group operations within the codeblock, and finally demonstrating the capability to perform fault-tolerant entanglement generation between the C4-Helix code and a surface code.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum error correction (QEC) is beginning to enable logical operations that outperform their unencoded physical counterparts, but useful fault-tolerant computation will require more than low-error quantum memory. An effective architecture must orchestrate efficient logical encoding, low-overhead logical operations, and access to the non-Clifford resources required for universal computation. Here, we introduce and experimentally validate such an architecture based on the $[[20,2,6]]$ $C_4$-Helix code, designed for the early fault-tolerant regime. Using Quantinuum Helios, a 98-qubit trapped-ion quantum processor, we experimentally demonstrate the principal components of this architecture: we perform repeated quantum error correction with an error of $4.6^{+6.2}_{-2.6}\times10^{-5}$ per logical qubit per QEC cycle. We benchmark the complete Clifford group on the two logical qubits of a single codeblock under active error correction, obtaining an error of $2.8^{+1.0}_{-1.6}\times 10^{-4}$ per two-qubit logical Clifford. We further demonstrate a fault-tolerant chain-map interface between $C_4$-Helix and a distance-5 surface code, preparing a heterogeneous three-logical-qubit GHZ state with a fidelity lower bound of $99.925^{+0.068}_{-0.245}\%$. In each case, the encoded implementation outperforms its corresponding unencoded physical baseline without relying on postselection. Circuit-level simulations indicate that improvements in physical fidelity bring the same architecture into the $10^{-6}$-$10^{-8}$ logical-error regime targeted for early fault-tolerant computation. Together, these results establish $C_4$-Helix as a hardware-validated fault-tolerant architecture rather than a bare quantum memory.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reframes the measurement problem not as a physical puzzle to be solved, but as a fundamental statistical inconsistency: quantum mechanics violates the axioms of classical probability theory. By establishing this non-Kolmogorovian nature of quantum statistics, the authors propose a framework that has profound implications for quantum gravity and the nature of objective reality.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a fundamental, statistical framework for understanding the measurement problem that transcends specific interpretations, which is crucial for developing a unified theory of quantum gravity.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the quantum measurement problem by seeking an interpretation-independent formulation, rather than attempting a direct solution.

**Main result.** The core finding is that quantum matter cannot be described by a Kolmogorovian probabilistic theory, suggesting the problem lies in the incompatibility between quantum statistics and classical probability axioms.

**Method.** The methodology involves analyzing the empirical statistics of quantum measurements, comparing them against the axioms of classical probability (Kolmogorov theory), and drawing parallels with theory-independent theorems like Bell's theorem.

**Model / system.** The focus is on the abstract mathematical properties of quantum mechanical predictions and their empirical statistics, rather than a specific physical Hamiltonian or system.

**Key observables.** Violation of the Law of Total Probability (LTP), violation of Bell-type inequalities, and the non-Kolmogorovian nature of quantum interference statistics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** None specified; the focus is on fundamental axioms and statistical properties.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The authors assume that the conflict arises because the standard axioms of probability (Kolmogorov) are insufficient to describe quantum reality, leading to the conclusion that no 'God's-eye view' exists.

**Paper structure.** The paper moves from reviewing the limitations of existing interpretations to establishing the non-Kolmogorovian nature of quantum statistics through statistical diagnostics, and finally discussing the philosophical implications for quantum gravity and the nature of reality.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this paper, we do not try to solve the measurement problem, but rather to properly formulate it. One of the reasons why it still lacks a precise, agreed definition is that the problem may take very different aspects depending on the interpretation of quantum mechanics embraced. Inspired by the methodology of theory-independent results like Bell's theorem, we propose to identify the common root of the puzzle in an interpretation-independent way i.e. as a property of the empirical statistics only, before deriving its philosophical consequences. The key observation is that quantum matter can not be described by a Kolmogorovian probabilistic theory. Arguing that the Kolmogorov axioms of probability theory are the postulates of epistemic uncertainty leads us to reformulate the measurement problem as the impossibility to build an ontology independent of epistemology for quantum matter. Said differently, there exists no God's-eye view on quantum systems. Although it is meaningless to solve the measurement problem as defined in this way, insofar as it is a feature of the universe's statistics, such a formulation may on the other hand bring side benefits. In particular, we argue that it allows to: (i) shed a new light on the variety of interpretations; (ii) propose a fundamental reason why quantum mechanics and general relativity are so incompatible, not relying on purely mathematical or technical arguments; (iii) guide the quest for quantum gravity.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper tackles the mathematical challenge of describing measurements on separated parts of an entangled quantum system. It introduces a novel framework using free vector spaces to calculate these local measurement projections, successfully bypassing the need to invoke a random collapse model. The key finding is that quantum nonlocality emerges naturally from the way local measurements project the state's possible outcomes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a deep mathematical underpinning for quantum nonlocality by reformulating measurement theory away from collapse postulates, which is fundamental to understanding open quantum systems dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the mathematical difficulty of describing measurement outcomes on physically separated subsystems of an entangled state when the state is represented in a tensor product Hilbert space.

**Main result.** It proposes a framework using the free vector space to calculate measurement projections, showing that nonlocality arises from the way these local measurements project the possible outcomes, eliminating the need for a random collapse postulate.

**Method.** The authors develop a mathematical formalism comparing the tensor product space to the free vector space, defining a canonical projection to relate global entangled states to local measurement outcomes.

**Model / system.** The system involves bipartite entangled quantum states ($\mathcal{H}_A \otimes \mathcal{H}_B$) that can be physically separated for measurement. The mathematical model contrasts the tensor product structure with the free vector space structure to handle measurement projections.

**Key observables.** Measurement outcomes on separated subsystems A and B, and the correlation between these paired outcomes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The entanglement structure of the state, and the choice of measurement basis (contextual phase classes).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that measurement outcomes can be derived by projecting the state's equivalence class in the free vector space, rather than relying on a random collapse postulate.

**Figures summary.** Not specified (The notes detail mathematical derivations and comparisons between different classes of outcomes, rather than describing specific figures).

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the initial problem of describing local measurements on entangled states, develops the mathematical machinery using free vector spaces, and concludes by demonstrating how the resulting correlated outcomes reveal the source of nonlocality.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The entangled states of composite quantum systems are well studied. The particle-like nature of these systems also means that, while entangled, they can be physically separated and measurements performed on the separated subsystems. Measurements on the separated subsystems A and B should pertain to vectors in the local Hilbert space of each subsystem, but to date it has not been clear how to elucidate the relevant states of the separated subsystems because it is not obvious how to resolve them from states given in the tensor product basis, except for the separable states. Here it is shown that the projections of any general entangled state that are detected by measurements on the separated subsystems can be obtained considering the corresponding (cosets of) states in the free vector space from which the tensor product space is defined. The result eliminates the need to invoke random collapse, and from this perspective nonlocality arises because of the way measurements on each separated subsystem projects possible measurement outcomes.

</details>

**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

**Summary.** This paper develops a robust framework for quantum-state discrimination under unknown unitary noise by solving a minimax decision problem. It introduces the Least Favorable Prior (LFP) and shows that the optimal measurement can be found by solving a dual SDP. The resulting LFP-based measurement significantly improves the worst-case success probability compared to standard methods.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous, mathematically grounded method for designing quantum measurements that are robust against unknown environmental noise or systematic errors, which is crucial for practical quantum information processing.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses robust quantum-state discrimination when the candidate states are corrupted by an unknown, common unitary perturbation (a nuisance factor). The goal is to find a measurement that minimizes the worst-case error probability over all possible perturbations.

**Main result.** The measurement derived using the Least Favorable Prior (LFP) achieves a substantially improved worst-case success probability and flattens the success-probability profile compared to standard optimal measurements.

**Method.** The problem is formulated as a minimax decision problem, which is solved by relating it to a Bayes measurement problem using the LFP. This optimization is implemented via Semidefinite Programming (SDP) techniques.

**Model / system.** The study considers discriminating between $K$ candidate quantum states $\{ho_a\}_{a=1}^K$ in a finite-dimensional Hilbert space, where the actual states are perturbed by $ho_a(	heta) = U(	heta)ho_aU(	heta)^\dagger$. The perturbation $	heta$ acts as a nuisance parameter.

**Key observables.** Worst-case success probability ($V_{	ext{succ}}^{n,N}$), success-probability profiles $P_{	ext{succ}}(M, 	heta)$, and the weight distribution of the LFP ($\mathbf{w}^\star$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of states $K$, the dimension of the Hilbert space, and the structure of the nuisance parameter space $\Theta$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theoretical framework handles continuous $\Theta$ but relies on discretizing it into a finite grid $\Theta_N$ for practical SDP computation.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare success-probability profiles for different measurements (LFP vs. Uniform-prior vs. Reference-point) in a qutrit model, showing the LFP measurement's superior worst-case performance. Table II summarizes these worst-case probabilities.

**Paper structure.** The paper first formulates the minimax risk and introduces the LFP concept. It then develops the mathematical machinery by showing the equivalence between the minimax problem and a Bayes problem over the LFP. Finally, it applies this framework to numerical examples (qubit and qutrit models) to demonstrate the performance gains.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study robust K-ary quantum-state discrimination when all candidate states are affected by the same unknown common unitary perturbation. The unknown perturbation does not represent the label to be identified, but acts as a nuisance factor that changes the performance of a fixed measurement. We formulate the problem as a minimax decision problem over the possible perturbations and propose using the Bayes-optimal collective measurement associated with a least favorable prior (LFP) on the nuisance-parameter space. For a finite discretization of this space, we show that the LFP can be computed by a semidefinite program and that the corresponding value coincides with the finite-grid minimax success probability. As numerical demonstrations, we consider a binary nonorthogonal qubit model and a nonorthogonal three-state qutrit model with an unknown common unitary perturbation. The LFP-based measurement substantially flattens the success-probability profile and improves the worst-case success probability compared with a reference-point optimal measurement and a uniform-prior Bayes measurement. The resulting LFP concentrates its weight on regions of the nuisance-parameter space that actively limit the robust discrimination performance, thereby providing both a constructive measurement design and a diagnostic description of the difficult nuisance-parameter regimes.

</details>

**Summary.** This research reports the measurement of the critical velocity in a superfluid of light flowing past an impurity using a sophisticated dual-wavelength optical setup. By independently measuring vortex nucleation, hydrodynamic force, and impurity displacement, the authors cross-validate the critical velocity threshold. This establishes the superfluid of light as a versatile and reliable platform for testing quantum hydrodynamic theories.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly controlled, optical platform to study fundamental quantum hydrodynamic phenomena, such as superfluid critical velocities and vortex dynamics, which are central topics in both quantum gas and quantum optics research.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper measures the critical velocity at which a paraxial superfluid of light transitions to a dissipative state when flowing past a mobile impurity.

**Main result.** The critical velocity was measured using three independent diagnostics (vortex count, hydrodynamic force, and impurity velocity), yielding consistent results that systematically fall below the Landau criterion.

**Method.** The critical velocity is determined by monitoring the onset of energy dissipation, specifically through the nucleation of quantized vortices, while simultaneously measuring the force and displacement on the impurity.

**Model / system.** The system is a 2D paraxial superfluid of light, modeled by the nonlinear Schrödinger equation, flowing past a highly repulsive, finite-mass impurity created optically in a warm vapor of Rb-87.

**Key observables.** Number of nucleated vortices, hydrodynamic force on the impurity, and the impurity's displacement velocity relative to the flow.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Mach number (v0/cs), impurity radius ($\sigma$), and the ratio $\sigma/\xi$ (impurity size to healing length).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the superfluid being governed by the NLS equation and assumes the three diagnostic methods provide a self-consistent benchmark for the critical transition.

**Figures summary.** Figures likely show the experimental setup and plots comparing the critical velocity ($eta_c$) extracted from the three different measurement techniques as a function of the impurity radius ($\sigma/\xi$).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical model (NLS equation for superfluid light), details the experimental setup using a dual-wavelength scheme, presents the three complementary measurement techniques, and concludes by cross-validating the results to establish a robust benchmark for the critical velocity.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We report the measurement of the critical velocity of a paraxial superfluid of light flowing past a mobile impurity, realized using a dual-wavelength optical scheme in a warm vapor of $^{87}\text{Rb}$. The superflow is two-dimensional by nature, while the impurity features a highly repulsive potential, a large tunable radius, and a finite mass. The critical velocity is evaluated by simultaneously monitoring three complementary observables: the number of vortices nucleated in the wake of the impurity, the hydrodynamic force exerted on it, and its displacement velocity relative to the flow. These independent methods yield consistent values for the critical velocity, which remain systematically below the Landau criterion and decrease with the impurity radius, in qualitative agreement with theoretical expectations. Our results demonstrate the reliability of these combined diagnostics, establishing paraxial superfluids of light as a versatile platform for exploring the impurity problem in quantum hydrodynamics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper systematically maps the complex, non-equilibrium phase behavior of active particles driven by chemical gradients. By varying chemical screening and boundary conditions, the authors predict the emergence of novel 'bubbly' phases, distinguishing them from simple collapse or gas states. These findings offer predictive rules for controlling active assembly in microfluidic settings.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study provides a detailed framework for understanding non-equilibrium phase transitions in active matter, which shares conceptual links with driven-dissipative quantum systems and pattern formation in open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the non-equilibrium phase behavior of autophoretic active particles, focusing on the emergence of novel bubbly phases influenced by boundaries and chemical screening.

**Main result.** The research reports the existence of boundary- and screening-induced bubbly phases (boiling and bursting bubbles), showing that boundary conditions actively select the non-equilibrium steady states.

**Method.** The authors employed systematic particle-resolved simulations to map out phase diagrams by analyzing the collective dynamics across varying physical parameters.

**Model / system.** The system consists of autophoretic active particles (Janus particles) whose motion is governed by chemo-attractive translational forces and chemo-repulsive rotational torques. The dynamics are modeled using equations of motion incorporating phoretic interactions and excluded-volume forces.

**Key observables.** Steady-state cluster fraction ($\langle S angle$), temporal fluctuation magnitude ($\sigma_S$), and coordination number ($\langle Q angle$) form a diagnostic metric triad.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Screening parameter ($\kappa$), torque magnitude ($\mu_r$), and boundary condition coefficients ($\Lambda_c$) are key control parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on particle-resolved simulations and uses the Weeks-Chandler-Andersen (WCA) potential for repulsive interactions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate phase diagrams showing transitions between gas, bubbly, and collapsed states as a function of $\kappa$ and $\mu_r$, and demonstrate structural anisotropy based on $\kappa$.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the influence of chemical screening ($\kappa$) and boundary conditions ($\Lambda_c$) on the phase diagram, using the metric triad to distinguish between macro-phase separation, bubbly states, and gas phases.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Spatial confinement and chemical screening fundamentally reshape the non-equilibrium phase behavior of autophoretic active particles. Here, we present a systematic study mapping the collective dynamics of self-propelled particles governed by chemo-attractive translational forces ($μ_t < 0$) and chemo-repulsive rotational torques ($μ_r > 0$) across varying screening parameters $κ$, torque magnitudes $μ_r$, and boundary condition coefficients $Λ^c$. Beyond standard chemotactic macro-phase separation and dynamic clustering, we report the emergence of novel boundary- and screening-induced bubbly phases, classified into boiling and bursting bubbles. Using a metric triad of steady-state cluster fraction $\langle S \rangle$, temporal fluctuation magnitude $σ_S$, and coordination number $\langle Q \rangle$, we draw phase diagrams to demarcate phases for no-flux boundaries ($Λ^c = 1$) and chemically permeable interfaces ($Λ^c = 0$) . Increasing chemical screening ($κ$) systematically suppresses long-range attraction, driving sequential phase transitions from macro-scale collapse toward bubbly states, dynamic micro-clusters, and homogeneous gas phases, while simultaneously inducing aggregate shape anisotropy. These findings provide predictive design rules for controlling active assembly and transport in microfluidic environments.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors present a novel, deterministic pick-and-place technique to transfer nanowire quantum dots (NWQDs) while preserving their high optical quality. By combining this transfer method with bottom mirror structures and electrostatic gates, they demonstrate the creation of highly efficient, tunable single-photon sources crucial for quantum technologies.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the engineering challenge of integrating quantum emitters into complex photonic circuits, which is foundational for scalable quantum light sources and quantum networking components.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is developing a scalable, deterministic method to engineer nanowire quantum dot (NWQD) devices by transferring them while maintaining their critical vertical growth geometry.

**Main result.** The developed pick-and-place transfer technique successfully preserves high optical quality, enabling the integration of bottom mirrors for 75% photon extraction efficiency and electrostatic gates for wavelength tuning.

**Method.** The work combines a deterministic, gas-assisted pick-and-place transfer technique with advanced optical characterization (PL, lifetime, HBT measurements) and theoretical modeling (FDTD simulations).

**Model / system.** The system involves semiconductor NWQDs (e.g., InAsP in InP) integrated into photonic nanowires. The process involves transferring these QDs from a growth substrate to arbitrary templates, such as those incorporating bottom gold mirrors or gate electrodes.

**Key observables.** Low-multiphoton probability ($g_2(0)=0.002$), high indistinguishability ($>80\%$), enhanced photon extraction efficiency (up to 75%), and measurable spectral tuning via gates.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The transfer process itself, the efficiency enhancement factor (e.g., 1.58 Purcell enhancement), and the achievable wavelength tunability ($\sim 3.6 	ext{ GHz}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The transfer process is assumed to preserve the quantum optical properties of the QD emission, and theoretical models rely on specific coupling strengths and modal reflectivities.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the deterministic pick-and-place transfer steps (detachment, gluing, placement) and show PL spectra before and after transfer, confirming spectral and linewidth preservation.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the development of the deterministic transfer technique, followed by comprehensive optical characterization demonstrating enhanced light collection efficiency using bottom mirrors, and finally, tuning capabilities using electrostatic gates.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Semiconductor quantum dots (QDs) are a leading platform for realising bright, wavelength-tunable sources of single and entangled photon pairs for photonic quantum technologies. Site-selected nanowire quantum dots (NWQDs) are a promising platform for fabricating such photonic devices in a scalable manner. However, implementing additional structures around the photonic nanowire while maintaining its vertical growth geometry has remained a challenge. In this work, we develop a deterministic pick-and-place technique to conduct a vertical-to-vertical transfer of NWQDs from the growth substrate to arbitrary templates. Using this transfer technique, we enhance the photon extraction efficiency to 75% by implementing a bottom gold mirror and tune the emission wavelength by 3.6 GHz via implementing electrostatic gates around the QD. Importantly, we measure low-multiphoton probability (g^(2)(0) = 0.002) and high indistinguishability (>80% for +/-100 ps) of the QD emission after the transfer process, yielding high-quality devices. These results demonstrate the repeatability and versatility of the developed transfer technique, which is an enabling step towards scalable single and entangled photon sources.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops new, thermodynamically grounded bounds on how much faster a stochastic process can survive when driven away from equilibrium. By analyzing both general and rare-event dynamics, the authors provide distinct linear and exponential bounds related to entropy production and excess heat. This is a significant theoretical advancement for understanding non-equilibrium dynamics in mesoscopic systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous connection of dynamical speed-up bounds to fundamental thermodynamic quantities like entropy production and heat generation provides powerful, non-equilibrium constraints applicable to open quantum systems and energy dissipation studies.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper derives new theoretical bounds controlling the maximum speed-up achievable in the survival rate of stochastic processes when subjected to non-equilibrium driving perturbations.

**Main result.** It establishes distinct bounds: a linear bound dependent on entropy production for general rates, and an exponential bound determined by excess heat for rare, constant-rate processes.

**Method.** The analysis employs nonlinear response theory, importance sampling, and techniques from large deviation theory applied to path probabilities in Markov jump processes and overdamped diffusions.

**Model / system.** The study applies to general mesoscopic systems modeled as continuous-time Markov jump processes and overdamped diffusions, exemplified by a unicyclic network and forced diffusion in a bistable potential.

**Key observables.** Survival probability $S(t)$, survival rate $r(t)$, and the difference in survival rates $ar{r}_p - ar{r}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Perturbation strength, entropy production rate, and the excess heat generated by the perturbation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation assumes that the path probability densities are absolutely continuous, excluding changes like varying the temperature in Langevin dynamics.

**Figures summary.** The notes mention comparing analytical predictions (e.g., for the Laplace transform of survival probability) against numerical integrations and simulations.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a structure of introducing the framework, deriving the bounds using nonlinear response theory, presenting the bounds in different mathematical forms, connecting these bounds explicitly to thermodynamic quantities, and concluding with model examples.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We derive a new set of bounds controlling the speed-up of the survival rate of trajectory-dependent observables induced by the application of a perturbation. Via the study of survival probabilities in two different contexts, continuous-time Markov jump processes and overdamped diffusions, we obtain different fashions of the bound on the achievable boost. These bounds, obtained by a nonlinear response theory of constrained path probabilities, are explicitly linked to thermodynamics. For arbitrary time-dependent survival rates, we find a linear bound that depends on both the perturbation strength and the entropy production rate prior to the perturbation. For rare processes characterized by a constant survival rate, the bound is exponential and is determined by the excess heat generated by the perturbation. We exemplify the inequalities on two prototypical model systems, namely, a unicyclic network and forced diffusion in a bistable potential.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study examines entanglement dynamics for two accelerated atoms interacting with a fluctuating environment near a boundary. By analyzing the master equation, the authors show that environment-induced interactions critically modulate entanglement, potentially leading to anti-Unruh effects. The results highlight the competition between different environmental coupling terms as the key determinant of quantum correlations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses open quantum systems dynamics in curved/accelerated spacetime, which is highly relevant to quantum optics and quantum information in non-trivial environments.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how environment-induced interactions affect the entanglement dynamics of two uniformly accelerated atoms.

**Main result.** The anti-Unruh phenomenon can occur under certain conditions, and the maximum entanglement achievable depends critically on the competition between environment-induced interatomic and atom-plate interactions.

**Method.** The dynamics are analyzed using the Gorini-Kossakowski-Lindblad-Sudarshan master equation in the weak-coupling limit.

**Model / system.** The system consists of two two-level atoms, uniformly accelerated and coupled to a fluctuating massless scalar field in a Minkowski vacuum with a perfectly reflecting boundary.

**Key observables.** Concurrence, $C[ho(	au)]$, is used as the primary measure of entanglement for the X state.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Proper acceleration ($a$), coupling constant ($\mu$), interatomic separation ($L$), and distance to the boundary ($z$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the weak-coupling limit and simplifies the initial state to an X state.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the time evolution of concurrence for different initial states and parameter regimes, showing how interactions suppress or promote entanglement decay.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops basic formulas first, followed by a detailed analysis of accelerated atoms coupled to the environment, deriving time evolution equations for density matrix elements.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the influence of environment-induced interactions on the entanglement dynamics of two uniformly accelerated atoms coupled to a fluctuating massless scalar field with a reflecting boundary. The two atoms are aligned vertically to the boundary. The entanglement behaviors are influenced by the competition between the environment-induced interatomic and the environment-induced atom-plate interactions, which can be characterized by certain critical values. The maximum of concurrence generated during evolution decreases non-monotonically with the acceleration, which implies the anti-Unruh phenomenon can exist for some situations even when both environment-induced interatomic and the environment-induced atom-plate interactions are considered.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper simulates the real-time dynamics of electric flux strings in 3+1D Lattice QED using Tree Tensor Networks. It establishes that string breaking, where electric energy converts into matter pairs, only occurs when a specific resonance condition between coupling and mass is met. The work provides crucial diagnostics for future quantum simulators tackling higher-dimensional lattice gauge theories.

**Why it may be interesting.** The use of TTNs and advanced perturbation theory to model non-equilibrium dynamics in a gauge theory provides a sophisticated benchmark for simulating complex quantum field theories, relevant for understanding thermalization and real-time evolution in strongly interacting systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to understand real-time string dynamics, particularly string breaking, in three-dimensional lattice quantum electrodynamics (LGT) to bridge quantum simulations with physical QED/QCD.

**Main result.** String breaking is found to occur resonantly when the released electric energy balances the pair production mass cost, causing a measurable conversion of electric flux into matter-antimatter pairs.

**Method.** The research employs advanced numerical techniques, including Tree Tensor Networks (TTNs) with TDVP evolution, complemented by channel-resolved time-dependent perturbation theory.

**Model / system.** The system is a $3+1$D U(1) LGT with dynamical matter (staggered fermions), governed by a Hamiltonian involving electric and magnetic terms.

**Key observables.** Electric Casimir expectation value ($E^2(t)$), matter density ($N(t)$), minimal-string probability ($P_{\min}(t)$), and bipartite entanglement entropy ($S(t)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The resonance condition is $g_2(2j-1) = 4m$, where $g_2$ is the coupling and $m$ is the mass.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the dressed-site formalism to maintain gauge invariance and uses perturbation theory whose validity is checked against state-fidelity criteria.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison of dynamics at resonance versus off-resonance, showing the collapse of $P_{\min}(t)$ and the correlated rise of $N(t)$ only at resonance. Resonance spectroscopy plots show the peak shifting toward the predicted strong-coupling value.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the physical problem, detailing the Hamiltonian and simulation methods (TTN/ED), analyzing the dynamics through channel classification (string breaking, pair production, etc.), and finally presenting quantitative results confirming the resonance mechanism via both simulation and perturbation theory.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Understanding real-time string dynamics in three spatial dimensions is essential for connecting quantum simulations of lattice gauge theories (LGTs) to the physical dimensionality of QED and QCD, where transverse fluctuations and competing local processes proliferate. We present the first real-time simulations of string breaking in $3+1$D lattice quantum electrodynamics. Using tree tensor networks, we simulate the quench dynamics of electric flux strings in a $3\!+\!1$D U(1) LGT with dynamical matter. At strong coupling, the string breaks resonantly at a sharp resonance condition of mass and gauge couplings, converting electric energy into matter--antimatter pairs that screen the static charges. Off resonance, we classify all competing channels---pair production, string deformations and extensions, and flux loops---whose multiplicity, extensive for pair production and flux loops, depletes the string sector even far from resonance. A channel-resolved perturbation theory quantitatively reproduces these dynamics and their Fourier spectrum. Our results establish diagnostics and benchmarks for upcoming quantum simulators of higher-dimensional LGTs.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper details a method to generate an approximate Cubic Phase State (CPS) in the vibrational mode of a trapped ion using its native nonlinear interaction. By analyzing the state's Wigner function, Nonlinear Squeezing, and an analytical cubicity measure, the authors confirm that the resulting state possesses the necessary non-Gaussian properties required for advanced continuous variable quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it connects fundamental concepts of quantum information (non-Gaussian resources) directly to a leading experimental platform (trapped ions), providing concrete, quantifiable metrics (like NLS and cubicity) for assessing quantum resource quality.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the need for non-Gaussian elements to achieve universal quantum computation using continuous variables, focusing on generating an approximate Cubic Phase State (CPS).

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that the vibrational mode of a trapped ion, governed by a nonlinear Jaynes-Cummings interaction, can generate a state approximating the CPS with high fidelity.

**Method.** The generation and characterization rely on analyzing the time evolution of the vibrational state, using approximations based on large initial occupation numbers and quantifying non-Gaussianity via Nonlinear Squeezing (NLS) and an analytical cubicity measure.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a trapped ion, and the dynamics are modeled using the nonlinear Jaynes-Cummings interaction potential. The state evolution is analyzed in the vibrational mode, starting from a coherent state.

**Key observables.** Wigner function (showing oscillating patterns), Nonlinear Squeezing ($\sigma^{(3)}(\lambda)$), and the analytical cubicity parameter ($	ilde{\gamma}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Large initial occupation number ($N \gg 1$), the Lamb-Dicke parameter ($\eta$), and the initial coherent state amplitude ($\alpha$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on assuming a large initial occupation number ($N \gg 1$) and using a Taylor expansion of the eigenfrequencies up to the third power of the quantum number $n$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the Bessel function dependence of eigenfrequencies, the fidelity between the generated state and the ideal CPS, the oscillating Wigner function characteristic of CPS, and the NLS value confirming non-Gaussianity.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical requirement for non-Gaussian states. It then models the trapped ion dynamics using the nonlinear Jaynes-Cummings interaction, deriving approximations for the time evolution. Finally, it validates the resulting state by calculating its fidelity, visualizing its Wigner function, and quantifying its non-Gaussian nature using NLS.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Universal quantum computation with continuous variables cannot be attained solely with a set of Gaussian operations, it requires the addition of a non-Gaussian element, at least of third order in the quadrature operators, such as the cubic phase state. In this work, we present a method to generate a quantum state in the vibrational mode of a trapped ion that exhibits characteristics compatible with the cubic phase state, such as the distinctive oscillating pattern in its Wigner function. This state emerges from the nonlinear Jaynes-Cummings interaction native to the trapped-ion model, and under the assumption of an initial coherent vibrational state with a large occupation number. Consequently, the evolved vibrational state approximates the cubic phase state with high fidelity, and we use the variance of a nonlinear combination of the quadratures to characterize it. Finally, we provide an analytical expression for its cubicity that shows the high performance of the approximate vibrational cubic phase state.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the critical dynamics of a two-species model undergoing an Active-to-Absorbing State Phase Transition (AAPT) under asymmetric coupling. Using perturbative Renormalisation Group techniques, the authors find that the distinct coupling leads to unique critical behaviors for each species. This work provides detailed insights into how non-reciprocal interactions modify universal scaling laws near non-equilibrium critical points.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study of AAPTs and non-equilibrium critical dynamics is highly relevant to understanding pattern formation and phase transitions in complex systems, which shares conceptual links with open quantum systems dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the phases and Active-to-Absorbing State Phase Transitions (AAPT) in a two-species model where the species A and its mutant B are coupled asymmetrically.

**Main result.** The asymmetric coupling leads to distinct upper critical dimensions for the two species, and the dynamics exhibit unusual breakdown of scale-invariance for species A below its critical dimension.

**Method.** The analysis employs perturbative Dynamical Renormalisation Group (DRG) techniques applied to the coupled Langevin equations describing the species densities.

**Model / system.** The system is a two-species model (A and B) governed by coupled noisy Langevin equations describing local population densities, $ho_A$ and $ho_B$.

**Key observables.** Upper critical dimensions ($d_c^A=6, d_c^B=4$), scaling exponents ($eta, \alpha, 
u_\perp, 
u_\parallel, z, \eta$), and the behavior of the order parameter.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling constants ($\mu, \lambda$), diffusion coefficients ($D_A, D_B$), and the spatial dimension ($d$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the lowest order (one-loop) approximation of the DRG, and the noise terms are assumed to be spatio-temporally white.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the mean-field phase diagram showing three distinct regions and transition lines related to DP universality. Figure 4 shows one-loop Feynman diagrams contributing fluctuation corrections to model parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the coupled Langevin equations, analyzing the mean-field theory (MFT) to establish transition lines, and finally applying the perturbative DRG to calculate universal scaling exponents and identify deviations from simple scaling laws.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We explore the phases and active-to-absorbing state phase transitions (AAPT) in a two-species model, where the species A and its mutant B are {\em asymmetrically} or {\em nonreciprocally} coupled. We identify a multicritical point that connects the global absorbing state of both the species and its mutant, with a uniform active state. The critical dynamics at this multicritical point is studied within the lowest order perturbation theory. This asymmetric coupling between species A and mutant B leads to unequal and distinct upper critical dimensions $d_c^A$ and $d_c^B$ respectively for A and B dynamics. We show that the multicritical point in this model is characterised by an unusual breakdown of scale-invariance by fluctuation-induced logarithmic modulations with power law behaviour of the order parameter and correlation lengths of the species A at all dimensions $d<d_c^A$. Above $d_c^A$, conventional scale-invariance is restored. The dynamics of the mutant species B belongs to the DP universality class with an upper critical dimension of $d_c^B=4$

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a quantum simulation technique for the wave equation on open domains by implementing Convolution Absorbing Boundaries (CPML). It overcomes the inherent exponential cost barrier associated with simulating non-unitary dynamics by introducing a classically precomputed Lyapunov symmetrizer. This remedy stabilizes the quantum circuit evolution, making long-time, high-fidelity simulations feasible.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly tackles the practical limitations of simulating open quantum systems on quantum hardware by providing a robust, scalable method to manage boundary reflections and computational cost.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is simulating the wave equation on open domains using quantum circuits, which typically suffer from wave reflection at boundaries.

**Main result.** The authors successfully developed a remedy using a Lyapunov symmetrizer that transforms the simulation into a stable, time-independent conditioning factor, vastly improving the feasibility over standard methods.

**Method.** The approach combines Convolution Absorbing Boundaries (CPML) to handle open domains, Schrödingerisation to make the non-unitary dynamics quantum-implementable, and a Lyapunov symmetrizer to stabilize the resulting quantum circuit evolution.

**Model / system.** The system is the wave equation ($\partial^2_t u = c^2 \Delta u$), which is reduced to a non-Hermitian evolution $\dot{\psi} = (-iH - \Lambda)\psi$. This is then mapped to a unitary evolution on an extended quantum register.

**Key observables.** Reflection coefficients, post-selection cost (exponentially growing penalty), and the conditioning factor $\kappa_2(S)$ derived from the symmetrizer.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Absorption strength, grid spacing ($\Delta x$), simulated time ($T$), and the shift parameter ($\epsilon$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The initial approach faces an exponential cost barrier due to the indefinite Hermitian part of the absorbing generator, necessitating the use of the Lyapunov symmetrizer for practical simulation times.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the four-step quantum process (acoustic physics $	o$ quantum encoding $	o$ absorbing layer $	o$ Schrödingerisation) and compare the performance of different absorbing boundary techniques.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the need for absorbing boundaries, details the CPML implementation, identifies the structural obstruction leading to exponential cost, and finally presents the Lyapunov symmetrizer as a remedy, followed by detailed analysis of its performance and limitations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Explicit quantum circuits for the wave equation, built by Hamiltonian simulation, are restricted to closed domains, in which outgoing waves reflect off the edge of the computational region and return. We lift that restriction with absorbing boundaries of the convolution type - a complex-frequency-shifted perfectly matched layer realised through exponential-kernel memory variables - and make the resulting non-unitary dynamics quantum-implementable by Schrodingerisation. We obtain three results. First, explicit gate-level circuits for the absorbing evolution, obtained by extending a Bell- basis term-evolution circuit to projector-valued operator strings and Trotterising to second order; these run end to end on seventeen to twenty-one qubits and agree with exact references to within a tenth of a percent to a percent. Second, a structural obstruction: the memory form of the absorbing generator carries an irreducibly indefinite Hermitian part, whose largest eigenvalue grows as the square root of the absorption strength divided by the grid spacing and survives any diagonal rescaling of the memory fields. The known recovery threshold for Schrodingerisation then makes the post-selection cost grow exponentially in the simulated time. Third, a remedy: a Lyapunov symmetrizer, precomputed classically, renders the transformed generator dissipative and replaces that time-dependent penalty with a time-independent conditioning factor of several hundred, measured on grids of up to four thousand unknowns and saturating under mesh refinement. The crossover is early: past it the symmetrized recovery is cheaper by four to twenty-six orders of magnitude in post-selection cost, and at the longest horizons tested it is the only recovery that works.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes how particle transport in a disordered 1D lattice exhibits rectification-like behavior when driven by density differences. The key finding is that this non-linear asymmetry is a sample-specific effect arising from broken spatial symmetry in the disorder realization. This mechanism suggests that sample heterogeneity, rather than an imposed directional bias, can cause non-linear transport responses.

**Why it may be interesting.** The mechanism of rectification arising purely from quenched disorder heterogeneity, rather than explicit external bias, provides a novel theoretical framework for understanding non-equilibrium transport in disordered quantum/many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the current response of a boundary-driven symmetric exclusion process (SEP) subjected to quenched site disorder.

**Main result.** Rectification-like transport can emerge from sample-specific spatial heterogeneity in the disorder, even if the disorder-averaged current remains antisymmetric.

**Method.** The analysis uses approximations like Galerkin projection and mean-field closure to solve the steady-state density profile and current response.

**Model / system.** The model is a hard-core particle exclusion process on a 1D lattice, driven by reservoirs of different densities ($ho_L, ho_R$) and featuring quenched site disorder via site-dependent hopping rates.

**Key observables.** Steady-state current ($J$), current-response coefficient, and the degree of current asymmetry under reversal of the reservoir density difference ($\Deltaho$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Mean reservoir density ($ho$), reservoir density difference ($\Deltaho$), and the nature/distribution of the site disorder $\{E_i\}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on approximations such as Galerkin projection at linear order and a mean-field closure at higher orders.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically shows the SEP on a random energy landscape. Figure 6 shows the dependence of the disorder-averaged current magnitude on reservoir parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from analyzing the linear response to the current-response coefficient, then extends to nonlinear terms to find rectification-like behavior, culminating in a symmetry analysis that distinguishes sample-specific effects from ensemble averages.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the current response of a boundary-driven symmetric exclusion process with quenched site disorder. Hard-core particles hop symmetrically on a one-dimensional lattice with site-dependent rates and are injected and removed at the boundaries by two reservoirs of different densities. We approximate the steady-state density profile using a Galerkin projection at linear order and a mean-field closure at higher orders, and thereby obtain the current as a nonlinear function of the reservoir density difference. At linear order in the reservoir density difference, the current-response coefficient depends on the mean reservoir density $ρ$, in contrast to the homogeneous case. Through the linear-response relation, this dependence leads to an equilibrium current-fluctuation coefficient that is asymmetric under $ρ\rightarrow 1-ρ$. Beyond linear response, nonzero even-order current contributions break the antisymmetry of the current under reversal of the reservoir density difference, producing rectification-like behavior in individual disorder realizations. We further show that spatial-reflection symmetry of the equilibrium density profile rules out such behavior, so broken spatial-reflection symmetry of the profile is a necessary condition for rectification-like behavior. Within the present approximation, we further find that, for continuously distributed site disorder, rectification-like behavior occurs arbitrarily close to equilibrium for almost every disorder realization. At the ensemble level, however, the disorder-averaged current remains antisymmetric because the disorder ensemble is invariant under spatial reflection. These results provide a mechanism for rectification-like transport arising from sample-specific spatial heterogeneity rather than from an explicitly imposed directional asymmetry.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces spacetime fractalization to build advanced quantum error-correcting codes known as fracton Floquet codes. By embedding fractal symmetries in both space and time, the resulting codes host excitations immobile in both dimensions. This leads to highly robust quantum memories with potentially lower time overhead for fault tolerance.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses fault-tolerance in quantum computation by proposing novel topological phases whose inherent symmetries (fracton order) lead to enhanced error correction capabilities in the time domain.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper generalizes the concept of 'fractalization' from spatial dimensions to spacetime to construct novel, fault-tolerant quantum memories.

**Main result.** They construct an intrinsically spacetime type-II fracton Floquet code exhibiting extreme discrete time crystal order and superlinear fault-distance scaling with time.

**Method.** The methodology involves applying fractalization techniques, originally used for constructing fracton models, to the time dimension of quantum error-correcting codes using concepts from Measurement Based Quantum Computing.

**Model / system.** The focus is on constructing fracton Floquet codes based on fractal symmetries in both space and time, extending Type-II fracton order to dynamical quantum phases. The models are related to fractalized cluster states (e.g., 3D Raussendorf cluster state).

**Key observables.** Immobility of syndrome excitations in space and time; superlinear scaling of the Floquet code fault-distance with time; response periods scaling exponentially with system size.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The fractalization procedure uses linear cellular automaton (LCA) rules, such as the Sierpinski rule, applied across stacked code blocks.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on generalizing LCA dynamics to the quantum domain and assumes the applicability of the no-strings rule in the time-fractalized setting.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the discrete fractal patterns generated by the Sierpinski LCA rule and show the evolution of excitations in both time-fractalized and full spacetime type-II Floquet codes over several time steps.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the spatial fractalization, then extending this concept to the time dimension using LCA rules and adaptive quantum circuits, culminating in the construction and analysis of the full spacetime type-II fracton Floquet code.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We generalize fractalization, a procedure for the construction of fracton models, from space to spacetime. We apply spacetime fractalization to construct fracton floquet codes with syndrome excitations that have limited mobility in space and time. This extends the notion of fracton order to intrinsically dynamical quantum phases of matter that are inequivalent to static fracton phases. We find spacetime type-II fracton floquet codes which have no topological excitations that are mobile in space or time. These codes exhibit an extreme form of quantum discrete time crystal order with response periods that scale exponentially in their linear system sizes. In this context, the no-strings rule that characterizes type-II fractons leads to a superlinear scaling of the floquet code fault-distance with time, potentially lowering the time overhead required for quantum error correction.

</details>

**Summary.** This work provides the first fully microscopic derivation of generalized hydrodynamics for the quantum Calogero model. By constructing a specific Wigner operator, the authors derive an exact transport equation for the local density of states. This result is significant because it establishes a rigorous, non-perturbative framework for quantum transport in integrable systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The derivation provides a first-principles, non-perturbative benchmark for quantum transport in integrable many-body systems, which is highly relevant for understanding dynamics in quantum gases and strongly correlated models.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to derive an exact, microscopic transport equation for the quantum Calogero model, overcoming limitations of previous approximations like the quantum Boltzmann equation.

**Main result.** The authors derive an exact transport equation for the local density of states, showing that the dynamics are governed by a simple free-streaming equation without dispersive or collisional corrections.

**Method.** The core method involves constructing a Hermitian Wigner operator in position-rapidity phase space whose expectation value defines the local density of states.

**Model / system.** The system is the quantum Calogero model, which describes inverse-square interacting bosons on a line, governed by a specific Hamiltonian. The analysis extends to both quantum and classical limits.

**Key observables.** Local density of states $\rho(x, t, \lambda)$, particle density $n(x, t)$, and quantum conserved charges $\hat{Q}_n$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The interaction strength parameter $\ell(\ell-\hbar)$ and the large-N limit are crucial parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on the existence of a quantum Lax pair and utilizes the local density approximation (LDA) for comparison with known solutions.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes, but the structure implies showing the evolution of the local density of states $\rho(x, t, \lambda)$ over time.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the physical problem and the Hamiltonian to constructing the Wigner operator, deriving its evolution equation, and finally showing that the moments of this operator recover the exact quantum conserved charges and the generalized hydrodynamics equation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We derive an exact transport equation for the quantum Calogero model, i.e. inverse-square interacting bosons on a line, from microscopic first principles.

</details>
