**Summary.** This work investigates the light-matter interaction in the CDW material 1T-TaS2 using ultrafast THz spectroscopy. The authors propose that the coherent excitation of lattice vibrations is governed by a non-resonant Impulsively Stimulated Raman Scattering mechanism involving two-photon absorption. This technique offers a powerful tool for studying collective modes in correlated solids using low-energy THz fields.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a detailed experimental framework for probing low-energy collective excitations (like CDW or superconducting modes) using THz fields, which is highly relevant for understanding non-equilibrium dynamics and coherent control in correlated materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to elucidate the mechanism by which Raman-active collective excitations in centrosymmetric materials couple to terahertz (THz) electric fields, as direct linear coupling is forbidden.

**Main result.** The coherent excitation of lattice modes is attributed to a modified impulsively stimulated Raman scattering (ISRS) mechanism involving two-photon absorption, termed non-resonant ISRS.

**Method.** The study employs ultrafast terahertz pump – optical probe (TPOP) and terahertz third harmonic generation (THG) spectroscopy on single crystals.

**Model / system.** The physical system is 1T-TaS2, a material known for its rich charge-density-wave (CDW) phase diagram, which exhibits distinct commensurate, nearly-commensurate, and incommensurate CDW phases.

**Key observables.** Coherent phonon frequencies (e.g., 2.05, 2.37, 2.65, 2.95, 3.12 THz), THz linear transmission changes across CDW transitions, and THG spectra.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature (especially near CDW transition $T_{CDW} \sim 220$ K), THz field strength (up to 500 kV/cm), and pulse characteristics (broadband monocycle vs. narrowband multicycle).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The THz-matter interaction mechanism is non-resonant and involves two-photon absorption, distinguishing it from standard ISRS or DECP mechanisms.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the proposed mechanisms (ISRS vs. non-resonant ISRS), temperature dependence of THz transmission across CDW transitions, and ultrafast reflectivity transients revealing coherent phonon oscillations.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically investigates the coupling mechanism by performing multiple THz spectroscopy measurements (TPOP, THG) across different temperature regimes and THz field bandwidths, culminating in the proposal of the non-resonant ISRS mechanism.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Time-domain ultrafast and nonlinear terahertz spectroscopy techniques are recently applied to many condensed matter systems for investigating their collective excitations. In centrosymmetric systems, these collective modes are typically Raman-active and therefore do not couple directly to the terahertz electric field. The mechanism by which light-matter interaction realizes in these studies has not been explicitly discussed in detail. In this work, we perform terahertz pump - optical probe and terahertz third harmonic generation investigations on 1T-TaS2, a material exhibiting a rich charge-density-wave (CDW) phase diagram including the commensurate, nearly-commensurate and incommensurate CDW phases. The transition between these distinct states leaves a clear signature on the dynamical Raman response. We investigate how the Raman-active phonons couple to a broadband monocycle terahertz field as well as a narrowband multicycle terahertz field. Our results indicate that a modified impulsively stimulated Raman scattering mechanism involving two-photon absorption, also known as non-resonant Raman scattering, underlies the coherent excitation and observation of the lattice modes. These results are relevant for future spectroscopy investigation and coherent control of collective modes using low-energy terahertz field as well as cavity electrodynamical dressing of solids.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops advanced concentration inequalities to provide rigorous, nonasymptotic bounds on the uncertainty when estimating thermodynamic quantities from limited experimental data. By analyzing generalized currents, the authors quantify the required observation time and sample size needed to distinguish between equilibrium and non-equilibrium states. This offers a powerful theoretical framework for modern stochastic thermodynamics and data-driven physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous, quantitative tools for analyzing non-equilibrium steady states, which is crucial for understanding energy dissipation and fluctuation theorems in complex physical systems, bridging statistical mechanics with data science.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper derives nonasymptotic concentration inequalities to rigorously control the uncertainty associated with estimating thermodynamic quantities from finite-time or small-sample stochastic trajectories.

**Main result.** It establishes refined inverse thermodynamic uncertainty relations (iTUR) and provides explicit confidence intervals, quantifying how observation time and sample size must scale to achieve a desired accuracy, especially highlighting differences between equilibrium and non-equilibrium dynamics.

**Method.** The methodology relies on deriving concentration bounds (e.g., Cramér-Chernoff approach) for generalized currents and their sample means, extending techniques from stochastic processes theory to finite-data inference.

**Model / system.** The analysis is performed on geometrically ergodic, time-homogeneous stochastic processes governed by overdamped Langevin equations on a continuous state space. The focus is on the dynamics' ability to relax to a stationary measure.

**Key observables.** Generalized current ($	ext{J}_t$), sample mean ($ar{	ext{J}}_{n,t}$), local steady-state entropy production rate ($\Sigma_{loc}$), and the overall entropy production rate ($\Sigma_U$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Relaxation time, spectral gap ($\lambda_{	ext{gap}}$), dissipation rate, and the ratio $\Sigma_U/\lambda_{	ext{gap}}$ which quantifies the deviation from equilibrium.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The dynamics are assumed to be geometrically ergodic, and the derivations rely on bounding moment-generating functions and utilizing properties of the underlying Markovian process.

**Figures summary.** The notes do not provide a summary of figures, but they detail the mathematical forms of the concentration inequalities and the dependence of the required observation time/sample size on the dissipation factor.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from deriving concentration inequalities for the generalized current in finite time, extends these bounds to the sample mean over multiple independent paths, and culminates in deriving refined uncertainty relations and practical bounds for minimal observation time and sample size.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We derive nonasymptotic upper bounds on the probability that a generalized current of a geometrically ergodic diffusion observed for any amount of time, or its sample mean over any arbitrary sample size, deviates from the stationary mean by more than any given amount. The concentration-of-measure behavior of generalized currents is universally governed by the relaxation time of the underlying dynamics, the locally observed dissipation rate, and the intrinsic local fluctuations of the observable. We uncover stark qualitative and quantitative differences in fluctuations in and out of thermodynamic equilibrium. We further obtain refined inverse thermodynamic uncertainty relations, bounding the variance of generalized currents from above. We construct nonasymptotic confidence intervals for controlling uncertainty in thermodynamic inference from small data, i.e., from short trajectories and small samples, and provide the first quantitative answer to when a trajectory is sufficiently long and a sample is sufficiently large. As an illustration, we apply our results to currents observed on a two-dimensional Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process in and out of equilibrium and show how they can be used to rigorously detect broken detailed balance.

</details>

Black DCNOT gates implement the backbones, gray CNOT gates the legs, and red CNOT gates the decoding sequence that recovers the individual qubit labels at the end. Note that the circuits are stretched horizontally for visualiza- tion purposes to better highlight the structure of the Par- ity Twine networks and can be parallelized in practice. Therefore, they do not indicate their relative execution times.</sub>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Parity Twine Networks (PTNs) as a general, hardware-aware framework for compiling quantum circuits on neutral-atom processors. By optimizing for gate count, depth, and atom movement, the authors demonstrate significant resource savings for algorithms like the QFT. This work is crucial for realizing fault-tolerant quantum computation on scalable physical platforms.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, resource-aware compilation strategy directly tailored to the physical constraints (connectivity, mobility) of state-of-the-art neutral-atom quantum hardware.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is optimizing quantum circuit resources (gate count, depth, and atom transport) for quantum algorithms running on physical hardware.

**Main result.** The proposed Parity Twine Network (PTN) framework achieves substantial resource savings for algorithms like the QFT, leading to estimated circuit fidelities three orders of magnitude higher than competing methods.

**Method.** The authors develop a hardware-aware co-design framework by adapting PTNs to specific neutral-atom architectures, comparing different native entangling gates (CZ, CZSWAP, iSWAP).

**Model / system.** The work focuses on neutral-atom quantum processors utilizing Rydberg-mediated gates and atom shuttling capabilities. The model uses Parity Twine Networks (PTNs) to encode arbitrary interaction graphs.

**Key observables.** Two-qubit gate count, circuit depth, atom transport distance, and estimated circuit fidelity.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The fidelity ratio comparison between mobile and static implementations, and the gate fidelity ($f_{CZ}$) threshold for performance crossover.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that atom shuttling can be utilized to realize composite gates like CZSWAP, and that the framework can be extended to approximate arithmetic circuits using Optimistic QFTs.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the information flow in Parity Twine chains and the physical construction of PTNs for various layouts (linear, square, ladder). Table III summarizes the scaling of gate counts and depths for QFT across these layouts.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the PTN framework, demonstrates its application to the QFT on different layouts, compares the resource costs of CZ vs. CZSWAP implementations, and extends the concept to approximate quantum arithmetic circuits.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present an efficient implementation of the Parity Architecture for neutral-atom quantum processors. We adapt Parity Twine Networks (PTNs) to different atom layouts, native entangling gates, and atom-shuttling capabilities. This provides a general framework for hardware-aware optimization of gate count, circuit depth, and atom transport for quantum circuits encoding arbitrary interaction graphs in a common basis. Specifically, we develop PTN constructions based on different native entangling-gate realizations, namely CZ, CZSWAP, and iSWAP, providing flexibility to accommodate different hardware capabilities on both static and mobile neutral-atom platforms. Using the quantum Fourier transform (QFT) as a representative example, we demonstrate substantial reductions in two-qubit gate count, atom transport, and circuit depth. These resource savings translate into an estimated circuit fidelity three orders of magnitude higher than competing compilation strategies for a 30-qubit QFT. We further extend the construction to the recently introduced optimistic QFT and discuss the broader applicability of PTNs to other quantum algorithms on neutral-atom platforms.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically analyzes quantum-enhanced phase estimation in an SU(1,1) interferometer using photon-added even and odd coherent states. The authors demonstrate that adding photons significantly boosts phase sensitivity, pushing performance beyond the Standard Quantum Limit. Crucially, they show that this photon addition mitigates the initial dependence of the measurement on whether the state was initially even or odd.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to quantum optics and open quantum systems, as it explores using structured, nonclassical light states (photon-added states) to surpass fundamental measurement limits in an established quantum optical setup (SU(1,1) interferometer).

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how using photon-added even and odd coherent states enhances phase estimation precision in an SU(1,1) interferometer compared to standard coherent states.

**Main result.** Photon addition significantly enhances phase sensitivity, pushing the achievable precision beyond the Standard Quantum Limit towards the Heisenberg Limit, while also diminishing the initial dependence on the state's even or odd parity.

**Method.** Phase sensitivity is analyzed using intensity detection and the quantum Cramér-Rao bound, quantifying performance via the quantum Fisher information.

**Model / system.** The physical model is an SU(1,1) interferometer, which is realized using two Optical Parametric Amplifiers (OPAs) acting on input states, specifically photon-added even/odd coherent states and a squeezed vacuum state.

**Key observables.** Phase sensitivity ($\Delta\phi$), Quantum Fisher Information (QFI), and the Quantum Cramér-Rao Bound ($\Delta\phi_{QCRB}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Photon-addition number ($m$), OPA gain ($g$), and squeezing parameter ($r$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the use of specific nonclassical input states (PAECS/PAOCS) and models the interferometer using the SU(1,1) transformation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the phase sensitivity ($\Delta\phi$) vs. OPA gain ($g$) for varying $m$, comparing results to the QCRB, SQL, and HL. Other figures show the convergence of the performance difference ($\delta$) and total photon number ($N_T$) as $m$ increases.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the metrological enhancement by comparing the performance of PAECS and PAOCS against standard coherent states, first using intensity detection, and then determining the ultimate limit via QFI and QCRB, concluding with the convergence analysis.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate phase estimation in an SU(1,1) interferometer employing $m$-photon-added even and odd coherent states as nonclassical input resources. The phase sensitivity is evaluated through intensity detection and the error propagation method, while the ultimate precision limit is determined from the quantum Cramér-Rao bound with the quantum Fisher information serving as the relevant metrological quantity. Our results demonstrate that photon addition significantly enhances the phase sensitivity, increases the quantum Fisher information, and reduces the quantum Cramér-Rao bound, leading to a clear improvement over the corresponding even and odd coherent states. Furthermore, the achievable sensitivity exceeds the standard quantum limit and gradually approaches the Heisenberg scaling with increasing photon-addition number. We also find that the $m$-photon-added even coherent states exhibit a modest advantage over their odd counterparts. As $m$ increases, however, this distinction becomes progressively weaker, suggesting that photon addition diminishes the role of the initial parity of the coherent state in determining the interferometric performance.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes a novel, topologically protected method for charging quantum batteries over long distances using a superconducting qubit chain modeled by the SSH Hamiltonian. By dynamically controlling the couplings with optimized pulses, the authors ensure efficient, directional energy transfer that is robust against noise and imperfections. This establishes a theoretical framework for enhancing quantum energy storage devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses quantum energy storage and transfer, which is highly relevant to quantum computing hardware development, particularly in superconducting qubit architectures.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of developing a robust, long-distance, and topologically protected charging protocol for quantum batteries.

**Main result.** They demonstrate that pulse-controlled topological charging can achieve complete energy transfer free of backflow, and this protocol remains robust against system imperfections and noise.

**Method.** The study uses theoretical modeling based on the SSH model and analyzes the time evolution of the system's zero-energy eigenstate using time-dependent Hamiltonian techniques.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as a one-dimensional SSH-type qubit chain where the ends act as the charger and battery. The dynamics are controlled by time-dependent pulse-mediated couplings, allowing the system to adiabatically switch between edge states.

**Key observables.** Charger energy ($E_C(t)$), battery energy ($E_B(t)$), extractable work (Ergotropy), and the adiabatic ratio ($C$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The control pulse shape (cosine vs. tangent) and the disorder strength ($W$) are key parameters used to test robustness and acceleration.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the adiabatic approximation, which requires the rate of change of couplings to be slow compared to the energy gap between eigenstates.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the SSH chain setup, the energy spectrum evolution, the time dependence of energy transfer, and the robustness of the zero-energy state against disorder.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the topological framework using the SSH model to implementing the charging protocol via pulse control, and finally analyzing the performance and robustness against noise using optimized pulse shapes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum batteries have emerged as a promising new generation of energy-storage devices for powering quantum technologies. Long-distance charging is particularly attractive because it minimizes interference between the charger and the battery, thereby attracting considerable interest. Here, we propose a topologically protected long-distance charging protocol for quantum batteries based on a pulse-controlled superconducting qubit chain. By dynamically modulating the pulse-mediated couplings, we realize topologically protected energy transfer from the charger to the battery. We show that the charging process is free of energy backflow and remains robust against imperfections in pulse control. Moreover, the energy stored in the battery at the target time is fully extractable, and the protocol remains effective for relatively large system sizes. To further accelerate charging, we optimize the pulse shape and elucidate the underlying physical mechanism. Our pulse-controlled topological quantum battery protocol provides a versatile framework for implementing long-distance topological charging and establishes a theoretical foundation for designing optimal-control strategies to enhance quantum battery performance.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes Coherent Quantum Learning (CQL), a novel quantum training framework where the optimization process is replaced by coherent Hamiltonian evolution. By encoding the loss function into the system's Hamiltonian, the system naturally evolves toward parameters that minimize the loss through quantum interference. This offers a purely quantum alternative to classical gradient-based optimization in quantum machine learning.

**Why it may be interesting.** The core idea of encoding optimization into Hamiltonian dynamics is highly relevant to AMO physics, suggesting that physical evolution itself can perform complex computational tasks like optimization, potentially linking quantum simulation to machine learning.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the need for a quantum mechanical training framework for quantum machine learning, moving beyond classical optimization loops.

**Main result.** It introduces Coherent Quantum Learning (CQL), a framework where parameter evolution is governed by a Hamiltonian encoding the loss function, achieving optimal parameter estimation via quantum interference.

**Method.** The training is realized by evolving the system unitarily under a time-dependent Hamiltonian that combines kinetic terms and a loss-encoding Hamiltonian. This process replaces classical gradient estimation with coherent interference.

**Model / system.** The model uses a parameterized quantum circuit whose parameters are treated as quantum degrees of freedom. The loss function $L(x, 	heta)$ is encoded into a diagonal Hamiltonian $\hat{H}_L$ acting on the parameter register.

**Key observables.** The probability amplitude concentrating near low-loss configurations, and the final state distribution peaking at optimal parameters.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The evolution time $	au$, the structure of the loss Hamiltonian $\hat{H}_L$, and the batch size for sequential updates.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes the ability to construct block encodings for the loss Hamiltonian and relies on sequential evolution for batched training.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 conceptually contrasts traditional hybrid training with CQL's loss-guided evolution. Figure 6 numerically compares the loss evolution of CQL against traditional gradient descent for a 1D binary classification task.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the CQL concept, detailing the Hamiltonian construction using block encodings for various loss types (polynomial, smooth). It then details the sequential, batched training algorithm and validates the approach with numerical simulations on classification and phase estimation tasks.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce Coherent Quantum Learning (CQL), a training framework for quantum learning models in which the model parameters are quantum degrees of freedom evolved under a Hamiltonian that encodes the loss function. Current quantum machine learning retains classical optimization: parameters are updated by a classical outer loop using gradient estimates from measurements, and quantum coherence has no role in the training dynamics, just as in any classical treatment of the same problem. In the quantum case, a parameter register initialized in superposition evolves unitarily, and probability amplitude concentrates near low-loss configurations through interference, without gradient computation or classical feedback. We give an explicit construction using block encodings and Hamiltonian simulation, applicable to arbitrary parameterized circuits. Numerical experiments on binary classification and interferometric phase estimation confirm that the evolved distribution peaks at the optimal parameters, matching gradient-based performance. The construction is compatible in principle with fault-tolerant implementations and extends to batched training via sequential Hamiltonian evolution.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper outlines a method to experimentally test the nonlinear interaction between photons in the vacuum using high-finesse laser interferometers. By analyzing the effective Hamiltonian derived from QED, the authors predict a measurable phase shift proportional to the optical power. The work details the necessary quantum estimation techniques to determine the sensitivity and feasibility of such a measurement.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly bridges high-energy physics predictions (QED vacuum effects) with cutting-edge experimental quantum optics techniques (interferometry and quantum metrology), making it highly relevant for AMO physicists working on quantum field measurements.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper proposes all-optical methods to test photon-photon interactions in the vacuum, which are predicted by Quantum Electrodynamics (QED). It aims to do this without requiring external magnetic fields.

**Main result.** The measurable signal is a phase shift ($\phi$) or a differential phase shift ($\delta$) in a standing-wave laser interferometer, which scales nonlinearly with the circulating optical power.

**Method.** The analysis involves deriving effective interaction Hamiltonians ($\hat{H}_{	ext{SM}}$ and $\hat{H}_{	ext{TM}}$) from the Euler-Heisenberg effective action and calculating the resulting phase shifts and achievable Signal-to-Noise Ratios (SNR) using quantum estimation theory.

**Model / system.** The system is a standing-wave laser interferometer utilizing a Fabry-Perot cavity. The underlying physics is the nonlinear interaction between photons mediated by vacuum zero-point fluctuations, described by QED effective Lagrangians.

**Key observables.** Phase shift ($\phi$), differential phase shift ($\delta$), and the Signal-to-Noise Ratio (SNR).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Optical power ($P_0$), cavity volume ($V$), vacuum susceptibility parameters ($\alpha^2$), and the cavity finesse ($F$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis uses the rotating-wave approximation (RWA) and assumes the detection is shot-noise limited, with some advanced sections considering squeezed light states.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from deriving the general QED interaction Hamiltonian ($\hat{H}_{	ext{int}}$) to specific single-mode ($\hat{H}_{	ext{SM}}$) and two-mode ($\hat{H}_{	ext{TM}}$) interactions. It then calculates the resulting phase shifts and finally analyzes the achievable precision limits using quantum estimation theory (QFI).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose all-optical tests of photon-photon interactions in the matter vacuum using standing-wave laser interferometers, which do not require external magnetic fields and use conventional laser sources. We show that the interferometric detection of photon-photon scattering predicted by quantum electrodynamics is within reach of laboratory-scale experiments with a sensitivity that improves nonlinearly with the circulating power within the cavity. We outline how such an experiment could be adapted to probe properties of quantum fields beyond the Standard Model of particle physics.

</details>

**Authors:** Antonio Palamara, Nicola Lo Gullo, Antonello Sindona, Francesco Plastina  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.03226v1>

**Summary.** This work introduces a novel diagnostic tool based on the entropy of the quantum coherence distribution to characterize quantum phases. By applying it to the interacting Aubry-André model, the authors demonstrate that this entropy remains robustly sensitive to quantum phase transitions, even when significant thermal fluctuations are present, outperforming simpler moment-based diagnostics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The focus on using coherence entropy to distinguish quantum phases from thermal noise is highly relevant for AMO physics, as many experimental platforms (like cold atoms) are inherently studied at finite, non-zero temperatures.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to develop a diagnostic framework to probe both single-particle and many-body quantum phases at finite temperatures, where thermal fluctuations typically obscure quantum critical signatures.

**Main result.** The entropy of the quantum coherence distribution ($H_c$) proves to be a significantly more robust indicator than individual moments, retaining clear signatures of underlying quantum phases across increasing thermal fluctuations.

**Method.** The authors construct a diagnostic based on the full probability distribution of quantum coherence, derived via a Two-Point Measurement protocol, and analyze its statistical moments, particularly its entropy.

**Model / system.** The analysis is first applied to the non-interacting Aubry-André (AA) model to study localization transitions, and then extended to the interacting AA model containing spinless fermions with nearest-neighbor interactions.

**Key observables.** Entropy of the coherence distribution ($H_c$), first and higher-order moments of the coherence distribution, and the relative entropy of coherence ($C[\hat{ho}]$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Finite temperature ($eta$), potential strength ($V_0$), and interaction strength ($U$). The localization transition is marked by $V_0=2$ in the non-interacting case.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis of the interacting model is computationally limited to small system sizes ($N=12$) due to the exponential growth of the Hilbert space.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the finite-temperature behavior of coherence moments and entropy for the non-interacting model, and compare ground-state versus thermal-state coherence and entropy across the phase diagram of the interacting model.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the diagnostic framework using the non-interacting AA model to show $H_c$'s robustness against temperature. It then extends this analysis to the interacting AA model to characterize three distinct many-body phases (Metallic, ABI, CDW) using $H_c$ as the primary tool.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce a diagnostic framework based on the full probability distribution of quantum coherence to probe single-particle and many-body phases at finite temperature. We first apply this approach to the localization transition in the non-interacting Aubry-André model and then extend the analysis to its interacting counterpart, whose phase diagram is substantially richer. We show that the moments of the coherence distribution provide clear signatures of quantum phase transitions and capture the emergence of many-body phases. More importantly, we find that the entropy of the coherence distribution emerges as an even more powerful indicator, retaining clear signatures of the underlying quantum phases while remaining remarkably robust against increasing thermal fluctuations.

</details>

**Authors:** Qi-Cheng Wu, Yan-Hui Zhou, Tong Liu, Dong-Xu Chen, Chui-Ping Yang  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.03421v1>

**Summary.** This paper investigates how the non-Hermitian skin effect in SSH lattices can topologically enhance the quantum precision limit, quantified by the CQFI. By comparing open boundary conditions (OBC) to periodic boundary conditions (PBC), the authors show a large, tunable enhancement factor $E(N)$. This establishes a novel, experimentally accessible spatial-domain strategy for quantum metrology.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, physically realizable mechanism—the NHSE—to boost quantum sensing precision beyond standard bounds, linking topological properties directly to metrological enhancement in open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To extend the covariant quantum Fisher information (CQFI) formalism to multi-mode non-Hermitian systems to find a tunable topological enhancement mechanism for quantum metrology.

**Main result.** The CQFI exhibits a tunable topological enhancement factor $E(N)$ due to the non-Hermitian skin effect (NHSE), which can surpass 30 for modest system sizes and significantly reduce estimation uncertainty.

**Method.** The authors calculate the total CQFI by summing contributions over the Generalized Brillouin Zone (GBZ) manifold, utilizing partial-fraction decomposition to find the asymptotic scaling behavior.

**Model / system.** The study uses non-Hermitian Su-Schrieffer-Heeger (SSH) lattices, which are analyzed using a Hamiltonian framework that incorporates non-Hermitian parameters ($\gamma_1, \gamma_2$) leading to the NHSE.

**Key observables.** Covariant Quantum Fisher Information (CQFI), Enhancement Factor $E(N) = F_{	ext{OBC}}^{	ext{total}} / F_{	ext{PBC}}^{	ext{total}}$, and the joint Cramér-Rao bound.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Non-Bloch decay rate ($\kappa$), system size ($N$), and non-Hermitian asymmetry parameters ($\gamma_1, \gamma_2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on extending CQFI from single-mode to multi-mode systems and assumes the applicability of non-Bloch band theory for open boundary conditions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the total CQFI scaling (OBC vs. PBC), the enhancement factor $E(N)$ vs. $N$ (showing large enhancement), and the non-monotonic dependence of $E$ on the non-Hermitian parameters $\gamma_1$.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the CQFI formalism for non-Hermitian systems, then derives the OBC total CQFI by summing over the GBZ, contrasting this with the PBC sum. The core development involves analyzing the resulting enhancement factor $E(N)$ and demonstrating its tunability and topological origin.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The covariant quantum Fisher information (CQFI) has recently been established as the ultimate precision benchmark for pseudo-Hermitian sensors [Phys. Rev. Lett. 136, 080802 (2026)], yet existing analyses are limited to single-mode systems. Here we extend the CQFI formalism to multi-mode non-Hermitian Su-Schrieffer-Heeger lattices and reveal tunable topological enhancement enabled by the non-Hermitian skin effect (NHSE). Under open boundary conditions, the NHSE deforms the conventional Brillouin zone into a generalized Brillouin zone of radius r = exp(\k{appa}), where \k{appa} denotes the non-Bloch decay rate. While the total CQFI scales linearly with system size N, its prefactor depends critically on \k{appa}, yielding an enhancement factor E(N) = F_OBC / F_PBC that exceeds 30 for N ~ 30, substantially outperforming periodic-boundary sensors. The enhancement is robust against moderate local disorder and supports multi-parameter estimation, with the joint Cramér-Rao bound reduced by up to 15 orders of magnitude. These findings establish a spatial-domain mechanism for quantum metrology that complements time-domain strategies and is experimentally accessible using topoelectrical circuits, photonic lattices, and superconducting circuits with current technology.

</details>

**Summary.** This work details how structural distortions in moiré superlattices of nonmagnetic materials can induce altermagnetism. By combining continuum elasticity theory with Hartree-Fock calculations, the authors show that electron interactions, mediated by moiré potential variations, open correlated gaps and generate symmetry-protected spin splittings. This magnetic order is highly tunable by external strains and twist angles.

**Why it may be interesting.** The focus on emergent magnetism (altermagnetism) in nonmagnetic, structurally tunable systems is highly relevant to understanding correlated phases in novel quantum materials, which often bridge concepts from condensed matter theory and quantum simulation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper proposes a mechanism for nonmagnetic materials to develop altermagnetic order through moiré interference, driven by structural relaxation and spontaneous twirls in moiré domain walls.

**Main result.** The moiré potential, coupled with electron interactions, opens a correlated insulating gap and induces spin-splitting that can manifest as $d$-wave or $g$-wave altermagnetism, which is tunable by twist angle and strain.

**Method.** The study uses theoretical modeling involving continuum elasticity for lattice relaxation and self-consistent Hartree-Fock theory to solve for spin-resolved mean-field band structures.

**Model / system.** The system is a moiré lattice formed by lattice-mismatched nonmagnetic layers. The physics is modeled using an effective continuum Hamiltonian incorporating kinetic energy, deformation potential, and electron-electron interactions (Hubbard physics).

**Key observables.** Altermagnetic spin splitting ($\Delta E_k$), spin density map ($ho_s(\mathbf{r})$), and anisotropic transport properties.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Twist angle ($	heta$), strain components ($	ext{B1g}, 	ext{B2g}$), and electron interaction strength (Hubbard coupling).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Assumes collinear spins and initially neglects SOC for some analyses; the model relies on continuum elasticity approximations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate lattice relaxation, Wannier orbitals for different domain types, non-interacting band structures, and spin-resolved mean-field band structures showing the evolution of spin splitting with twist angle and strain.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the structural mechanism (lattice relaxation/domain walls) to developing the electronic model (Hamiltonian/HF theory), and finally analyzing the resulting magnetic order (altermagnetism) and its tunability via external parameters like strain and twist.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose a mechanism for nonmagnetic materials to develop altermagnetic order by moiré interference with nonmagnetic substrate, which is driven by structural relaxation and spontaneous twirls in moiré domain walls of lattice-mismatched moiré square lattices. When doped with one electron per moiré domain, a correlated insulating gap is opened by electron interaction. Depending on the location of the moiré potential minima, the moiré bands can show d-wave or g-wave altermagnetic splitting. The former can be enhanced by a finite twist angle; the latter is sensitive to strains that drive a transition to d-wave.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a unified theoretical framework to explain the conflicting experimental reports on the magnetic ground state of RuO2. By modeling the competition between Heisenberg and Stoner magnetism within a flat energy landscape, the authors show that intrinsic material defects select the final magnetic state by tuning the localized exchange coupling. This provides a general mechanism for understanding magnetic phase selection in correlated oxides.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of a nearly flat energy landscape where subtle perturbations dictate macroscopic quantum phases is highly relevant to understanding frustrated quantum magnets and emergent quantum phenomena.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper resolves the long-standing controversy regarding the magnetic ground state of Rutile RuO2, which is experimentally reported as either nonmagnetic or altermagnetic.

**Main result.** The magnetic state selection is governed by an exceptionally flat energy landscape, where material perturbations primarily modify the localized Heisenberg exchange interaction, leading to sample-dependent magnetic order.

**Method.** The authors developed a generalized environment-dependent spin-lattice Hamiltonian, parameterized using high-throughput DFT data and solved via large-scale Monte Carlo simulations.

**Model / system.** The system is Rutile RuO2, modeled by a Hamiltonian unifying localized Heisenberg exchange, itinerant Stoner magnetism, and SOC-mediated spin-lattice interactions. The framework is designed to capture the competition between these magnetic terms.

**Key observables.** Magnetic energy landscape, Néel temperature (T_N), staggered magnetization (M_stag), and the magnetic ground state selection mechanism.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Localized Heisenberg exchange coupling, itinerant Stoner polarization contribution, and the strength of spin-orbit coupling (SOC) anisotropy ($\lambda$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The magnetic state selection is primarily controlled by the competition between Heisenberg and Landau terms, and the framework is generalizable to other perturbations like strain or doping.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the nearly degenerate energy minima (NM vs AM states) on the flat energy landscape, and show how varying exchange coupling ($\kappa_J$) or Landau coupling ($\kappa_A$) independently modulates the calculated magnetic order parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the unified Hamiltonian and parameterization from DFT data. It then uses MC simulations to map the energy landscape, demonstrating the near-degeneracy. Finally, it analyzes how external perturbations (defects) select specific magnetic states by modifying the exchange terms, while SOC only controls orientation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Rutile RuO2 is a prominent candidate for altermagnetism, yet its magnetic ground state remains highly controversial, with experiments reporting either a nonmagnetic state or altermagnetic order. Here, we develop a generalized environment-dependent spin-lattice framework that unifies localized Heisenberg exchange, itinerant Stoner magnetism via Landau spin fluctuations, and spin-orbit-coupling-mediated spin-lattice interactions. Parameterized from a high-throughput first-principles database using machine-learning and solved by large-scale Monte Carlo simulations, the framework reveals an exceptionally flat magnetic energy landscape in RuO2, where the nonmagnetic state lies nearly degenerate with multiple altermagnetic configurations. We find that material perturbations, exemplified by intrinsic defects, select distinct magnetic ground states primarily by modifying the localized Heisenberg exchange, with perturbation-induced itinerant Stoner polarization provides an essential secondary contribution. Spin-orbit coupling controls the orientation and stability of the Néel vector, but does not determine the emergence of long-range magnetic order. These results provide a unified explanation for the conflicting experimental observations and establish a general microscopic framework for understanding how material perturbations select competing magnetic ground states in systems with nearly flat magnetic energy landscapes.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Shuttling-aware Dynamical Decoupling (SADD), a novel compiler technique for trapped-ion quantum processors. SADD overcomes the limitation of standard decoupling methods by jointly optimizing refocusing pulses and ion movement paths. By integrating transport constraints into the error mitigation objective, the authors demonstrate a significant boost in quantum gate fidelity for QCCD architectures.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the practical limitations of applying quantum error correction techniques (like DD) to solid-state or ion-trap quantum hardware that requires physical movement of qubits, providing a crucial compiler-level optimization strategy.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Conventional dynamical decoupling methods fail in Quantum Charge-Coupled Device (QCCD) architectures because they ignore the constraints imposed by ion shuttling during quantum operations.

**Main result.** The proposed Shuttling-aware Dynamical Decoupling (SADD) compiler pass significantly improves final-state fidelity by jointly optimizing pulse placement and ion rerouting.

**Method.** SADD is an offline compiler pass that minimizes a derived phase objective function by jointly selecting refocusing pulses and local ion rerouting paths.

**Model / system.** The system involves trapped ions in a QCCD architecture, where noise is modeled by a position-dependent detuning Hamiltonian, and the process is constrained by physical ion transport trajectories.

**Key observables.** Average final-state fidelity, the dephasing exponent ($\chi(T)$), and the derived phase objective function ($J_\Phi$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The degree of dephasing noise relative to control/transport errors, and the total schedule length.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The methods assume a rank-one model for detuning fluctuations and that the optimization can be performed by minimizing a compile-time phase proxy objective function.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the coupled effects of ion transport on pulse availability and noise sampling, showing how rerouting can cancel phase accumulation.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the failure of conventional DD in shuttling environments, derives a joint objective function ($J_\Phi$) that incorporates trajectory-aware phase accumulation, and then details the greedy, local optimization process using a CP-SAT solver to minimize this objective.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Dynamical decoupling (DD) helps maintain high-fidelity quantum computations by suppressing dephasing noise through carefully timed refocusing pulses. In quantum charge-coupled device (QCCD) architectures, however, where ions are shuttled throughout the device, transport constrains when pulses can be applied and affects the phase accumulated by an ion. Conventional DD methods do not account for shuttling and may therefore schedule pulses that must be omitted or shifted after transport scheduling, weakening the protection from dephasing. We therefore introduce shuttling-aware dynamical decoupling (SADD), an offline compiler pass that jointly selects refocusing pulses and local ion rerouting while preserving logical-gate timings and the total schedule length. In benchmark simulations, SADD improves average final-state fidelity over both the original schedules and a simple nearest-feasible Hahn-echo baseline when dephasing dominates control and transport errors and varies slowly enough for DD. Rerouting enables otherwise infeasible pulse timings, while spatial information about the noise can provide further gains. These benefits disappear, however, when the added transport introduces too much error. Overall, our results show that coordinating DD with ion transport is an effective compiler strategy for reducing dephasing in QCCD processors.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a detailed thermodynamic analysis of quantum error correction, treating the syndrome memory reset as an entropy-driven energy cost. By analyzing different levels of syndrome information (from abstract labels to raw bits), it quantifies the minimum work required to maintain a reusable syndrome register. The findings establish a clear hierarchy of energetic overheads that dictate the feasibility and efficiency of fault-tolerant quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous thermodynamic quantification of overheads in quantum computation, directly linking fundamental physical principles (Landauer's principle) to the practical engineering constraints of fault-tolerant quantum hardware.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper characterizes the unavoidable energetic cost associated with clearing the syndrome memory in quantum error correction (QEC).

**Main result.** It establishes a hierarchy of syndrome-memory energetic costs, showing that the cost depends critically on the joint structure of the code, the noise model, and the specific measurement/syndrome representation used.

**Method.** The analysis uses theoretical bounds derived from the Knill-Laflamme conditions and quantifies entropy production, comparing the ideal von Neumann entropy bound against the entropy generated by physical measurement processes.

**Model / system.** The focus is on subspace quantum error-correcting codes, specifically analyzing stabiliser codes under independent local Pauli noise. The system involves an auxiliary syndrome memory register that must be reset after each error correction cycle.

**Key observables.** Energetic cost ($\Delta W_{synd}$), Von Neumann entropy ($S_{vN}(\sigma)$), Shannon entropy of the syndrome distribution ($H[D_V(\sigma)]$), and various entropy measures related to syndrome representation (e.g., abstract label entropy, parity-check entropy).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Code distance ($d$), noise type (independent local Pauli noise), and the level of syndrome representation (abstract labels vs. parity-check bits).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the energetic cost of retaining and separately erasing syndrome outcomes must be accounted for, contrasting the ideal reversible erasure with physical measurement inefficiencies.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates different potential sources of energetic costs in a single round of repetition code, highlighting the syndrome-related cost ($\Delta W_{synd}$) as the primary focus.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the analysis in layers: starting from abstract KL conditions, specializing to stabiliser codes under Pauli noise, and finally analyzing the cost at the physical bit-level of syndrome recording and erasure.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum error correction acts as an entropy pump, transferring noise-induced uncertainty from a protected quantum system into syndrome information stored in an auxiliary memory. Repeated operation requires this memory to be cleared which unavoidably contributes to the energetic cost of error correction. Here, we characterise this contribution for subspace quantum error-correcting codes and identify how it depends on the joint structure of the code, the noise, and the representation of the retained syndrome information. Starting from the Knill-Laflamme conditions, we construct an effective syndrome state whose von Neumann entropy sets a lower bound on the ideal work required to maintain a reusable syndrome register. Projective syndrome readout generally generates additional entropy, and we quantify the resulting gap through measurement inefficiency. We then specialise to stabiliser codes under independent local Pauli noise and analyse two classical levels of syndrome representation. At the level of abstract error labels, degeneracies among single-qubit errors reduce the leading-order entropy of processed recovery labels. At the parity-check level, lower-weight checks reduce the marginal entropy generated by individual measurement outcomes in the low-noise regime. We identify the additional burden associated with retaining and separately erasing these outcomes as a bit-level inefficiency, and illustrate both costs for the five-qubit, Steane, generalised Shor, and rotated surface codes. Our results establish a hierarchy of syndrome-memory energetic costs and identify the code, noise, and measurement structures that control the ideal thermodynamic burden of subspace quantum error correction.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes using frequency-multiplexing on a 2D ion crystal to implement parallel, high-fidelity entangling gates necessary for quantum error correction using qLDPC codes. By designing adiabatic pulses, the scheme suppresses common errors like crosstalk and trap frequency drift. The results show that this approach can achieve a target logical error rate of $10^{-12}$ using moderate laser power, advancing the feasibility of large-scale quantum computation in ion traps.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the scaling bottleneck in trapped-ion quantum computing by proposing a hardware-efficient method (frequency multiplexing) to implement complex quantum error correction circuits.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is implementing scalable, parallel nonlocal entangling gates required for quantum low-density parity-check (qLDPC) codes without relying on slow and error-prone physical qubit shuttling.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that a frequency-multiplexing scheme on a 2D ion crystal can achieve a logical error rate of $10^{-12}$ for a specific qLDPC code using moderate laser power.

**Method.** The method combines frequency-multiplexing to enable parallel gates with adiabatic pulse design to suppress gate infidelity and crosstalk errors.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a two-dimensional (2D) ion crystal hosting qubits. The theoretical model involves designing entangling gates using an effective Hamiltonian and analyzing error sources like crosstalk and trap frequency drift.

**Key observables.** Logical error rate ($p_L$), gate fidelity ($\delta_F$), and crosstalk error ($\epsilon_c$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The system is analyzed using a $[[248, 10, 18]]$ bivariate bicycle code on 512 ions, with gate time $T=1$ ms.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the Rotating Wave Approximation (RWA) for crosstalk estimation and assumes specific noise models for error rate extrapolation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the frequency-multiplexed gate scheme, performance metrics showing error scaling with detuning and drift, and the final logical error rate vs. Rabi rate trade-off.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the need for parallel gates, detailing the frequency-multiplexing technique and its robustness, to simulating the full QEC circuit performance and estimating the final logical error rate.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum low-density parity-check (qLDPC) codes admit high encoding rates but require nonlocal entangling gates for syndrome measurement. Instead of physically moving the qubits which slows down with the increasing qubit number, here we propose to achieve parallel nonlocal entangling gates on a two-dimensional (2D) ion crystal using frequency-multiplexing. Adiabatic conditions ensure the suppression of gate infidelity and crosstalk error, as well as their robustness against slow drift in the trap frequency which is a leading error source in ion trap. We consider a numerical example of a $[[248,10,18]]$ bivariate bicycle code on a 2D crystal of 512 ions. By optimizing the mapping of the qubits and the assignment of the frequency bands for multiplexing, we show that a moderate laser power is sufficient for parallelism, and that a logical error rate of $10^{-12}$ can be achieved under realistic noise parameters.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces the HyperDet wavefunction, a novel, phase-agnostic ansatz for strongly correlated quantum systems. It uses a learnable fusion tensor optimized via VMC to achieve high accuracy across various correlated phases, including FCIs. The method's key strength is its ability to diagnose topological order and phase transitions by analyzing the tensor's structural properties, such as its singular-value spectrum.

**Why it may be interesting.** The phase-agnostic nature and the ability to extract topological invariants and symmetry information directly from the learned tensor structure make it a powerful, general tool for understanding complex correlated phases beyond simple mean-field assumptions.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a robust, phase-agnostic trial wavefunction ansatz for strongly correlated quantum many-body systems exhibiting spontaneous symmetry-breaking order, fractionalization, and/or topological order.

**Main result.** The proposed HyperDet wavefunction achieves high accuracy ($\geq 99.9\%$) across diverse correlated phases, and its structure naturally encodes parton-level topological data and tracks phase transitions via its singular-value spectrum.

**Method.** The ansatz is constructed by fusing auxiliary fermionic parton Slater determinants into physical orbitals using a fully learnable fusion tensor, optimized via Variational Monte Carlo.

**Model / system.** The method is applied to strongly correlated systems, specifically focusing on Fractional Chern Insulators (FCIs) in both bosonic and fermionic models, and tested on the extended Fermi-Hubbard model on a checkerboard lattice.

**Key observables.** Wavefunction overlap with exact-diagonalization states, singular-value spectrum of the bipartite fusion matrix, parton Chern numbers, and the connected static structure factor $S(\mathbf{q})$ (for CDW diagnosis).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Filling fractions ($
u$), hopping strengths ($t, t', t''$), and interaction strengths ($V_1, V_2, V_3$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the fusion tensor can be learned variationally to capture the necessary physics across different phases, and that the method can be extended to include internal degrees of freedom.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the structure of the HyperDet wavefunction and show the connected static structure factor $S(\mathbf{q})$ exhibiting sharp Bragg peaks in the candidate CDW phase, alongside finite-size scaling of the charge gap.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the HyperDet ansatz using a fusion tensor, details the variational optimization using VMC, and then applies the framework to diagnose and characterize multiple phases (FCI, AHC, CDW) in the extended Fermi-Hubbard model using various diagnostic tools.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Describing competing phases of strongly correlated systems often requires trial wave functions built from phase-specific assumptions. We propose the \emph{hyperdeterminant (HyperDet) wavefunction} as a phase-agnostic ansatz for both bosonic and fermionic quantum many-body systems exhibiting spontaneous symmetry-breaking order, fractionalization, and/or topological order with anyonic excitations. The HyperDet structure emerges naturally by fusing auxiliary fermionic parton Slater determinants into physical orbitals through a fully learnable \emph{fusion tensor} $\mathcal F$. Optimized using variational Monte Carlo, a single HyperDet architecture can achieve exceptionally high overlaps $\geq 99.9\%$ with exact-diagonalization ground states throughout the entire fractional Chern insulator phase in both bosonic and fermionic models, and across their nearby competing phases. We introduce the singular-value spectrum of the \emph{bipartite fusion matrix} as a structural diagnostic of fusion tensor, and find that its redistribution tracks many-body phase transitions without computing phase-specific observables. The optimized fusion tensor also encodes the parton-level topological data: it reproduces the parton Chern numbers expected for the bosonic and fermionic FCI states, completing their field-theory descriptions and the resulting topological order. Its intrinsic gauge structure further determines whether physical symmetries admit virtual lifts and, when faithful lifts exist, extracts their projective class; for the bosonic FCI, this recovers the expected parton translation fractionalization. We thus anticipate the HyperDet wavefunction to be a promising variational platform for both accurate ground-state searches and phase-diagram explorations across strongly correlated phases, and for providing interpretable theoretical insights from parton-level microscopics to field-theory descriptions.

</details>

### [Redesigning quantum theory](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03333v1)

**Authors:** Matthew B. Weiss  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.03333v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `quantum measurements` **3/5**

**Summary.** This dissertation attempts a foundational reconstruction of quantum theory by interpreting it through the lens of QBism, viewing quantum mechanics as probability theory augmented by specific nonclassical coherence rules. The authors derive these rules from first principles, showing that coherence with a specific type of reference measurement is sufficient to imply the entire structure of finite-dimensional quantum mechanics. It offers a novel, theory-agnostic method for self-testing these underlying mathematical structures.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work tackles the foundational axioms of quantum mechanics, which underpins all applications in AMO physics, by re-deriving the core rules from subjective probability principles.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to redesign quantum theory by deriving its fundamental nonclassical coherence conditions from first principles, moving beyond standard interpretations.

**Main result.** Coherence with a single reference measurement corresponding to a complex projective 3-design is shown to imply coherence with all of finite dimensional quantum mechanics.

**Method.** The authors use a QBist framework, treating quantum mechanics as probability theory supplemented by nonclassical coherence conditions, and comparing this to generalized probabilistic theories.

**Model / system.** The focus is on the abstract mathematical structure of quantum theory itself, particularly the relationship between subjective probability assignments, reference measurements, and the Born rule.

**Key observables.** Probabilities assigned to measurement outcomes, quantum states (represented by density matrices), and the structure of the state space.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The choice of the reference measurement (especially those related to complex projective $t$-designs, $t\ge 3$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The fundamental nonclassical coherence relation can be derived from first principles, often by approximating it as a gentle modification of the law of total probability.

**Paper structure.** The dissertation is structured logically, starting with an introduction covering foundations and QBism, moving through probability theory derivations, analyzing the Born identity, and concluding with discussions on the Bloch form and reconstruction of quantum theory.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

QBism understands quantum mechanics to be probability theory supplemented by additional nonclassical coherence conditions. In this dissertation, we develop these nonclassical coherence conditions from first principles, emphasizing the role of a well chosen reference measurement. After treating standard probability on subjective Bayesian lines, we demonstrate an equivalence between the QBist approach and the existing framework of generalized probabilistic theories. We show that the fundamental nonclassical coherence relation may almost always be taken to be a gentle modification of the law of total probability, and give a coherentist account of when an experimental scenario has a classical explanation. Finally, we show that when the reference measurement is chosen to correspond to a complex projective 3-design, the shape of quantum state space is implicit in the probabilities which characterize the reference measurement itself. Thus coherence with this single reference measurement, properly understood, implies coherence with all of finite dimensional quantum mechanics. We then attempt a modest reconstruction of quantum theory along these lines, one practical consequence of which is a method for self-testing complex projective $t$-designs for $t\ge 3$ in a theory-agnostic way.

</details>

### [Spurious quantum correlations](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04157v1)

**Authors:** Shashaank Khanna, Matthew F. Pusey, Roger Colbeck  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.04157v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `quantum measurements` **3/5**

**Summary.** This theoretical paper develops a framework to distinguish genuinely quantum correlations from 'spurious' ones by comparing predictions across multiple possible causal structures. It uses advanced causal inference tools and quantum probability theory to show that while some quantum effects can be explained classically under different causal assumptions, others provide evidence for genuine quantum non-locality. This refines the understanding of what truly necessitates quantum mechanics in physical theories.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous, information-theoretic framework for distinguishing genuine quantum non-locality from correlations that might simply be artifacts of an incomplete or overly restrictive causal model.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates 'spurious quantum correlations'—quantum correlations that appear non-classical under one causal assumption but possess a natural classical explanation under another, without requiring fine-tuning.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that while some non-classical correlations are spurious (explainable classically in another structure), other structures exist where the quantum nature cannot be reduced to a classical explanation, providing a criterion for genuine quantum effects.

**Method.** The analysis uses causal inference tools (like d-separation and the IC* algorithm) to compare quantum compatibility (using POVMs) against classical compatibility across various causal structures.

**Model / system.** The study centers on correlations derived from measurements on entangled particles, formalized using probability distributions $P(CDEF)$ and quantum states on tensor product Hilbert spaces.

**Key observables.** Non-classicality (violation of classical bounds, e.g., inequality (5)) and the determination of spuriousness relative to alternative causal structures.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Causal structures ($H_1, H_2, H_3, H_4$), quantum state parameters ($\alpha, eta, \phi$), and the requirement of avoiding fine-tuning.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the existence of alternative causal structures and relies on the formal definitions of classical and quantum compatibility.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare different causal structures ($H_1$ vs $G_1$, $H_2$ vs $G_2$, etc.) to illustrate where correlations are found to be spurious or non-spurious.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from Bell's theorem to define spurious correlations, develops formal definitions (NCNFT, Spurious), and then applies these tools to specific causal structures to prove theorems regarding the nature of quantum correlations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In his seminal paper, Bell [Physics Physique Fizika 1, 195 (1964)] considers the correlations that result from space-like separated measurements on a pair of entangled particles. He uses relativity theory to motivate the Bell causal structure, then shows the existence of quantum correlations that cannot be explained classically within this causal structure. Classical explanations of such quantum correlations are possible in alternative causal structures, for instance, those that allow superluminal causal influences, but, as shown in [New Journal of Physics 17 033002 (2015)], all such alternative explanations require fine tuning (causation without correlation). Here we discuss the existence of spurious quantum correlations --- correlations that look quantum in one causal structure, but have a natural classical explanation in another. More precisely, there are causal structures that admit non-classical quantum correlations, but for which the same correlations have a classical explanation in another causal structure without fine tuning. The realisation in the other causal structure can also be achieved without breaking any natural constraints on the causal structure that follow from relativity theory. However, similarly to non-classical quantum correlations in the Bell causal structure, we find other causal structures with non-classical quantum correlations that do not have a classical causal explanation in any alternative causal structure without fine tuning.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper investigates the Frenkel line, a dynamic crossover boundary in the phase diagram of the Yukawa fluid, using self-consistent relaxation theory. The authors show that this line, which separates different dynamic regimes, can be determined purely from the static structure factor $S(k)$ by analyzing the longitudinal excitation spectrum. This provides a powerful theoretical tool for characterizing the dynamics of simple, strongly interacting fluids.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical fluid dynamics, the theoretical framework of self-consistent relaxation theory and the analysis of collective excitations are highly relevant to understanding non-equilibrium dynamics in many-body quantum systems, such as those studied in quantum gases or strongly correlated materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to determine the 'Frenkel line,' a dynamic crossover line in the phase diagram of a supercritical fluid, by analyzing the collective vibrational dynamics.

**Main result.** The authors derive an analytical expression showing that the Frenkel line can be determined directly from the static structure factor $S(k)$, by equating the local maximum and the roton minimum of the longitudinal excitation dispersion relation.

**Method.** The study employs the self-consistent relaxation theory, expanding the dynamic structure factor $S(k, \omega)$ using frequency moment expansions and relating the crossover condition to the equality of two characteristic frequencies.

**Model / system.** The physical system modeled is the Yukawa fluid, which is characterized by purely repulsive interparticle interactions, making it a suitable model for supercritical fluids lacking a sharp liquid-vapor transition.

**Key observables.** The longitudinal collective excitation dispersion relation $\omega_L(k)$, the static structure factor $S(k)$, and the roton minimum frequency $\omega_L^r(k)$ are key observables.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The coupling parameter $\Gamma$ and the Debye screening parameter $\kappa$ define the thermodynamic state; the ratio $\Gamma_F/\Gamma_m$ characterizes the line.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory assumes that higher-order time scales in the expansion of the time correlation function are equalized ($rac{1}{\Delta_4(k)} = rac{1}{\Delta_5(k)} = \dots$).

**Figures summary.** Figure 2 illustrates the phase diagram showing both the melting line and the calculated Frenkel line for the Yukawa fluid. Figure 3 shows the dispersion curves $\omega_L(k)$ illustrating the relationship between the roton minimum and the main maximum.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the self-consistent relaxation theory to calculate the dynamic structure factor $S(k, \omega)$. It then derives the dispersion relation for longitudinal excitations and establishes the condition $\omega_L^m(k) = \omega_L^r(k)$ that defines the Frenkel line, finally comparing the theoretical result to molecular dynamics simulations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

For a simple model fluid, the Yukawa fluid, the condition for dynamic crossover, known as the Frenkel line, is defined. The condition is related to the fact that the roton minimum in the dispersion relation of longitudinal acoustic-like excitations exists only when the collective vibrational dynamics of particles dominates in the liquid. Based on the self-consistent relaxation theory for the Yukawa fluid, thermodynamic states are determined in which the roton minimum disappears. The obtained values of the state parameters for the Frenkel line are consistent with the results of studies in which the position of this line on the phase diagram of the Yukawa fluid was determined using molecular dynamics simulations. It is shown that the Frenkel line in this system can be determined directly from the structural characteristic -- the static structure factor. A physical interpretation of the roton minimum frequency for the simple liquids near the Frenkel line is proposed.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper analyzes the complex spectral properties of random matrices formed by multiplying a deterministic matrix by a random non-Hermitian ensemble. It extends free probability tools to this multiplicative setting, deriving functional equations that govern the spectral boundary. The results provide a powerful framework for understanding spectral flow in complex, non-Hermitian physical models.

**Why it may be interesting.** The techniques developed for analyzing spectral properties of non-Hermitian products are highly relevant to open quantum systems, where non-Hermiticity often arises from coupling to an environment, and the product structure can model sequential physical processes.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the spectral properties of multiplicative deformations of non-Hermitian random matrices, specifically matrices formed by the product of a deterministic matrix and a rotationally invariant random ensemble.

**Main result.** The boundary of the complex eigenvalue distribution of the product AB is determined by solving a system of functional equations involving specialized transforms ($\mathcal{R}_1$ and $\mathcal{R}_2$) derived from the random matrix ensemble B.

**Method.** The analysis employs advanced techniques from random matrix theory, including generalizations of free probability theory (R- and S-transforms) and the replica method to handle the non-Hermitian product structure.

**Model / system.** The model consists of the product of two large matrices, $\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}$, where $\mathbf{A}$ is deterministic and $\mathbf{B}$ is a rotationally invariant random non-Hermitian matrix ensemble. The analysis extends concepts from additive matrix sums to multiplicative products.

**Key observables.** The limiting eigenvalue distribution ($ho$), the spectral edges (boundary of $	ext{supp}(ho)$), the Stieltjes transform ($g_{AB}(z)$), and the eigenvector self-overlap ($o_{AB}(z)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The large dimension limit ($N	o\infty$), the parameters defining the deterministic matrix $\mathbf{A}$, and the structure of the random ensemble $\mathbf{B}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The existence of a continuous limiting spectral distribution $ho$ is assumed; the derivation of the multiplicative relation is noted to be significantly more complex than the additive case.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes, but the analysis revolves around deriving functional relations (e.g., Equations 23-26, 70, 71, 72) that govern the spectral functions.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing analogues for the additive case, then deriving the core multiplicative relations for the spectral density and overlap, and finally analyzing the behavior at the spectral boundary using specialized transforms.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this paper, we study spectral properties of multiplicative deformations of non-Hermitian random matrices. We consider matrices of the form $\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}$, where $\mathbf{A}$ is a deterministic $N\times N$ matrix (not necessarily Hermitian) and $\mathbf{B}$ is a rotationally invariant random matrix. We show that, as $N\to\infty$, the boundary of the complex eigenvalue distribution of $\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}$ is governed by simple equations involving the $\mathcal{R}_1$ and $\mathcal{R}_2$ transforms of $\mathbf{B}$.

</details>
