**Summary.** This paper rigorously connects quantum thermalization to quantum error correction, showing that the process of a system reaching thermal equilibrium naturally possesses optimal error-correcting properties. By analyzing information leakage, the authors derive universal bounds that govern how well information can be protected in thermalizing quantum systems, establishing a fundamental physical limit for approximate QEC.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a deep, fundamental link between non-equilibrium statistical mechanics (thermalization) and quantum information theory (QEC). For AMO physicists, it suggests that thermalization itself can be viewed as a physical mechanism for robust quantum information storage, which is highly relevant for quantum memory and computation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work aims to bridge the gap between quantum thermalization dynamics and the established framework of Quantum Error Correction (QEC). It seeks to characterize the inherent error-correcting properties present in systems evolving toward thermal equilibrium.

**Main result.** The authors establish a universal relationship between the encoding rate, distance, and thermal entropy density, showing that finite-temperature thermalization itself achieves the optimal entropic quantum Singleton bound. They also show that energy conservation limits leakage to classical information.

**Method.** The analysis uses the language of QEC, treating late-time states as codewords and analyzing information leakage using quantum mutual information, comparing it against measurement-restricted and classical information measures.

**Model / system.** The study considers isolated many-body quantum systems evolving unitarily (e.g., using an ergodic Hamiltonian). Specific models include the Scrooge ensemble for finite temperature and mixed-field Ising models for analyzing energy leakage.

**Key observables.** Encoding rate ($r$), distance, thermal entropy density ($s$), quantum mutual information $I(E:R)$, and the energy separation ($\Delta e$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature (finite vs. infinite), erasure fraction ($p$), and the energy separation scale ($\Delta E_N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often assumes that all initial product states thermalize locally to the same Gibbs state, or it analyzes the breakdown when energy differences are introduced.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the collapse of the rate-distance trade-off onto the entropic quantum Singleton bound, and show the agreement between quantum mutual information, energy-restricted mutual information, and classical divergence measures up to a critical threshold.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the general connection between thermalization and QEC, deriving the universal rate-distance bound at infinite temperature. It then extends this to finite temperatures using the Scrooge ensemble, and finally investigates the role of conserved quantities by analyzing energy leakage thresholds.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum thermalization explains how an isolated many-body system naturally evolves towards a thermal state, rendering information about the initial conditions inaccessible to local measurements. This is precisely the mechanism utilized in quantum error correction, where information is protected by design through a nonlocal encoding. In this work, we leverage this connection to port the rigorous framework of (approximate) quantum error correction to the study of quantum thermalization. Treating typical late-time states as codewords, we characterize the error-correcting properties of generic thermalizing dynamics. We numerically uncover a universal relationship between the encoding rate, distance, and thermal entropy density of the emergent code. At infinite temperature, this universal curve saturates the quantum Singleton bound, achieving the same optimal limit as Haar-random codes. At finite temperature, we introduce a code family based on the Scrooge ensemble, the natural thermal analogue of the Haar ensemble, and prove it saturates the entropic quantum Singleton bound, establishing this family as optimal within entropic constraints. Our extracted universal curve independently saturates this same bound, revealing that finite-temperature thermalization is itself optimal. Finally, we show how conserved quantities limit the error-correcting behavior of thermalization: codewords with differing energies, or other conserved charges, leak only classical information, and correctability persists until the difference reaches the scale of thermal fluctuations. Our results reveal a universal optimal coding structure in thermalizing dynamics, while introducing new optimal codes that achieve fundamental limits of approximate quantum error correction.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper mathematically proves that the mean-field Ehrenfest approach for coupled electron-nuclear dynamics is an exact theory when nuclei are treated classically. By demonstrating its equivalence to the rigorous Exact Factorization method, the authors validate the use of this simple, effective scheme within Time-Dependent Density Functional Theory (TDDFT). This provides a strong theoretical foundation for simulating complex molecular systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a formal, exact justification for a widely used, computationally tractable method (Ehrenfest dynamics) in simulating molecular and condensed matter systems, which is crucial for theoretical work in open quantum systems and molecular dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To rigorously prove that the mean-field Ehrenfest picture for coupled electronic-nuclear motion is an exact theory, rather than an approximation, in the classical limit for the nuclei.

**Main result.** The mean-field Ehrenfest molecular dynamics is proven to be mathematically equivalent to the more complex Exact Factorization approach in the classical limit, validating its use in TDDFT.

**Method.** The authors use rigorous mathematical derivations, comparing the force derived from the full quantum evolution in the classical limit against the established results from the Exact Factorization formalism.

**Model / system.** The system involves coupled electronic motion (governed by TDSE) and nuclear motion (treated classically), described by a mixed quantum-classical Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** The force acting on the nuclei, $F_i(t)$, and the electronic density, $n_e(\mathbf{r}, t)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The strict classical limit for the heavy nuclei particles.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to the mixed quantum-classical dynamics and the strict classical limit for the nuclei.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem, derives the force equation from the full quantum evolution, proves its equivalence to the Exact Factorization result through detailed mathematical steps (including appendices), and concludes by justifying the use of TDDFT.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Re-visiting the problem of the coupled electronic--nuclear motion, we prove that the so called `mean-field' Ehrenfest picture is an exact theory, rather than an approximation, in the classical limit for nuclei. By establishing its full equivalence to the much more involved Exact Factorization approach to the problem (Abedi {\it et al.}, Europhys. Lett. {\bf 106}, 33001 (2014)), our conclusion rehabilitates the mean-field Ehrenfest molecular dynamics as a simple, effective, while formally exact scheme, amenable to the use within the framework of the time-dependent density functional theory.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces 'magic' as a diagnostic tool to probe quantum resources in the Kitaev spin liquid, moving beyond entanglement measures. By analyzing the optimal magic witnesses, the authors show that the local thermal states are constrained to a compact operator space. This space is rigorously characterized as a Euclidean Jordan algebra, offering a powerful, reduced description of the underlying physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The development of a general methodology linking quantum resource diagnostics (magic) to specific, structured algebraic frameworks (EJAs) provides a powerful, reduced description for complex, finite-temperature quantum states in strongly correlated models.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how the concept of 'magic' (nonstabilizerness) can be used as a diagnostic tool to reveal the underlying compact operator structure governing local quantum resources in many-body systems.

**Main result.** The optimal magic witnesses reveal that the local thermal states are governed by compact operator spaces that possess the rich algebraic structure of finite-dimensional Euclidean Jordan algebras.

**Method.** The authors use linear programming and optimization techniques to find optimal magic witnesses, which then define an associated operator space whose algebraic structure is analyzed using Majorana fermion representations.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on the Kitaev honeycomb model (KHM) for spin-1/2, analyzing local reduced density matrices ($ho_A$) for subsystems like the three-site and six-site hexagonal plaquette at finite temperature.

**Key observables.** Reduced density matrices ($ho_A$), Robustness of Magic ($	ext{RoM}$), Optimal Magic Witnesses ($W$), and the resulting operator space $V$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($eta$), and the specific geometry of the local marginal (e.g., six-site hexagonal plaquette).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the Majorana representation of operators to interpret the algebraic structure of the operator space associated with the stabilizer polytope facets.

**Figures summary.** Figures schematically illustrate how the magic witness structure reveals a reduced operator space for describing the stabilizer polytope and genuine multipartite entanglement across temperature. Another figure shows the thermal evolution of the $	ext{RoM}$ for various marginal sizes.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by introducing the KHM and the concept of magic, then develops the methodology using $	ext{RoM}$ and optimal witnesses. It applies this to the three-site and six-site marginals, culminating in a detailed algebraic characterization of the resulting operator space.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum many-body systems exhibit rich and complex behaviour. Magic has recently emerged as a powerful new diagnostic for probing such systems, complementing other key features such as many-body entanglement. Here, starting from an investigation of the onset of magic within subsystems of a larger many-body quantum system, we show that intricate structures emerge which shed important light on the underlying many-body physics. Focusing on the Kitaev honeycomb model, we first identify the temperature below which local subsystems acquire magic. Remarkably, we find that the optimal magic witnesses active at the onset of magic reveal compact operator spaces that continue to capture the local thermal states throughout their subsequent evolution. For the six-site hexagonal marginal, this connection can be made stronger: the same operator space emerges independently from the symmetries of the local marginal and forms the symmetry-resolved plaquette algebra. When these symmetries are realized exactly, the local state lies entirely within this algebra and the reduced robustness of magic is equal to the full robustness of magic. The same reduced description can also be applied to other local quantum resources, which we illustrate using genuine multipartite entanglement. Furthermore, the operator spaces possess a rich algebraic structure in the form of finite-dimensional Euclidean Jordan algebras, with a natural interpretation in terms of bond and bond-cycle operators. Our general methodology therefore show how the onset of local magic can reveal a compact, physically meaningful operator structure underlying the finite-temperature Kitaev spin liquid, and opens up a new avenue towards reduced descriptions of quantum resources in many-body systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses NQR spectroscopy and DFT calculations to map the structural consequences of Sn doping in the kagome metal $	ext{CsV}_3	ext{Sb}_5$. The findings reveal that Sn substitution causes measurable local lattice distortions, and theoretically predicts that the system favors competing, structurally distinct ground states at high doping levels.

**Why it may be interesting.** The interplay between structural distortions (local effects) and electronic instabilities (global phase transitions) in low-dimensional, strongly correlated materials is a key area of modern condensed matter physics, relevant to understanding charge density waves and superconductivity.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how chemical substitution (Sn for Sb) modifies the competing lattice structures and electronic phase diagram in kagome metals like CsV3Sb5.

**Main result.** Sn substitution induces local structural distortions observable via NQR, and at full doping, theoretical analysis suggests the stabilization of two nearly degenerate V-trimerized structures characterized by specific phase shifts.

**Method.** The research combines experimental $^{121}$Sb Nuclear Quadrupole Resonance (NQR) measurements with first-principles Density Functional Theory (DFT) calculations to probe atomic-scale effects.

**Model / system.** The system is the kagome metal $	ext{CsV}_3	ext{Sb}_{5-x}	ext{Sn}_x$. The analysis focuses on how Sn impurities perturb the local electronic environment and drive structural instabilities in the V-based kagome lattice.

**Key observables.** NQR satellite peaks, local structural distortions, and the stabilization of V-trimerized phases with zero- or $\pi$-phase shifts.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Doping concentration ($x$), temperature (up to room temperature), and the specific site of substitution ($	ext{Sb}_1$ site).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often relies on modeling the doping effect using random site occupancy models (binomial distribution) and assumes that the observed NQR features correlate directly with local structural changes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the evolution of NQR spectra with Sn doping, showing the emergence of satellite peaks, and show phonon dispersion curves indicating lattice instabilities for the fully doped case.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first analyzing the experimental NQR data to show impurity-induced local distortions across a doping range, then using DFT and phonon calculations to predict the structural consequences, culminating in the identification of competing, nearly degenerate ground states at high doping.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Understanding the effect of chemical substitution on competing phases of kagome metals is crucial for disentangling the interplay between local structural distortions and electronic instabilities. In \cvs, Sn substitution strongly modifies the electronic phase diagram, yet the microscopic mechanism driving this remains unclear. Here, we combine $^{121}$Sb nuclear quadrupole resonance (NQR) measurements and density functional theory calculations to investigate the atomic-scale effects of Sn substitution in CsV$_3$Sb$_{5-x}$Sn$_x$. At low Sn concentrations, the observed satellite NQR peaks exhibit signatures of local structural distortion induced by Sn substitution, qualitatively consistent with our computational analysis. These impurity-induced features persist across the entire experimentally investigated doping range, up to $x$ = 0.65, and remain observable up to room temperature. For $x=1$, the fully doped idealized case, the estimated dynamical instabilities of the kagome lattice suggest the stabilization of two nearly energy-degenerate equilibrium structures characterized by V-trimers, distinguished by a zero- or $π$-phase shift between adjacent layers along the $c$-axis. Together, these results show that Sn substitution drives a complex interplay between local impurity-induced distortions and competing structural instabilities in vanadium-based kagome compounds.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a theoretical model for discrete-time quantum walks by mapping them onto a lattice of energy-dependent point interactions. By relating the coin operator to the scattering matrix, the authors show that the walk's spectral properties and entanglement dynamics are controlled by the transmission coefficient. This framework is valuable for understanding wave propagation in structured media.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful, physically transparent link between wave scattering theory (Kronig-Penney type potentials) and the dynamics of quantum walks, offering tunable control over spectral gaps and entanglement via the energy parameter.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work extends the scattering quantum walk (SQW) framework to model discrete-time quantum walks (DTQWs) on a lattice featuring energy-dependent point interactions.

**Main result.** The transmission modulus controls the quasienergy gap, bandwidth, and long-time coin-position entanglement, while different point interaction types yield distinct spreading and entanglement behaviors.

**Method.** The analysis compares the Coined Quantum Walk (CQW) and Scattering Quantum Walk (SQW) formalisms, utilizing the scattering matrix derived from zero-range potentials to define an energy-dependent coin operator.

**Model / system.** The model is a DTQW analog of a periodic array of point interactions, mathematically realized by relating the coin operator to the scattering matrix of zero-range potentials, allowing the walker's wavenumber to act as a continuous control parameter.

**Key observables.** Quasienergy spectrum, spatial probability distribution, and coin-position entanglement.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The walker's wavenumber (energy $k$), the transmission modulus $|t(k)|$, and the specific type of point interaction ($\delta, \delta', 	ext{crossed, asymmetric}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The dynamics are analyzed by assuming the scattering matrix $S(k)$ and coin operator $C_c(k)$ are uniform across all vertices at a fixed energy $k$.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare probability curves for SQW and CQW, show reflection/transmission probabilities vs. $k$ for four interaction types, and illustrate the time evolution of entanglement for these cases.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the equivalence between CQW and SQW, derives the energy-dependent coin operator from scattering theory, analyzes the spectral properties using the transmission modulus, and finally characterizes the long-time entanglement behavior for various point interactions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this work, we extend the scattering quantum walk (SQW) framework to a lattice of energy-dependent point interactions. This yields, within the coined quantum walk (CQW) formalism, a coin operator that is directly related to the scattering matrix of zero-range potentials. The model thus provides a discrete-time quantum-walk (DTQW) analog of a periodic array of point interactions of the Kronig-Penney type, where the walker's wavenumber serves as a continuous, physically transparent control parameter for the coin operation. We analyze the spectra and the dynamics of position probability and entanglement, yielding distinct results for specific energies and point interactions. We relate the spectral structure to the spatial probability distribution and explicitly characterize the long-time entanglement behavior for each point interaction. The transmission modulus determines the quasienergy gap, bandwidth, and maximum group velocity, while also controlling the long-time coin-position entanglement for the initial state considered. The four families of one-dimensional point interactions ($δ$, $δ'$, crossed and asymmetric) realize qualitatively distinct transmission profiles and span the full range of behavior, including enhanced or strongly suppressed spreading and oscillatory entanglement.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates that entanglement can be generated across a black hole event horizon using gravitational interactions. However, it finds that attempting to extract this entanglement radially causes severe decoherence via soft-graviton emission. A key finding is the geometric duality showing that tangential harvesting protocols can bypass this decoherence, allowing entanglement to be effectively teleported to the exterior.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a novel, extreme gravitational setting to test fundamental principles of quantum information preservation, linking quantum entanglement dynamics directly to general relativity and Hawking radiation analogues.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper challenges the assumption that entanglement cannot be dynamically created across a black hole event horizon, and compares the feasibility of radial versus tangential entanglement extraction.

**Main result.** Dynamical entanglement can be created across the horizon, but radial extraction is severely limited by soft-graviton bremsstrahlung, whereas tangential harvesting via optical probes allows for state teleportation to infinity.

**Method.** The analysis uses a gravitational retarded-potential model and quantum information protocols, including Bell-state measurements and decoherence analysis based on the Horodecki criterion.

**Model / system.** The system involves two macroscopic optical probes (A and B) falling across a Schwarzschild black hole event horizon, modeled using retarded Liénard-Wiechert potentials and quantum state manipulation techniques.

**Key observables.** Entangling Phase ($\Phi$), Decoherence Bound ($\Gamma$), Extraction Error Rate ($p$), and CHSH expectation value.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The decoherence bound $\Gamma \ge rac{729}{160\pi} \Phi$, and the minimum variational constant $C^* = 27/16$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model relies on a leading-order quadrupole approximation and assumes the use of macroscopic optical probes to manage recoil and bypass acoustic shattering.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the spacetime diagram of retarded signals and soft-graviton emission during radial escape, and show the extraction error rate and suppressed CHSH correlations for radial versus tangential protocols.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical possibility of entanglement generation across the horizon using a gravitational potential model. It then contrasts the highly decohering process of radial extraction with the successful, symmetry-preserving protocol of tangential entanglement harvesting.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

It has long been assumed that a particle---having crossed a black hole event horizon unentangled with another particle in the exterior universe---can no longer dynamically entangle with that particle. Here, we demonstrate within a gravitational retarded-potential model that entanglement can in fact be created from scratch between freely falling spatial superpositions across an event horizon. Yet, extracting this state radially requires non-inertial deceleration, triggering soft-graviton bremsstrahlung, and establishing a strict dephasing bound in terms of entangling phase, $Γ\ge\frac{729}{160π}Φ$. This decoheres the entangled state. By contrast, equivalent macroscopic optical masses allow local tangential harvesting of entanglement via quantum erasure, thus revealing a remarkable geometric duality: Spacetime irreversibly degrades entanglement the moment the localized mass is dragged away from the horizon, while allowing the transverse teleportation of its entangled state to infinity.

</details>

**Summary.** This work explores using non-KAM quantum systems, modeled by the Kicked Harmonic Oscillator, for enhanced quantum sensing. By calculating the Quantum Fisher Information (QFI), the authors demonstrate that resonance conditions lead to a unique, highly enhanced polynomial growth in QFI. This identifies resonance-induced symmetry as a powerful, distinct metrological resource.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, physically realizable mechanism (resonance-induced symmetry) for achieving super-classical scaling in quantum metrology, offering a resource distinct from traditional chaos-based enhancements.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates utilizing non-Kolmogorov-Arnold-Moser (non-KAM) systems as quantum sensors to enhance quantum parameter estimation.

**Main result.** The Quantum Fisher Information (QFI) is remarkably enhanced at resonance conditions, exhibiting anomalous hexic growth ($I(t) \sim t^6$) which is distinct from standard chaos-assisted sensing.

**Method.** The study combines numerical analysis with analytical derivations to compute the QFI, relating its growth rate to the algebraic growth of the mean excitation number ($\langle\hat{n}(t)angle \sim t^\alpha$).

**Model / system.** The primary model is the Kicked Harmonic Oscillator (KHO), a paradigmatic non-KAM system, whose dynamics are governed by a time-periodic Hamiltonian involving harmonic oscillator terms and periodic kicks.

**Key observables.** Quantum Fisher Information (QFI), Loschmidt Echo (LE), and the mean excitation number $\langle\hat{n}(t)angle$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The commensurability parameter $R = 2\pi/\omega	au$, which dictates whether the system is resonant or non-resonant.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the assumption that the enhancement stems from resonance-induced translational symmetry rather than exponential instability, and the QFI is bounded by $I(t) \lesssim t^{2\alpha+2}$.

**Figures summary.** The notes mention comparing LE behavior for different resonance conditions, showing enhanced growth at resonance points.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces QFI, details the KHO model, presents main results comparing growth rates ($\alpha=0, 1, 2$), elucidates the role of translation invariance, and compares the findings with classical Fisher Information.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Non-KAM (Kolmogorov-Arnold-Moser) systems, when subjected to weak time-dependent perturbations, exhibit an abrupt transition to classical chaos through the breakdown of invariant phase-space tori. We showcase the utilization of non-KAM systems in the quantum regime as quantum sensors, leveraging their sensitivity at \textit{resonances}. Quantum Fisher information (QFI) is a central quantity in quantum parameter estimation theory that measures how much information a quantum state contains about an unknown parameter that is encoded into it. In other words, it quantifies the sensitivity of a quantum state to small changes in that parameter. In this work, through numerical analysis in conjunction with analytical results, we study the performance of the non-KAM systems for quantum sensing applications by computing the QFI. We find that the growth of the QFI is remarkably enhanced when the resonance condition is satisfied. For frequency estimation under Floquet unitary encodings, we derive a transport bound: if the mean excitation number grows as $\langle\hat n(t)\rangle\sim t^α$, the QFI obeys $I(t)\lesssim t^{2α+2}$. The quantum kicked harmonic oscillator, a paradigmatic non-KAM system, realizes the full hierarchy: localized dynamics ($α=0$) yield quadratic growth, delocalized diffusion along stochastic webs ($α=1$) yields quartic growth, and translationally invariant resonances ($α=2$) saturate the bound with anomalous hexic growth, $I(t)\sim t^{6}$, established analytically at resonance $R=2$ and numerically at $R=4$. The enhancement stems from resonance-induced translational symmetry rather than exponential instability, identifying non-KAM resonances as a metrological resource distinct from chaos-assisted and criticality-based sensing.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically analyzes how $p$-wave antialtermagnets can generate magnetization and nonreciprocal orbital currents using magnons. The key finding is that the orbital magnetic moments of these magnons mediate a linear thermal Edelstein effect. This connects the fields of magnonics and orbitronics by showing how thermal gradients can induce orbital currents.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between magnon orbital moments, thermal gradients, and resulting currents provides a novel mechanism for controlling quantum states using thermal energy, which is relevant for understanding energy transport in quantum materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the generation of magnetization and nonreciprocal orbital currents in $p$-wave antialtermagnets via magnon excitations.

**Main result.** These magnets efficiently generate magnetization via a linear thermal Edelstein effect from orbital magnetic moments, and they exhibit perfectly nonreciprocal orbital currents through even-order nonlinear responses.

**Method.** The analysis relies on symmetry arguments, linear spin wave theory, and the application of the Boltzmann equation to the magnon distribution function.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using a generalized Heisenberg spin Hamiltonian incorporating various exchange, anisotropy, and Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interactions, applied to a coplanar ground state inspired by CeNiAsO.

**Key observables.** Magnon spin ($\mathbf{s}_{qm}$), orbital magnetic moment ($\mu_{qm}$), thermal magnon Edelstein effect, and nonreciprocal orbital currents.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis considers the presence or absence of spin-orbit coupling and the specific nature of the $p$-wave magnetic ordering.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the existence of a coplanar ground state and utilizes approximations like the relaxation time in the Boltzmann equation.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically shows the magnetic ground state on a rotated square lattice. Figure 2 displays the magnon spectrum ($\omega_{qm}$) along high-symmetry directions, highlighting $p_x$-wave momentum dependence.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical framework using a complex spin Hamiltonian, analyzes the magnon spectrum and properties using linear spin wave theory, and then derives transport coefficients by solving the Boltzmann equation to determine the temperature and gradient responses.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that $p$-wave magnets efficiently generate magnetization via a linear thermal Edelstein effect arising from the orbital magnetic moments of their magnons. Furthermore, they can generate perfectly nonreciprocal orbital currents through a purely even-order nonlinear response. This makes them promising candidates for orbital-current rectification. Because these transport phenomena originate from the orbital magnetic moment of magnons, they connect magnonics and orbitronics. More generally, we find that odd-parity-wave magnets with coplanar ground states host magnons with zero spin magnetic moment. Instead the magnons carry a collinear, out-of-plane orbital magnetic moment, realizing an orbital version of antialtermagnetism. We establish these general results using symmetry arguments and demonstrate them explicitly for a coplanar ground state with the minimal number of sublattices, inspired by the ground state of CeNiAsO. Our conclusions hold both in absence and presence of spin-orbit coupling.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a mathematical framework called 'network design' to address the problem of ensuring that complex systems assemble into a single, unique structure. By encoding local building block constraints into a 'design set,' the authors derive the Unigraphical Design Theorem to test for this guaranteed reproducibility. This reframes system assembly as a mathematically testable property, opening a path for the rational engineering of complex structures.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical network theory, the concept of encoding local, deterministic constraints to guarantee a unique global structure is highly relevant to understanding the emergent order in quantum many-body systems or the constraints governing quantum circuit design.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Network science lacks mechanisms to consistently reproduce exact network topologies, focusing instead on generating statistical ensembles.

**Main result.** The authors introduce the Unigraphical Design Theorem (UDT) to mathematically determine when a system's local constraints guarantee a unique, reproducible network assembly.

**Method.** They developed a 'network design' framework encoding local constraints into a design set, extending unigraph theory to incorporate node types and pairwise binding rules.

**Model / system.** The framework is applied to diverse systems, including protein complexes, molecular assemblies, electronic circuits, and robotic components, treating them as networks built from constrained building blocks.

**Key observables.** Unigraphicality (whether the final topology is unique, |F|=1), Specificity ($\psi$), Diversity ($\phi$), and Redundancy ($r$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The design set components: Component Vector (N), Capacity Vector (C), and Binding Matrix (O).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The initial definition of the set F deliberately disregards system-specific dynamics (like binding energies) to provide a general mathematical test.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the framework using protein complexes (eIF2B) to show the difference between statistical ensemble generation and unique, constraint-driven assembly, and show the application to various physical systems.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the gap between statistical network modeling and exact reproducibility, introduces the design set and the UDT, details the concepts of unigraphical and guided assembly, applies this to thousands of real-world systems, and concludes with implications for rational engineering.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

From protein complexes to electronic circuits, many natural and engineered systems function only if assembled in an exact, reproducible fashion. The structure of each of these systems can be understood as a network, yet network science lacks the mechanisms to consistently reproduce exact topologies, focusing instead on generating network ensembles. We introduce the framework of network design where we encode the local constraints obeyed by a system's building blocks in a design set, and derive the Unigraphical Design Theorem, which determines when these constraints guarantee reproducible assembly into a unique structure, a process we call unigraphical assembly. For systems whose design sets do not specify a unique outcome, we identify guided assembly as a second route to reproducibility, in which temporal ordering decomposes construction into unigraphical steps. Applying these results to 3,618 reproducible systems, including protein complexes, molecules, and robots, we classify those that undergo unigraphical assembly and those that require guided assembly. We further identify a diversity-redundancy boundary that explains how systems trade component variety for structurally interchangeable parts while retaining unique assembly. Finally, we experimentally test the theory using 3D-printed components to re-engineer generative construction sets into systems that assemble unigraphically into prescribed topologies. Network design thus reframes reproducibility as a mathematically testable property of real networks, opening a route to the rational engineering of complex systems.

</details>

### [Enhancing noise robustness in device-independent conference key agreement with asymmetric parity-CHSH inequalities](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03551v1)

**Authors:** Makoto Ishihara, Wojciech Roga, Jonatan Bohr Brask, Masahiro Takeoka  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.03551v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `quantum measurements` **2/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel multipartite Bell inequality, the asymmetric parity-CHSH inequality, to improve the security of device-independent conference key agreement. By deriving a tight analytical lower bound on the conditional von Neumann entropy, the authors demonstrate that this inequality, combined with noisy preprocessing, significantly enhances the protocol's resilience to experimental noise and detection inefficiencies. This advances the practical feasibility of multi-party quantum cryptography.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides concrete, mathematically rigorous tools (entropy bounds derived from generalized Bell inequalities) to quantify and improve the security of quantum key distribution protocols, which is central to quantum information theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to enhance the noise robustness of device-independent conference key agreement (DI-CKA) protocols.

**Main result.** The combination of a new asymmetric parity-CHSH inequality and noisy preprocessing significantly improves the key rate's robustness against detection inefficiencies and various types of noise.

**Method.** The authors derive a tight analytical lower bound on the conditional von Neumann entropy, which quantifies the achievable key rate, based on the violation of the generalized Bell inequality.

**Model / system.** The protocol relies on an N-partite partially entangled Greenberger-Horne-Zeilinger (GHZ) state shared among multiple remote parties.

**Key observables.** Conditional von Neumann entropy $H(A|E, x^*)$, the asymmetric parity-CHSH value ($S_{P	ext{-CHSH}}^\alpha$), and the resulting key rate $K$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Detection efficiency ($\eta$), global depolarizing noise ($p_G$), local depolarizing noise ($p_L$), and the asymmetry parameter ($\alpha$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the parties perform specific measurements and models the imperfections using depolarizing noise channels.

**Figures summary.** The figures and tables summarize the threshold values ($\eta_{th}, p_G^{th}, p_L^{th}$) required for a positive key rate, demonstrating how optimizing parameters improves robustness across different numbers of parties (N).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the asymmetric parity-CHSH inequality, details the DI-CKA protocol, derives the entropy bound, proves its tightness, and finally analyzes the robustness against various experimental imperfections.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Conference key agreement allows multiple remote parties to establish a shared secret key with information-theoretical security. In device-independent conference key agreement, security can be guaranteed with minimal assumptions on the devices used, provided that a violation of a Bell inequality is observed. However, implementations are extremely challenging because high detection efficiency is required to observe loophole-free Bell violations. Here, we enhance the robustness of device-independent conference key agreement by introducing a new family of multipartite Bell inequalities called the asymmetric parity-Clauser-Horne-Shimony-Holt (CHSH) inequalities. We derive a tight analytical lower bound on the conditional von Neumann entropy of the outcomes of one of the parties in a protocol based on this inequality, including noisy preprocessing. Using this bound, we analyze robustness to detection inefficiencies as well as local and global depolarizing noise. We show that the combination of the asymmetric parity-CHSH inequality and noisy preprocessing can significantly improve the robustness to imperfections.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper uses advanced MCMC simulations to determine the asymptotic growth rates of $d$-dimensional integer partitions for $d=3$ to $d=6$. The key finding is that in these intermediate dimensions, the growth rate of these partitions exceeds that of the simpler MacMahon numbers. This provides crucial quantitative data filling a gap in the understanding of partition function asymptotics in statistical physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the topic is pure combinatorics/statistical mechanics, the use of advanced MCMC techniques (like Wang-Landau) to estimate partition function asymptotics is a sophisticated numerical method relevant to calculating partition functions in complex quantum lattice models.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to numerically estimate the asymptotic growth rates of the number of $d$-dimensional integer partitions, $p_d(n)$, for intermediate dimensions $d=3, 4, 5, 6$.

**Main result.** The simulations establish that for $d=3$ through $d=6$, the asymptotic growth of $p_d(n)$ is strictly faster than that of the corresponding MacMahon numbers.

**Method.** The authors employed Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) simulations, utilizing adaptive weight learning and the Wang-Landau algorithm to estimate the density of states and average edge counts on the partition graph.

**Model / system.** The system studied is the counting of $d$-dimensional integer partitions, $p_d(n)$, which relates to the partition function of certain statistical models.

**Key observables.** The primary observables are the asymptotic limits of $\log p_d(n)$ scaled by appropriate powers of $n$, such as $\lim_{n	o\infty} n^{-d/(d+1)} \log p_d(n)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensions $d=3, 4, 5, 6$ are the focus, comparing results against known MacMahon leading order coefficients.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The main assumptions include the existence of the asymptotic limits and the ability to estimate the stationary distribution of the partition graph using MCMC techniques.

**Figures summary.** The notes do not provide a summary of figures, but they detail the numerical results for the asymptotic limits for $d=3, 4, 5, 6$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical context and the open question (comparison to MacMahon numbers) to detailing the advanced MCMC methodology (WL algorithm, graph traversal) and finally presenting the numerical fitting and comparison of the derived asymptotic coefficients.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

It was recently shown by Yeliussizov \cite{Yeliussizov} that integer partitions in dimensions $d \geq 7$ asymptotically grow strictly faster than MacMahon numbers. As MacMahon numbers match with integer partitions in dimensions $d = 1,2$, the comparison of asymptotics of integer partitions with MacMahon numbers in intermediate dimensions ($d = 3,4,5,6$) is an open question. In this work, we perform Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) simulations till $N=15000$ by using adaptive weight learning followed by conventional MCMC steps to numerically estimate the asymptotics of integer partitions in these intermediate dimensions. We numerically establish that in these intermediate dimensions, partitions asymptotically grow faster than MacMahon numbers. More specifically, assuming that the limits exist, we show: $\lim_{n\to\infty}n^{-3/4}\log p_3(n) = 1.8196 \pm 0.0019$, $\lim_{n\to\infty}n^{-4/5}\log p_4(n) = 1.7215 \pm 0.0045$, $\lim_{n\to\infty}n^{-5/6}\log p_5(n) = 1.6521 \pm 0.0059$, and $\lim_{n\to\infty}\log n^{-6/7}p_6(n) = 1.652 \pm 0.021$ for partitions in dimensions $d=3,4,5,$ and $6$ respectively. These numbers are all larger than MacMahon leading order asymptotic coefficients of $1.7898, 1.6614, 1.5737,$ and $1.509$ respectively. Additionally, we also find estimates for some of the sub-leading asymptotic terms in $\log p_d(n)$ in each of the dimensions.

</details>

**Summary.** This work explores the complex quantum phase diagram of a spin chain modified by anisotropic four-spin interactions. By mapping the system to fermions, the authors identify multiple topologically distinct gapped phases, including higher-winding sectors up to $|
u|=3$. This demonstrates that extended interactions offer a powerful mechanism to realize and study complex topological matter in one-dimensional quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The ability to engineer higher-winding topological phases using controllable, anisotropic multispin interactions provides a versatile theoretical platform for understanding exotic quantum states beyond standard short-range models.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the quantum critical and topological properties of a spin-1/2 XX chain incorporating anisotropic four-spin cluster interactions under a transverse magnetic field.

**Main result.** The model exhibits a rich phase diagram featuring multiple gapped topological phases characterized by quantized winding numbers up to $|
u|=3$, which are stabilized by the extended cluster interactions.

**Method.** The analysis employs fermionization techniques, mapping the spin Hamiltonian to a quadratic fermionic form solvable via Bogoliubov transformations in momentum space.

**Model / system.** The system is a spin-1/2 XX chain with nearest-neighbor XX interactions ($H_{XX}$) augmented by anisotropic four-spin cluster interactions ($H_{CI}$) and subjected to a transverse magnetic field ($h$).

**Key observables.** Quantized winding numbers ($
u$), the bulk entanglement spectrum degeneracy pattern, and the ground-state energy density ($E_G$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Exchange anisotropy ($J$), transverse field ($h$), and cluster interaction strengths ($\alpha, eta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on mapping the spin system to spinless fermions and analyzing the resulting single-particle energy spectrum $\varepsilon(k)$.

**Figures summary.** Figures schematically represent the four-spin cluster interaction Hamiltonian and show the topological phase diagram using winding numbers in the $(eta, h)$ plane, alongside the bulk entanglement spectrum for different winding numbers.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by introducing the model Hamiltonian, detailing the fermionization and diagonalization methods, and then analyzing the resulting phase diagram, topological transitions, and critical points.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the quantum critical and topological properties of a spin-$1/2$ XX chain with anisotropic four-spin cluster interactions in a transverse magnetic field. The interplay of exchange anisotropy, cluster interactions, and the external field produces a rich phase diagram with multiple gapped topological phases and distinct quantum critical boundaries. We identify several Lifshitz-type transitions arising from reconstructions of the low-energy Fermi-point structure, including an unconventional multicritical point where distinct critical branches intersect and the momentum-space topology undergoes a singular reorganization. In the isotropic limit, a conventional Lifshitz transition emerges through the merging and annihilation of Fermi points, revealing distinct mechanisms of Fermi-point reconstruction within the same model. The gapped phases are characterized by quantized winding numbers $ν=0,\pm1,-2,$ and $\pm3$, with the higher-winding phases induced by the extended cluster interactions. The $ν=\pm3$ phases exhibit a richer topological structure than conventional short-range Kitaev-type chains and are separated from other sectors by field- and interaction-driven gap closings. These topological distinctions are further reflected in characteristic degeneracy patterns of the lowest levels of the bulk entanglement spectrum. Our results demonstrate that anisotropic multispin interactions provide a versatile route to realizing higher-winding topological phases and unconventional multicriticality in one-dimensional quantum systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes holographic fluctuation equations containing apparent singularities to determine the resulting spectral properties. By studying the near-boundary behavior, the authors show that the singularity's merging with the boundary dictates whether the spectral function acquires a zero or a pole. This provides a general, powerful tool for calculating transport coefficients in strongly coupled quantum matter systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between bulk singularities (apparent singularities) and the spectral properties (zeros/poles) of boundary correlators is a powerful tool for understanding transport and response functions in strongly interacting quantum systems modeled by holography.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how the merging of an apparent singularity with the boundary affects the spectral functions derived from holographic fluctuation equations.

**Main result.** The merging of the singularity can generate either a zero or a pole in the corresponding spectral function, and the holographic product formula remains applicable.

**Method.** The analysis uses near-boundary matching techniques on second-order linear differential equations (fluctuation equations) derived from holographic setups.

**Model / system.** The model is a holographic setup, relating bulk fluctuations in backgrounds like AdS black branes to boundary two-point functions ($ho$). The governing equations are reduced to Schrödinger-like forms with potentials that can exhibit apparent singularities.

**Key observables.** Spectral function $ho(\omega)$ and the two-point correlator $G_{12}(\omega, k_i)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The approach depends on classifying equations into $(−)$ and $(+)$ classes, and analyzing the limit where an apparent singularity approaches the boundary ($r^* 	o 0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the apparent nature of the singularity and relies on matching solutions between inner (near-boundary) and outer regions.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the general holographic setting for spectral functions, details the near-boundary analysis using inner/outer matching, deduces general properties for zeros based on the singularity merging, and concludes with applications.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study general holographic fluctuation equations with apparent singularities, and the associated spectral functions. We consider the regime where an apparent singularity approaches the boundary, for which the near-boundary behavior of solutions can be determined via an inner/outer matching type of calculation. Depending on properties of the equation, we find that the merging of an apparent singularity with the boundary may generate either a zero or a pole for the corresponding spectral function. Our results also indicate that the recently introduced holographic product formula still applies in the presence of apparent singularities.

</details>

### [Large deviations for linear regressions](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03111v1)

**Authors:** Silvia Bartolucci, Fabio Caccioli, Francesco Caravelli, Pierpaolo Vivo  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.03111v1>

**Summary.** This theoretical work calculates the large-deviation statistics for the minimum training loss in high-dimensional linear regression. Using advanced statistical physics tools like the replica method, the authors derive the rate function that governs rare fluctuations in the optimal loss. The results confirm the theoretical framework's consistency across different regularization methods (Ridge, Lasso) and regimes.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the core topic is machine learning statistics, the mathematical machinery—replica calculations, large deviation theory, and the use of order parameters—is highly relevant to disordered systems and statistical physics problems in condensed matter.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper computes the large-deviation statistics of the minimum training loss in linear regression, characterizing rare sample-to-sample fluctuations beyond the typical Gaussian regime.

**Main result.** The rate function governing the large deviation probability $P(E = P\xi) \approx e^{-P \psi(\xi)}$ is derived for various regularizers, showing consistency between replica methods and direct calculations.

**Method.** The zero-temperature replica method is employed to calculate the scaled cumulant generating function $\Phi(s)$, from which the rate function $\psi(\xi)$ is obtained via a Legendre transform.

**Model / system.** The model is standard linear regression with $N$ observations and $P$ predictors, using Gaussian design and noise. The loss function incorporates general separable regularizers like Ridge ($\ell_2$) and Lasso ($\ell_1$).

**Key observables.** The minimum regularized training loss $E$, the intensive loss $\xi = E/P$, and the rate function $\psi(\xi)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio $r = P/N$ (predictors to observations), the regularization strength $\lambda$, and the signal distribution $p(eta)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes Gaussian design and noise, and for some derivations, the light-tailedness of the teacher prior $p(eta)$ is required.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the rate function $\psi(\xi)$ and the predicted mean loss for OLS, Ridge, and Lasso, comparing theoretical predictions against numerical simulations across different ratios $r$.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by first establishing the general framework using the replica method to find $\Phi(s)$. It then applies this to OLS, Ridge, and Lasso, deriving the rate function $\psi(\xi)$ for each case, and finally confirming consistency across different theoretical approaches and limits (e.g., $\lambda 	o 0$).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Linear regression is one of the simplest and most widely used tools to learn patterns from data: it fits a set of coefficients so that a linear combination of predictors best matches observed responses. The quality of the fit is measured by the residual sum of squares, the total squared mismatch between predictions and data, whose minimum defines the training loss. We consider Gaussian design and noise, with teacher coefficients independently drawn from a general distribution $p(β)$, and a general class of separable regularizers, including Ridge and Lasso. Using the zero-temperature replica method, we compute analytically the large-deviation statistics of the minimum training loss for large numbers $P$ of predictors and $N$ of observations, with $r=P/N$ fixed. The rate function we compute governs rare sample-to-sample fluctuations of the optimal loss. Extensive numerical simulations are in excellent agreement with our theory and clearly show a pronounced deviation from the Gaussian regime of typical fluctuations in the tails.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work investigates the competition between electron crystal (Wigner) and liquid (Laughlin) states in moiré materials characterized by non-uniform quantum geometry. By comparing the energies of both phases, the authors demonstrate that geometric non-uniformity drives the system towards crystallization. The stability boundary is governed by a quantum analogue of the Lindemann criterion related to the crystal's zero-point fluctuations.

**Why it may be interesting.** The identification of quantum geometry-controlled zero-point fluctuations as the stability determinant provides a novel, purely quantum-geometric mechanism for phase transitions in strongly correlated electron systems, which is highly relevant to understanding moiré heterostructures.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the open question of the mechanism selecting between competing correlated states—Fractional Chern Insulator (FCI) states and generalized Wigner crystals—in systems subjected to non-uniform quantum geometry.

**Main result.** The study finds that strong non-uniform quantum geometry favors electron crystallization, establishing a quantum analogue of the Lindemann criterion where the crystal's zero-point fluctuations control the phase boundary.

**Method.** The authors compare the calculated energies of the Wigner crystal state and the Laughlin liquid state by minimizing the orbital spread and using perturbative expansions and Monte Carlo simulations.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using Aharonov-Casher (AC) bands, which approximate the topmost band in Moiré materials hosting Chern bands. The Hamiltonian incorporates a spatially varying magnetic field, $B(r) = B_0 + \delta B(r)$.

**Key observables.** Crystalline energy ($E_{AC}^C$), Laughlin state energy ($E_{AC}^L$), and the orbital spread ($\langle r^2 angle_c$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Filling fraction $
u = 1/m$ and the degree of geometric non-uniformity, parameterized by $B_1$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on variational ansatz wave functions and approximations such as neglecting terms involving three or more crystal sites in the energy calculation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the flat AC band and localized orbitals depending on the sign of $B_1$. They also show phase diagrams comparing crystalline and liquid energies across varying $B_1$ values for different fillings.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the AC band model, calculating the energies for both the Laughlin and Wigner crystal states using various approximations (perturbative, variational), and finally mapping out the phase diagram based on the competition between these two energy functionals.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Moiré materials host fractional Chern insulators and electron crystals in close proximity, but the mechanism selecting between them remains an open question. We address this competition in Chern bands with ideal but momentum-dependent quantum geometry -- Aharonov-Casher bands. We present an ansatz wave function for generalized Wigner crystals and, by comparing its energy to that of the competing Laughlin-like state, map out the phase diagram at filling fraction $ν=1/m$ as a function of the degree of geometric non-uniformity. Our work identifies quantum geometry-controlled zero point fluctuations of the charge density of the generalized Wigner crystal as the mechanism controlling its relative stability, implying a kind of quantum Lindemann criterion for the crystal-liquid phase boundary.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper simulates the motion of a rod falling through randomly placed obstacles, revealing that its average speed does not simply decrease with obstacle density. Instead, the speed shows a nonmonotonic behavior—speeding up in an intermediate density regime—which is attributed to the interplay between particle anisotropy and rotational memory effects. The findings provide scaling laws describing this complex, driven transport phenomenon.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study of non-equilibrium transport in anisotropic particles interacting with disordered media touches upon concepts relevant to driven many-body systems and energy dissipation in complex environments.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how particle shape (anisotropy) influences transport dynamics when a rod falls through a field of randomly placed obstacles under external driving.

**Main result.** The mean descent speed exhibits a nonmonotonic dependence on obstacle density, showing an initial decrease, a subsequent increase, and a final decrease before trapping.

**Method.** The study uses numerical simulations to solve the translational and rotational equations of motion for a dissipative rod interacting with fixed obstacles.

**Model / system.** The model consists of a dissipative rod (spherocylinder) falling under gravity through a 2D random array of fixed point obstacles, governed by contact forces and dissipation.

**Key observables.** Mean descent speed (mean vertical velocity), rotational relaxation time, and mean trapping distance.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Obstacle density ($ho$), rod aspect ratio ($l/d$), and the friction coefficient ($\mu$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on scaling arguments derived from collision rates and assumes specific physical mechanisms govern the transitions between different transport regimes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the nonmonotonic variation of mean fall speed versus density for long rods, and show the dependence of rotational relaxation time on density.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical model and equations of motion, presents simulations showing the nonmonotonic drift, derives scaling laws for the three density regimes (low, intermediate, high), and finally tests the robustness of the speedup by including contact friction.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

How particle shape affects transport through obstacle fields under external driving is a fundamental question in nonequilibrium physics. We simulate a dissipative rod falling under gravity through randomly placed fixed obstacles. As the obstacle density increases, the mean descent speed decreases, increases, and then decreases again before trapping. The rod can therefore fall faster in a denser obstacle field. Scaling arguments based on collision rates and rod geometry explain all three regimes, their crossovers, and the mean fall distance before trapping. These results reveal nonmonotonic driven transport arising from particle anisotropy.

</details>

**Summary.** This work establishes a superconducting Peierls instability, drawing an analogy between the metal-insulator transition and the formation of an edge Pair-Density Wave (PDW) in superconductors. By analyzing the instability of Andreev Bound States (ABS) at the boundary of a 2D superconductor, the authors demonstrate a novel mechanism for spontaneous symmetry breaking. This mechanism is computationally verified using the extended Hubbard model and suggests a broad applicability beyond specialized topological systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The mechanism provides a general framework for understanding symmetry breaking in superconductors that does not require exotic topological features, making it relevant to many unconventional pairing theories.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the superconducting analog of the Peierls instability, showing how an electronic instability at the edge of a superconductor can induce a gap-opening Pair-Density Wave (PDW).

**Main result.** The instability leads to the spontaneous formation of an edge PDW, which gaps the dispersing Andreev Bound States (ABS) at the boundary, representing a novel mechanism for translation-symmetry breaking in superconductors.

**Method.** The analysis uses self-consistent Bogoliubov-de Gennes (BdG) calculations and examines the instability of the edge state against pairing fluctuations.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using a square-lattice extended Hubbard model, focusing on a mixed-symmetry s+d+ip superconducting state on a ribbon geometry.

**Key observables.** Superconducting Kohn anomaly (softening of a pairing mode), edge PDW gap ($\Delta_{	ext{PDW}}$), and the gapping of the ABS spectrum.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The instability is driven by the zero-energy crossings of a *dispersing* ABS, which is key to its broad applicability.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on mean-field theory and focuses on the boundary realization, which is distinct from previous flat-band or topologically protected examples.

**Figures summary.** Figures schematically compare the conventional Peierls instability (CDW) with the superconducting analog (edge PDW). BdG calculations confirm the self-consistent development of the edge PDW structure.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the analogy to the Peierls instability, then develops the superconducting counterpart by analyzing pairing fluctuations and the superconducting Kohn anomaly. Finally, it uses BdG calculations on the extended Hubbard model to confirm the gap-opening PDW structure.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Peierls instability is a foundational mechanism in condensed matter physics, showing how the electron--phonon interaction can transform a simple metal into an insulating state with a new lattice periodicity. In a one-dimensional (1D) metal the Peierls instability follows from the enhanced electronic response at wavevector $Q=2k_F$ (connecting the Fermi points) producing a Kohn anomaly: a softening of the phonon mode at the same wavevector. Condensation of this mode then generates the tell-tale Peierls periodic lattice distortion and charge-density wave (CDW) that gaps the electronic spectrum. Here we show an analogous instability at the edge of a two-dimensional (2D) superconductor, where a dispersing Andreev bound state (ABS) hosts Bogoliubov Fermi points at $\pm k_c$. The enhanced quasiparticle response at the connecting wavevector $Q=2k_c$ couples directly to pairing-fluctuations and produces a superconducting Kohn anomaly: a softening of a pairing mode at the same wavevector. Condensation of this mode then generates an edge pair-density wave (PDW) that gaps the ABS. We refer to this as a superconducting Peierls instability and identify the boundary quasiparticle structure that enables it. As a concrete realization, we consider a square-lattice extended Hubbard model whose mixed-symmetry $s+d+ip$ state hosts a dispersing ABS with zero-energy crossings at finite edge momenta. Using self-consistent Bogoliubov--de Gennes (BdG) calculations we show that the order parameter develops an edge PDW, with the wavevector set by the superconducting Kohn anomaly, that gaps the ABS crossings. Our results identify a novel mechanism for spontaneous translation-symmetry breaking in a superconductor. As this superconducting Peierls mechanism does not require an extensive zero-energy flat band or topologically protected edge states, it may apply broadly to unconventional superconductors.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports on the experimental observation of coupling between plasmons and antiferromagnetic order in a graphene/$	ext{NiPS}_3$ heterostructure. Using terahertz s-SNOM, the authors tracked plasmon dynamics and found that the plasmon dispersion renormalizes precisely when the underlying material enters an antiferromagnetic state. This establishes a novel platform for developing coherent spin-plasmon devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the coupling between electronic excitations (plasmons) and magnetic order (magnons) in a tunable 2D platform, which is a cornerstone problem in emergent magnetoplasmonics and open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the elusive interaction between plasmons (collective electron oscillations) and magnons (spin waves) in low-dimensional magnetic systems.

**Main result.** The researchers observed a clear renormalization of the plasmon-polariton dispersion coinciding with the onset of antiferromagnetic order, providing evidence of plasmon-magnon coupling.

**Method.** The work combines advanced experimental techniques like terahertz s-SNOM with theoretical modeling (DFT) to probe the coupled dynamics.

**Model / system.** The platform is a hybrid system consisting of a graphene monolayer placed on top of a $	ext{NiPS}_3$ microcrystal, which acts as a 2D antiferromagnet.

**Key observables.** Plasmon-polariton dispersion, plasmon wavepacket dynamics, spectral weight redistribution, and the renormalization of the plasmon-polariton velocity across the antiferromagnetic transition temperature ($T_N$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The system operates in the 2D limit, utilizing terahertz radiation, and the key transition is the antiferromagnetic ordering temperature ($T_N \approx 155 	ext{ K}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis initially rules out direct magnon-plasmon hybridization based on the observed increase in plasmon velocity, suggesting dielectric screening changes are the dominant mechanism.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the device structure, the comparison of excitation energy scales, and spacetime maps showing the measured plasmon-polariton velocity increasing across the antiferromagnetic transition temperature.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical motivation for plasmon-magnon coupling, details the $	ext{Graphene}/	ext{NiPS}_3$ platform, presents s-SNOM measurements tracking plasmon dynamics across $T_N$, and interprets these results via spectral analysis.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The interaction between plasmons and magnons is a long-sought phenomenon with implications for fundamental physics and spintronics applications. In three-dimensional systems, this coupling is suppressed by the large mismatch in energy scales, but two-dimensional (2D) plasmons with gapless dispersion can overlap with magnons over a broad spectral range. Despite numerous theoretical predictions, experimental observation of magnon-plasmon interaction has remained elusive. In this work, we study a first-of-its-kind hybrid plasmon-magnon platform based on 2D materials. By deploying scattering-type scanning near-field optical microscopy (s-SNOM) with terahertz radiation, we image propagating plasmon wavepackets at a graphene/NiPS$_3$ interface and track their dynamics across the antiferromagnetic transition of NiPS$_3$. We observe a clear renormalization of the plasmon-polariton dispersion concurrent with the onset of antiferromagnetic order. With complementary Raman scattering and nano-terahertz spectroscopy, we unveil spectral weight redistribution and dielectric screening changes, potentially associated with the multi-magnon continuum, as the underlying mechanism. These results provide solid evidence of coupling between plasmon and antiferromagnetic order, marking a cornerstone for a potential platform for hybrid magnon-plasmon interactions in 2D materials, opening avenues for coherent spin-plasmon devices and tunable terahertz spintronic components.

</details>

**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.03541v1>

**Summary.** This paper introduces BU-MBAR, a novel, highly efficient algorithm for solving the Multi-state Bennett Acceptance Ratio (MBAR) equations used in enhanced sampling simulations. It achieves this by treating the problem as a limit of the Weighted Histogram Analysis Method (WHAM) with dynamically adjusted binning. The method is shown to be significantly faster and more robust than existing solvers, making it a powerful tool for analyzing complex molecular simulation data.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical simulation statistics, the core concept of adaptive resolution and convergence acceleration through systematic approximation (binned-to-unbinned) has parallels in developing scalable quantum simulation techniques or analyzing complex quantum data sets.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a robust and fast solution strategy for the Multi-state Bennett Acceptance Ratio (MBAR) equations, which are used for statistically optimal weighted analysis of simulation data from multiple thermodynamic conditions.

**Main result.** The proposed BU-MBAR method significantly outperforms existing solvers like PYMBAR in terms of wall-clock time and scaling, while maintaining convergence to the true unbinned limit.

**Method.** The BU-MBAR method interprets the MBAR equations as the limit of the Weighted Histogram Analysis Method (WHAM) with dynamically adapted bin width, iteratively refining the solution from a binned approximation to the unbinned limit.

**Model / system.** The method is generally applicable to enhanced sampling data analysis involving multiple trajectories at different thermodynamic conditions ($eta_i$). A specific test case used is the alanine dipeptide simulated using the amber14SB force field.

**Key observables.** Free-energy estimates ($f_i$), Mean-Absolute Error (MAE) of relative free energies, and wall-clock time scaling ($	au$) vs. number of replicas ($M$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The algorithm uses fixed hyperparameters like initial bin count ($n_0=100$), convergence tolerance ($\epsilon=10^{-3}$), and an adaptive heuristic criterion.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that the MBAR equations can be accurately approached by taking the limit of the WHAM equations as the bin width approaches zero, guided by a dynamic bin adaptation heuristic.

**Figures summary.** Figures show the estimated free-energy profile of the alanine dipeptide, the convergence comparison (MAE vs. iterations/time) between BU-MBAR and PYMBAR, and the superior scaling of BU-MBAR versus PYMBAR as the number of replicas increases.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the scientific problem (MBAR solution) and the limitations of current methods. It then details the BU-MBAR approach, which involves iteratively refining the solution by adapting the bin width based on a heuristic criterion. Finally, it validates the method by applying it to a physical system (alanine dipeptide) and comparing its performance and scaling against established solvers.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The multi-state Bennett acceptance ratio (MBAR) equations combine the data collected under different thermodynamic conditions in a statistically optimal way. Due to their practical importance, several solution strategies have been devised to optimize the convergence of the resulting set of coupled equations. However, even with graphics processing unit (GPU) acceleration, the convergence of these methods can still be either unstable or slow. We propose a new approach where the equations are interpreted as the limit of infinitesimal bin width of the respective weighted histogram analysis method (WHAM). In the proposed binned-to-unbinned MBAR (BU-MBAR) method, the bin width is adapted dynamically to ensure a stable and efficient convergence to the asymptotic solution.

</details>
