**Summary.** This theoretical work develops a comprehensive framework linking the topology of the quasiparticle spectrum (Fermi-point topology) to the emergent conformal field theory describing quantum criticality in extended spin chains. It demonstrates that the central charge is determined by the independent low-energy continuum sectors, not just the number of gap closings. This provides deep insight into how interactions and fields drive unconventional critical phases.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a detailed, quantitative framework for understanding how topological features in momentum space dictate universal low-energy physics (CFTs), which is crucial for understanding quantum critical points in strongly interacting quantum materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to establish a microscopic link between the momentum-space topology of quasiparticle excitations and the universal conformal structure (CFT) characterizing quantum criticality in 1D quantum spin chains.

**Main result.** The emergent central charge ($c_{	ext{eff}}$) is determined not by a simple count of gapless Fermi points, but by the number and conformal content of independent low-energy continuum sectors after accounting for lattice symmetries.

**Method.** The authors use Jordan-Wigner transformations to map the spin Hamiltonian to a fermionic Bogoliubov problem, followed by continuum field theory analysis and the examination of entanglement spectra.

**Model / system.** The system is an extended quantum spin chain incorporating nearest-neighbor interactions, four-spin cluster interactions, exchange anisotropy, and a transverse magnetic field. The Hamiltonian is complex, involving multiple coupling constants.

**Key observables.** Effective central charge ($c_{	ext{eff}}$), Fermi-point topology, and entanglement spectra (real- and momentum-space).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling constants ($\alpha, eta$), transverse magnetic field ($h$), and the critical points defined by the collapse of the Bogoliubov spectrum.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the model being solvable via the Jordan-Wigner transformation and that the low-energy physics can be decomposed into independent continuum sectors.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the phase diagram showing critical boundaries in parameter space and show the evolution of the quasiparticle spectrum across Lifshitz transitions.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining the complex spin model, analyzing the quasiparticle spectrum and its topology across various parameter regimes (zero-field, anisotropic, field-driven), and finally using CFT and entanglement spectra to derive the effective central charge at critical points.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum criticality in one-dimensional quantum systems is characterized by emergent conformal field theories (CFTs), whose central charge counts independent gapless degrees of freedom. Establishing a microscopic connection between this universal conformal structure and the momentum-space topology of the underlying quasiparticle spectrum remains challenging. Here, we uncover a direct correspondence between Fermi-point topology, conformal criticality, and quantum entanglement in an extended quantum spin chain with competing cluster interactions, exchange anisotropy, and a transverse magnetic field. We show that interaction- and field-driven Lifshitz transitions generate conformal critical phases with effective central charges $c_{\rm eff}=1/2$, $1$, $3/2$, $2$, and $3$, including a multicritical point where Ising and Luttinger-liquid sectors coexist. Importantly, the central charge is not determined simply by the number of lattice gap closings or Fermi points, but by the number and conformal content of independent low-energy continuum sectors after accounting for lattice symmetries, reciprocal-lattice identifications, and mode equivalences. Thus, Lifshitz transitions may leave the conformal anomaly unchanged or modify the central charge depending on whether spectral reconstruction generates new independent continuum sectors. Real- and momentum-space entanglement spectra provide complementary microscopic signatures, revealing both the conformal content and momentum-space organization of critical modes. Our results establish a microscopic framework linking Fermi-point topology to emergent CFTs and show how interaction-driven spectral reconstruction can generate higher-central-charge criticality and unconventional multicritical behavior.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops NS-Flows, a generative model approach, to overcome the computational bottleneck of sampling rugged, high-dimensional energy landscapes characteristic of atomistic systems. By replacing slow MCMC steps with direct flow draws, the method drastically speeds up thermodynamic calculations, providing a powerful tool for statistical physics simulations.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical statistical mechanics, the development of generative models (Normalizing Flows) to sample complex, high-dimensional energy landscapes is a powerful tool applicable to quantum many-body systems where free energy estimation or phase space sampling is crucial.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is efficiently resolving the full thermodynamics of complex atomistic systems from a single simulation, which is hindered by the slow Markov-chain updates required in standard Nested Sampling (NS) methods.

**Main result.** The authors introduce NS-Flows, a conditional normalizing flow that significantly accelerates the process for atomistic systems, achieving massive reductions in energy evaluations and wall-clock time.

**Method.** They replace MCMC sampling within NS with a single conditional normalizing flow, trained on self-consistent 'live sets' of data, allowing for direct parallel draws corrected by importance-weighted rejection resampling.

**Model / system.** The comparison is drawn between a high-dimensional Gravitational Wave (GW) posterior (smooth degeneracies) and an eight-particle 2D Lennard-Jones (LJ) system (discrete, combinatorial multimodality).

**Key observables.** Energy landscape structure, generation efficiency of the flow, and the comparison of coupling structures (sparse vs. dense).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The LJ potential parameters, the NS energy bound, and the dimensionality of the system (e.g., 2D for LJ).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The flow training assumes sufficient overlap between the true conditional distribution and the model distribution for accurate reweighting, and it is designed to handle the combinatorial nature of atomistic barriers.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 contrasts the smooth, continuous degeneracy ridges of the GW posterior with the discrete, hard-walled multimodality of the LJ energy landscape across various diagnostic cuts.

**Paper structure.** The paper diagnoses the fundamental topological difference between smooth GW posteriors and rugged, combinatorial atomistic landscapes, leading to the proposal and testing of NS-Flows to overcome the MCMC bottleneck in the latter.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Nested sampling (NS) resolves the thermodynamics of an atomistic system from a single simulation, but its practical reach is limited by the Markov-chain updates needed to decorrelate walkers within each likelihood-constrained ensemble. Flow-based NS has removed this bottleneck for gravitational-wave (GW) inference, yet its transfer to atomistic systems is not merely a change of application. Comparing a GW150914-like binary-black-hole likelihood with an eight-particle two-dimensional Lennard-Jones (LJ) system of comparable dimensionality, we show that the two landscapes differ fundamentally: atomistic multimodality is discrete and combinatorial, generated by particle permutations separated by hard collision walls, and its coordinate coupling is dense and collective, whereas the GW posterior exhibits smooth degeneracies and localized parameter coupling. Guided by this diagnosis, we introduce NS-Flows: a single conditional normalizing flow, conditioned on the NS energy bound and trained on a sliding window of recent live sets, that replaces MCMC by direct parallel draws corrected by importance-weighted rejection resampling. Live sets supply data self-consistently, allowing flow training without structured priors or a pre-existing dataset. For LJ disks in PBC, the algorithm reduces energy evaluations by over two orders of magnitude and wall-clock time by roughly one third, an advantage that becomes increasingly favorable as the cost of the potential grows. The flow's generation efficiency further acts as a physical diagnostic: it varies non-monotonically along the annealing trajectory, is lowest in the dense disordered regime, and is quantitatively captured by the constrained ensemble's internal mode complexity together with target drift across the training window, identifying liquid-like ensembles, rather than prior-target separation, as the hard case for current flow architectures.

</details>

**Summary.** This work quantifies the quantum complexity of nuclear processes, such as scattering and fission, by tracking entanglement and non-local magic resources over time. By showing that these complexity measures remain high in the outgoing fragments, the authors argue that simulating these dynamics accurately requires quantum computation. This establishes a theoretical case for quantum advantage in nuclear structure physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** It bridges the gap between abstract quantum information theory (complexity measures) and concrete, complex physical systems (nuclear reactions), providing a theoretical benchmark for quantum simulation feasibility in nuclear physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the quantum complexity structure of strongly correlated quantum many-body systems to determine if they are suitable candidates for quantum computational advantage.

**Main result.** The analysis of nuclear scattering and fission dynamics shows that significant quantum complexity, quantified by entanglement and non-local magic, persists in the outgoing states, suggesting quantum computers would offer substantial advantages.

**Method.** The study analyzes the time-evolution of quantum complexity indicators, specifically bipartite entanglement entropy and non-local magic, using theoretical frameworks like the stabilizer formalism and Gaussian state approximations.

**Model / system.** The models include a one-dimensional system of strongly interacting fermions governed by the Negele potential for scattering, and a realistic simulation of $^{240}	ext{Pu}$ fission using time-dependent Hartree-Fock-Bogoliubov (TDHFB) theory.

**Key observables.** Bipartite entanglement entropy, non-local magic (non-stabilizerness), and the Anti-flatness ($	ext{F}_A$) measure.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The complexity measures are sensitive to the dynamics, particularly in the region beyond scission during fission.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis uses Gaussian states (FGS) as a tractable proxy for the full dynamical evolution, despite FGSs being classically simulable.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by defining and analyzing quantum complexity measures (entanglement, non-local magic) and applying these tools to two distinct physical scenarios: 1D fermion scattering and $^{240}	ext{Pu}$ fission dynamics.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum computers promise advantages for simulating strongly correlated quantum many-body systems, like atomic nuclei, that are beyond the reach of classical computers. Realizing this potential requires understanding the quantum complexity structure of the target problem. We investigate the time-evolution of two key indicators of quantum complexity, bipartite entanglement entropy and non-local magic (non-stabilizerness), in nuclear reaction dynamics. We analyze two representative dynamical processes: scattering in a one-dimensional model of strongly interacting fermions governed by the Negele potential, and a realistic simulation of $^{240}$Pu fission within time-dependent Hartree-Fock-Bogoliubov (TDHFB) theory. In the former case, we find that interactions dynamically generate both entanglement and non-local magic, leaving persistent signatures of quantum complexity in the outgoing states. In the latter, we observe that substantial quantum complexity survives in the spatial bipartition of daughter fragments well beyond scission. The presence of significant non-local magic and entanglement in both cases strongly indicate that quantum computers would provide substantial advantages for accurately simulating nuclear reaction dynamics.

</details>

**Summary.** The researchers used electron paramagnetic resonance on $	ext{Tb}^{3+}$ ions to study quantum coherence, finding that conventional models failed to explain the measured coherence time ($T_2$). They successfully incorporated dynamical angular fluctuations of the crystal axis into their model, demonstrating that ambient vibrations are a dominant, quantifiable decoherence mechanism for anisotropic solid-state spins.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a direct, measurable link between solid-state quantum coherence properties and macroscopic environmental noise (vibrations), offering a novel, highly sensitive quantum sensor platform.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To utilize the spin transitions of strongly anisotropic rare-earth ions as a highly sensitive quantum coherence probe for measuring ambient angular fluctuations.

**Main result.** The dominant decoherence pathway is found to be due to dynamical angular fluctuations of the crystal axis, which can be quantified and modeled by extending standard spin-bath theories.

**Method.** Pulsed electron paramagnetic resonance (EPR) measurements are used to map the Hahn-echo coherence time ($T_2$) as a function of temperature and magnetic field, comparing results against detailed theoretical models.

**Model / system.** The system involves $	ext{Tb}^{3+}$ ions in a $	ext{CaWO}_4$ crystal, which possesses strong magnetic anisotropy ($g_{\perp}=0$). The analysis models the spin Hamiltonian and incorporates contributions from spin-lattice relaxation, impurity diffusion, and crucially, angular jitter.

**Key observables.** Hahn-echo coherence time ($T_2$), $	ext{EPR}$ linewidth ($\Gamma_{	ext{EFS}}$), and the temperature/field dependence of $T_2$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature range (2 to 10 K), resonant field ($10^3$ to $10^4$ G), and the angular noise spectral density ($\sim 36 	ext{ n}\circ/\sqrt{	ext{Hz}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The initial model failed because it neglected the dynamical angular fluctuations of the crystal axis, which was confirmed by controlled mechanical experiments.

**Figures summary.** Figures show $	ext{EPR}$ spectra illustrating field-dependent transitions, linewidth measurements ($\Gamma_{	ext{EFS}}$) showing angular divergence, and $T_2$ measurements demonstrating deviations from simple spin-bath models.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical sensitivity of the spin system to orientation, presents initial modeling failures, and culminates in a successful two-parameter extension that incorporates dynamical angular fluctuations to reproduce all experimental data.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Strongly anisotropic non-Kramers rare-earth ions combine giant longitudinal g-factors with a vanishing transverse component imposed by time-reversal symmetry, a combination that makes their spin transitions exquisitely sensitive to the orientation of the applied magnetic field. We show that this sensitivity carries a dual identity: it is simultaneously an overlooked decoherence channel and the basis for a spin-coherence-based angular probe. Using pulsed electron paramagnetic resonance at X-band, we report the first measurements of the quantum coherence of Tb$^{3+}$ in a native-doped CaWO$_4$ crystal (15 ppb) and map the Hahn-echo coherence time $T_2$ as a function of temperature (2 to 10 K) and resonant field ($10^3$ to $10^4$ G). A parameter-free model combining spin-lattice relaxation, instantaneous diffusion and spectral diffusion from all independently quantified impurities overestimates $T_2$ by an order of magnitude at low temperature and wrongly predicts the field dependence of $T_2$, inconsistent with the observed monotonic decrease of $T_2$ with $B_r$. A two-parameter extension, including dynamical angular fluctuations of the crystal axis, reproduces the full dataset across multiple setups and laboratories. Two controlled experiments nominally identical except for different mechanical configuration of the setup establish the mechanical origin of the dominant contribution. The two-parameter extension corresponds to an angular amplitude noise spectral density of overall order 36 n°/$\sqrt{Hz}$ from global external vibrations (ranging from 10 to 66 n°/$\sqrt{Hz}$ depending on the exact setup mechanical configuration) estimated at $\sim$ 2.5 kHz plus a temperature-dependent contribution assumed to come from local phonon-driven angular jitter. It identifies and highlights a decoherence pathway of practical relevance to any anisotropic solid-state spin system.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a comprehensive wavefunction framework to describe anyon superconductivity, moving beyond conventional BCS theory. By constructing hierarchy wavefunctions derived from topological orders, the authors successfully diagnose superconducting order and topological properties for systems like the $\nu=2/3$ state. This establishes a unified theoretical link between anyon condensation, superconducting pairing, and topological field theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous wavefunction approach provides a powerful, concrete tool to analyze topological superconductivity, bridging abstract CFT concepts with measurable physical diagnostics like ODLRO.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to develop a systematic wavefunction description for anyon superconductors, as a general framework is currently lacking despite existing field-theoretic descriptions.

**Main result.** A unified wavefunction-level framework is established, successfully linking anyon condensation, superconducting order, and topological field theory for various fractional quantum Hall states.

**Method.** The authors develop a 'hierarchy-wavefunction construction' using the plasma analogy and topological field theories to analyze superconducting states derived from parent topological orders.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on anyon superconductors, where superconductivity arises from mobile anyon condensation rather than conventional Cooper pairing. Specific models include the $\nu=2/3$ state, Laughlin states ($\nu=1/3$), and the Pfaffian state.

**Key observables.** Off-Diagonal Long-Range Order (ODLRO), condensate charge, chiral central charge, and residual topological order.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis examines the long-distance limit of the anyon-Hilbert-space formulation, which applies to ideal Chern bands and moiré bands.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The formalism relies on the correspondence between quantum Hall hierarchy states and anyon superconductivity, and the plasma analogy requires the exponent matrix to be positive semidefinite.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the general hierarchy wavefunction construction, then applying it to specific fractional quantum Hall states (semions, $\nu=2/3$, $\nu=1/3$), and finally using the reduced density matrix and ODLRO operator to confirm the superconducting nature across all cases.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Anyon superconductivity arises from the condensation of mobile anyons rather than from a conventional Cooper instability, yet a systematic wavefunction description remains lacking. We develop a hierarchy-wavefunction construction for superconducting states derived from parent topological orders and identify their off-diagonal long-range order, condensate charge, chiral central charge, and residual topological order through the plasma analogy and topological field theories. We construct Abelian and non-Abelian examples descending from the semion state, a $ν=2/3$ hierarchy state, the $ν=1/3$ Laughlin state, $ν=1$ integer quantum Hall state, and Pfaffian state. Remarkably, for the semion case, the superconducting many-semion wavefunction is equivalent to a state of fermionized anyons filling two effective Landau levels, recovering Laughlin's original construction of semion superconductor. Finally, we show that hierarchy wavefunctions emerge naturally in the dilute, long-distance limit of the anyon-Hilbert-space formulation, which applies to ideal Chern bands and moiré bands of twisted bilayer MoTe$_2$. Our results establish a unified wavefunction-level framework linking anyon condensation, superconducting order, and topological field theory.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Algebraic Operator Decomposition (AOD), a method to break down complex quantum operations into many smaller, shallow, and independent sub-circuits. By processing these fragments in parallel and summing the results classically, it proactively mitigates noise accumulation by keeping the circuit depth below hardware coherence limits. This shifts the computational bottleneck from deep quantum coherence to manageable classical sampling overhead.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a novel, mathematically rigorous framework for structuring quantum computations that directly addresses the depth limitations inherent in NISQ devices, which is highly relevant to open quantum systems modeling.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is mitigating noise and decoherence in Noisy Intermediate-Scale Quantum (NISQ) computing by avoiding error correction entirely.

**Main result.** The proposed Algebraic Operator Decomposition (AOD) successfully reduces the required quantum circuit depth by fracturing global operators into shallow, independent sub-channels.

**Method.** AOD decomposes a global linear operator into a sum of local operators, which are then processed in parallel using a MapReduce model based on a commutative monoid.

**Model / system.** The framework is built upon operators belonging to a Vector Space over Additive Abelian Groups, applicable to complex quantum operations like inner products, convolutions, and differential operators.

**Key observables.** The reconstructed global observable ($\langle T angle$), the required shot budget ($S_{	ext{total}}$), and the error bound ($\Delta T$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Noise parameters ($\epsilon_{1Q}, \epsilon_{2Q}$), hardware-dependent coherence threshold ($D_{	ext{max}}$), and the number of partitions ($K$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach assumes independent local noise channels and trades reduced quantum depth for increased classical reconstruction and sampling overhead.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the quantum circuits for specific scenarios, such as the Hadamard Test for inner products and the evolution across discrete operational slices for differential operators.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces AOD, details its application to various global operations (e.g., dot products, convolutions), derives theoretical bounds on error containment and sampling cost, and concludes with optimization strategies.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present an operator-decomposition architecture that mathematically maps a global operator into independently executable local operators, reducing the maximum quantum circuit depth at the cost of classical reconstruction and sampling overhead. By framing complex Quantum Circuits around an operator in a vector space that can be algebraically pre-decomposed, AOD complements quantum error correction and error-mitigation approaches by performing algebraic decomposition before quantum execution.   Our approach leans in the computer science definition of a Monoid: a design pattern and mathematical concept consisting of a data type, a combining function that is associative, and a safe identity (neutral) element that does not change other values when combined. Simulation wise we define a MapReduce programming model where the addition (+) is the reducer, thus leveraging a naturally stable commutative monoid which carries zero "negative-probability tax" or phase conflicts.   Furthermore, we define a Vector Space of Linear Operators over Additive Abelian Groups that benefit from this paradigm, including: Inner Products, Series expansions, Traces and Convolutions. Finally, we present the mathematical foundations and simulation results for this paradigm.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper derives universal, rigorous lower bounds on the anomalous dimensions characterizing sharp corners (cusps) of line defects in a CFT. By applying bootstrap consistency checks across various defect geometries, the authors establish fundamental constraints on the underlying quantum field theory structure. This work provides powerful mathematical tools for constraining physical theories based on symmetry and consistency.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on CFT, the techniques involving universal bounds derived from consistency conditions (bootstrap) are highly relevant to understanding critical points and strongly correlated systems where such constraints often appear.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to establish general, universal bounds on the cusp anomalous dimensions ($\Gamma$) associated with line defects in a Conformal Field Theory (CFT). These bounds are derived from fundamental principles like unitarity and the consistency of defect geometries.

**Main result.** The authors prove that singlet cusps are the lightest possible cusps and derive an optimal, universal lower bound for the dimension of a right-angle bare cusp using bootstrap constraints.

**Method.** The analysis employs advanced techniques including the Operator State Correspondence (OSC), the Cusp Operator Expansion (COE), and applying bootstrap constraints derived from varying defect geometries (e.g., rectangular or wedge shapes).

**Model / system.** The study is set in the context of conformal line defects within a Conformal Field Theory (CFT). The analysis uses concepts from radial and flat quantization to model the dynamics of these defects.

**Key observables.** Cusp anomalous dimensions ($\Gamma$), the universal defect Casimir energy ($\epsilon_{aar{a}}$), and the bare cusp dimension ($\Gamma_{aar{a}}^0(	heta)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The cusp angle ($	heta$), the separation distance ($L$), and the geometry of the defect configuration (e.g., right angle, rectangular).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory is assumed to be local, unitary, and possess a complete defect Hilbert space. The derived bounds are claimed to be valid in any scale-invariant theory.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates cusped defect changing operators; Figure 2 shows defect configurations on a sphere punctured at right angles; Figure 3 illustrates the wedge-shaped defect used in the proof of one of the key inequalities.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds by first establishing foundational results regarding singlet cusps and then deriving increasingly general bounds by applying consistency checks (bootstrap constraints) to increasingly complex defect geometries (e.g., rectangular, cuboid).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

An illuminating probe of the dynamics of a line defect is the global geometry of its worldline. For a conformal line defect in a conformal field theory, sharp corners in the worldline, i.e. cusps, host dynamical degrees of freedom characterized in part by a spectrum of scaling dimensions, called cusp anomalous dimensions. We present various general bounds on cusp anomalous dimensions following from unitarity and cutting-and-gluing consistency of different defect geometries. We first establish, for cusps involving conjugate defects, that level crossings upon varying the cusp angle are forbidden between the lightest singlet cusp and any non-singlet cusp, proving that singlet cusps are the lightest. Then, we study line defects arranged in a rectangular geometry, which are subject to bootstrap constraints reminiscent of the spinless modular bootstrap. We find an analytic ``magic" functional that produces an optimal and universal lower bound on the dimension of a right angle bare cusp in terms of the universal defect Casimir energy between the corresponding defect and its conjugate in flat space.

</details>

**Summary.** This work addresses the information bottleneck in QML by introducing Discretization-Aware Fine-Tuning (DAFT). DAFT modifies the representation learning process to minimize data collisions during quantization, which is crucial for near-term quantum devices. The results prove that achieving quantum advantage requires explicitly aligning continuous chemical embeddings with the discrete constraints of quantum encoding.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on ML, the core concept of information compression and the necessity of preserving continuous information structure when mapping to discrete quantum states is highly relevant to understanding quantum channel capacity and encoding schemes in quantum information theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge in Quantum Machine Learning (QML) is the information bottleneck where high-dimensional classical data must be compressed into limited quantum registers, leading to cross-class collisions.

**Main result.** The proposed Discretization-Aware Fine-Tuning (DAFT) method significantly reduces these collisions, enabling the quantum model to surpass a matched classical baseline at higher qubit counts.

**Method.** DAFT adapts a pre-trained chemical foundation model using a differentiable soft collision loss to ensure that the resulting quantized representations remain highly informative.

**Model / system.** The system uses near-term quantum devices processing molecular representations derived from chemical foundation models (like ChemBERTa-77M) via bit-string encoding.

**Key observables.** Collision count, quantum classification accuracy, and the performance gap compared to a matched classical baseline.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Qubit count (tested up to 10 qubits), and the degree of information compression imposed by the bit-string encoding.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The comparison isolates the effect of representation by ensuring both quantum and classical models receive identical discretized bit-string inputs.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the pipeline comparing baseline vs. DAFT, and tables summarize the reversal of performance advantage from classical-wins to quantum-wins as qubit count increases.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of data compression in QML, details the DAFT methodology (including soft collision loss), evaluates it on a molecular property prediction benchmark, and concludes by demonstrating the conditions necessary for quantum advantage.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A key challenge in practical quantum machine learning (QML), particularly for discriminative tasks such as classification, is the limited capacity of near-term quantum devices to encode high-dimensional classical data into small quantum registers. In optimized basis-encoded (bit-bit) settings, this constraint leads to cross-class collisions, where samples with different labels are mapped to the same discrete bit-string and thus become indistinguishable to any downstream model. In this work, we investigate how data representation affects QML performance under such severe information bottlenecks. We introduce discretization-aware fine-tuning (DAFT), a method that adapts a pre-trained chemical foundation model to produce representations that remain informative after quantization. DAFT reduces collision probability through a differentiable soft collision loss. We evaluate both quantum and classical models under a controlled setting in which they receive identical discretized bit-string inputs, isolating the effect of representation from model architecture. On the blood-brain barrier penetration (BBBP) molecular property prediction benchmark using ChemBERTa-77M, DAFT reduces collision counts by several orders of magnitude and improves quantum classification accuracy by more than 12 percentage points compared to a frozen backbone. Importantly, without DAFT, classical models outperform QML under the same input constraints. With DAFT, however, this comparison reverses at higher qubit counts. At 10 qubits, the quantum model surpasses a matched classical baseline trained on identical bit-strings (0.883 vs. 0.855, $p = 0.026$). These results show that, in information-constrained regimes, achieving a quantum advantage critically depends on aligning continuous representations with discrete quantum encodings.

</details>

<a id="paper-2609.03994"></a>

**Summary.** This work uses Electron Spin Resonance (ESR) spectroscopy to map the quantum critical phase diagram of the heavy-fermion metal YbRh2Si2. By tracking changes in the g-factor and linewidth, the authors distinguish the physics in paramagnetic, antiferromagnetic, and quantum-critical regimes. The results offer direct, microscopic evidence supporting theories of strongly correlated electron systems near a quantum critical point.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed mapping of magnetic excitations (via ESR) across a quantum critical point provides a powerful, dynamic probe of the underlying many-body interactions, which is crucial for understanding non-Fermi liquid behavior in strongly correlated materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how electron spin resonance (ESR) properties evolve across the quantum critical phase diagram of a heavy-fermion metal.

**Main result.** ESR measurements reveal distinct behaviors in the g-factor and linewidth across paramagnetic, antiferromagnetic, and quantum-critical regimes, providing microscopic insight into the underlying correlated electron physics.

**Method.** The research combines novel, low-temperature ESR spectroscopy with theoretical modeling using effective spin Hamiltonians and molecular field approximations.

**Model / system.** The system is the heavy-fermion metal YbRh2Si2, which exhibits a quantum phase transition between a paramagnetic Fermi-liquid (PFL) and an antiferromagnetic (AFL) state due to the interplay between local magnetic moments and conduction electrons.

**Key observables.** ESR g-factor, ESR linewidth (Delta B), and the resonance field, measured as functions of temperature and magnetic field.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Quantum critical point, Paramagnetic Fermi-Liquid (PFL) phase, Antiferromagnetic Liquid (AFL) phase, Kondo temperature scale ($T^*$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on approximating the system using an effective spin Hamiltonian and employing the molecular field approximation for theoretical modeling.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the overall phase diagram probed by ESR, show exemplary ESR spectra across different phases, and detail the temperature/field dependence of the effective g-factor, often comparing experimental fits to theoretical predictions.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical model and experimental setup using ESR. It then presents results showing distinct ESR signatures in the PFL, AFL, and quantum-critical regimes, followed by theoretical analysis comparing these findings to established models of correlated electron magnetism.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In the heavy-fermion metal YbRh2Si2, quantum criticality at a suppressed antiferromagnetic order is governed by the interplay of local magnetic moments and itinerant conduction electrons. We demonstrate how this can be investigated by a new experimental approach that enables the observation of electron spin resonance (ESR) across a broad range of frequencies and fields at very low temperatures. This allowed us to cover a large part of the phase diagram from the paramagnetic Fermi-liquid phase to the phase with antiferromagnetic order and including the quantum-critical regime. Both the ESR g-factor and the linewidth present distinct behaviors in these three regimes, providing further insight into the physics across a quantum critical point. Notably, when cooling down at a field directly towards the quantum critical point, both g-factor and linewidth continuously decrease. Furthermore, we observe a very good matching of the g-factor behavior upon field-tuning and temperature-tuning towards the quantum-critical point. We analyze and discuss the results in the context of present theories on ESR in strongly correlated electron systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a generalized s-d model to unify the description of magnetism, itinerant carriers, and excitons in layered magnetic semiconductors like $	ext{CrSBr}$. By analyzing the system's response across different magnetic field regimes, the authors predict specific excitonic states and provide a guiding principle based on conserved quantum numbers for understanding these complex magneto-optic effects.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit inclusion of magnetic order into the exciton physics via the s-d model provides a powerful theoretical tool for understanding magneto-optics, which bridges condensed matter theory with quantum optics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a unified, analytically tractable minimal model describing the interplay between magnetic order, itinerant electrons, and excitons in layered magnetic semiconductors.

**Main result.** The model successfully captures the coupling between magnetic order and excitonic phenomena, providing a framework to understand magneto-optic responses in these materials.

**Method.** The authors employ a generalized s-d model Hamiltonian, reducing it for magnetically ordered states and analyzing the resulting electronic structure and exciton binding energies.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on layered van der Waals magnetic semiconductors, using $	ext{CrSBr}$ bilayer as a case study. The model incorporates magnetic order, electron/hole dynamics, and excitons via an extended Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Bright and dark excitonic states, optical selection rules (conservation of a spin/layer pseudospin quantum number), and exciton binding energy.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnetic canting angle ($\alpha$), hopping amplitudes ($t_{	au x}, t_{	au y}, t_{	au \perp}$), and onsite exchange couplings.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The localized-spin operators are replaced by their expectation values for a magnetically ordered state, and the analysis often relies on a two-channel exciton model approximation.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically shows the reduced low-energy model for $	ext{CrSBr}$ bilayer. Figure 2 summarizes the field-driven evolution of magnetic order (AFM $	o$ Canted $	o$ FM). Figure 7(b) plots layer probabilities.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the unified Hamiltonian, then analyzing the electronic structure evolution under magnetic field canting using symmetry principles (SRLP), and finally calculating the resulting Wannier-Mott exciton properties and binding energies.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The recent discovery of excitons coupled to the magnetic order, and the consequent strong magneto-optic responses, in some van der Waals magnetic semiconductors has triggered intense activity at the interface of magnetism and semiconductor optics. Here, we present an analytically tractable minimal model that describes magnetic order, electrons, holes, and excitons within a unified framework, thereby capturing a wide range of phenomena. It treats the magnetic order and itinerant carriers to be comprised by distinct electronic orbitals that are mutually coupled via orbital-dependent onsite exchange, similar to the treatment of metallic magnets using an s-d model. Investigating CrSBr bilayer as a case study, we benchmark our model and its predictions against recent experimental and ab-initio results finding good agreement as well as new insights enabled by the model's simplicity. Examining the optical selection rules, we find the conservation of a quantum number formed from a combination of spin and layer pseudospin to be a useful guiding principle, even in noncollinear magnetic configurations. Our analysis finds a series of bright and dark excitonic states in such layered A-type antiferromagnets. The presented framework should be valuable in achieving intuitive understanding of recently discovered excitonic phenomena and guiding the discovery of other excitonic states in layered magnetic semiconductors.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper shows that Matrix Product States (MPS) can efficiently simulate complex Quantum Error Correction (QEC) circuits, even those containing non-Clifford gates. The key finding is that simulation efficiency hinges not on the circuit itself, but on sophisticated, circuit-level optimizations like reordering qubits and gates. These optimizations can reduce resource requirements by orders of magnitude, positioning MPS as a vital tool for fault-tolerant quantum simulation.

**Why it may be interesting.** The focus on optimizing tensor network representations for QEC circuits provides a powerful, resource-aware simulation tool that complements existing near-Clifford simulators, which is highly relevant for understanding resource scaling in quantum computation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is simulating large-scale Quantum Error Correction (QEC) circuits, especially those involving non-Clifford gates, to advance fault-tolerant quantum computing.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that Matrix Product State (MPS) techniques can simulate complex QEC circuits, including large magic-state distillation, efficiently by employing targeted circuit-level optimizations.

**Method.** The work utilizes MPS tensor network methods to represent and simulate quantum states and gates, focusing heavily on optimizing the sequence of qubits and gates to minimize the required bond dimension.

**Model / system.** The model involves simulating various QEC circuits, such as the rotated surface code and magic-state distillation, which are represented by stabilizer states and are analyzed using MPS formalism.

**Key observables.** Bond dimensions ($\chi_{\max}$), simulation time, and fidelity of the simulated logical states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Circuit distance ($d$), number of physical qubits (up to 187), and the specific ordering choices (e.g., 'northeast' vs 'naive').

**Assumptions / limitations.** The efficiency of MPS is critically dependent on finding the optimal qubit and gate ordering, which is computationally difficult.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the impact of ordering choices (e.g., 'rainbow' and 'mountain' problems), showing how reordering qubits or gates drastically reduces the maximal bond dimension required for simulation.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the necessity of simulating non-Clifford QEC circuits, then introducing MPS as the tool, and finally detailing specific optimizations (qubit/gate ordering) and benchmarking these optimizations on increasingly complex circuits (surface code, distillation).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Simulating quantum error correction (QEC) circuits including non-Clifford gates at scale is important to accelerate progress toward fault-tolerant quantum computing. Here we demonstrate that matrix product state (MPS) techniques can handle many QEC circuits exactly and without restriction on gate types. Crucially, we find that MPS efficiency depends sensitively on implementation choices, and we introduce a series of targeted optimizations that reduce bond dimensions and simulation time by several orders of magnitude compared to naive approaches. We illustrate this with examples including: (a) a rotated surface code quantum memory up to distance 11, (b) logical Bell-state preparation up to distance 9, (c) a 15-to-1 magic-state distillation circuit including hundreds of QEC rounds that we optimize to be simulated with only 11 logical qubits (187 physical qubits) and a maximal bond dimension of 64 in under 40 seconds, and (d) a narrow, deep random circuit that scales linearly with the number of T gates. These results demonstrate the importance of circuit-level optimizations and position MPS as a valuable complement to near-Clifford simulators for QEC circuits.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper uses parameterised graph theory to analyze the structural constraints governing Tensor Network States (TNSs). It proves that graph parameters like cutwidth bound the required bond dimensions for efficient representations (MPS/TTN). Crucially, it derives complexity bounds for state tomography, providing a theoretical roadmap for learning and characterizing complex quantum states.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous, graph-theoretic tools to quantify the computational cost associated with simulating or characterizing quantum states, which is crucial for understanding the limits of quantum simulation algorithms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to characterize the structural graph parameters that determine the computational tractability of representing quantum states as Tensor Networks (TNSs) and to bound the complexity of learning these states.

**Main result.** It establishes that graph parameters like cutwidth and tree-cutwidth bound the bond dimension overhead for MPS/TTN representations, and derives explicit graph-dependent complexity bounds for state tomography.

**Method.** The authors employ Parameterised Graph Theory, introducing the concept of 'entanglement rerouting' as a tensor-network analogue to analyze structural dependencies.

**Model / system.** The focus is on quantum states represented by Tensor Networks (TNSs) on a graph $G$, which encode the entanglement structure of the quantum system.

**Key observables.** Bond dimension, sample and computational complexity of tomography, and fidelity of state reconstruction.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Cutwidth ($	ext{cw}(G)$), Tree-cutwidth ($	ext{tcw}(G)$), and a new 'learning complexity' parameter.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The work extends its analysis to the 'agnostic setting,' allowing it to handle input states that might not perfectly belong to the restricted class of representable TNSs.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates classical information rerouting, while Figure 2 illustrates the tensor-network analogue, 'Entanglement rerouting,' showing how virtual indices can be rerouted through an intermediate vertex.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces entanglement rerouting and proves bounds relating cutwidth/tree-cutwidth to MPS/TTN bond dimensions. It then derives complexity bounds for realizable TNS tomography and finally extends the framework to agnostic tomography for arbitrary input states.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Parameterised graph theory studies how the complexity of graph-theoretic problems depends on structural parameters of the input graph. This perspective has proved useful in analysing tensor-network simulation (Markov and Shi, 2008). Its implications for tensor-network representations and tomography are less well understood. In particular, which graph parameters determine whether a tensor-network state (TNS) admits a tractable matrix product state (MPS) or tree tensor network (TTN) representation, and which control the complexity of learning the state?   We address these questions using parameterised graph theory. First, we show that cutwidth and tree-cutwidth bound the bond dimension overhead required to represent a TNS as an MPS or TTN. In the TTN case, tree-cutwidth also bounds the local dimension of the grouped subsystems. The proofs are based on entanglement rerouting, a tensor-network analogue of rerouting information in a classical network. Second, we derive graph-dependent upper bounds on the sample and computational complexity of realisable TNS tomography, with exponents that depend on cutwidth, tree-cutwidth, and a new graph parameter, learning complexity, which we bound in terms of degree and treewidth. We obtain these results by extending the disentangling MPS learner of (Cramer et al., 2010), as analysed further in (Bakshi et al., 2025; Lin et al., 2025), to TTNs and to tensor networks on arbitrary known graphs. Finally, we extend the framework beyond the realisable setting. For an arbitrary input state, our agnostic learner outputs a pure state whose fidelity is within additive error $ε$ of the optimum over tensor-network states on the given graph with a given bond dimension, with explicit graph-dependent bounds on sample and computational complexity.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper develops a quasi-local formulation for the $\alpha$-$z$ Rényi Quantum Null Energy Condition (QNEC) by employing fixed-ray escort states. It derives an escort-averaged entanglement first law and successfully verifies the QNEC for coherent excitations in a free scalar field. The work provides a sophisticated link between quantum information theory and fundamental energy constraints in QFT.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work connects fundamental energy conditions (QNEC) from general relativity/QFT to quantum information measures (Rényi entropy) via novel mathematical tools (escort averaging), which is highly relevant for understanding quantum constraints in curved spacetime or holographic settings.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to derive a quasi-local form for the $\alpha$-$z$ Rényi Quantum Null Energy Condition (QNEC) using fixed-ray escort states.

**Main result.** A quasi-local form for the $\alpha$-$z$ QNEC is obtained, which is verified for coherent excitations in a free scalar field theory, confirming the conjectured diagonal QNEC for this family.

**Method.** The analysis employs the fixed-ray escort integral representation for Rényi divergence and utilizes modular-energy–entropy decompositions, alongside Fréchet differentiation for variations.

**Model / system.** The study is set in the context of Quantum Field Theories (QFTs), analyzing subregions and null deformations, with explicit calculations performed for free scalar field theory using coherent states.

**Key observables.** $\alpha$-$z$ Rényi divergence, escort-averaged entanglement entropy ($S_{\text{esc}}^{\alpha,z}$), null energy density expectation value ($\langle T_{vv} \rangle$), and the escort-transport term ($X_{\alpha,z;++}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The parameters $\alpha$ and $z$ govern the Rényi divergence, and the analysis is sensitive to the specific choice of the fixed ultraviolet regulator.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on the fixed-ray escort integral representation, and the resulting bound is a quasi-local resolution based on an auxiliary state, not a direct lower bound on the original null energy.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by establishing the fixed-ray escort representation, deriving the modular-energy–entropy decomposition, and then applying these tools to derive the quasi-local QNEC, culminating in explicit calculations for the free scalar field coherent state family.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The fixed-ray escort integral representation expresses $α$--$z$ Rényi divergence as an average over ordinary relative entropy of a family of escort states. Working in the standard UV-regulated density-matrix description of QFT subregions, we use this representation to derive an escort-averaged entanglement first law, an escort-averaged representation of the $α$--$z$ information kernel, and an escort-averaged Bekenstein-type bound for ball-shaped regions in conformal field theories. For the conjectural $α$--$z$ quantum null energy condition (QNEC), we obtain a quasi-local form in which the null energy is evaluated in an escort-averaged state and is corrected by an escort-transport term encoding the failure of escort formation to commute with restriction to a null-deformed region. The $z=α$ specialization gives a similar quasi-local form of the Rényi QNEC for sandwiched Rényi divergence. We explicitly compute the Rényi QNEC, including the explicit escort transport term, for coherent-state excitations in a free scalar field theory. For the same coherent family, we obtain a positive $α$--$z$ null Hessian, verifying the conjectured diagonal $α$--$z$ QNEC for this family.

</details>

### [Relativistic transformation of temperature for exotic cosmological fluids](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03124v1)

**Authors:** Soroor Pouryazdan, Babak Vakili  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.03124v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the relativistic transformation of temperature for exotic cosmological fluids, contrasting the behavior of simple barotropic fluids with those exhibiting complex, density-dependent equations of state like the Chaplygin gas. Using covariant thermodynamics, it shows that negative pressure can suppress temperature growth under boosts, while other models exhibit distinct, non-linear increases in transformed temperature.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on cosmology, the rigorous application of relativistic thermodynamics and the study of non-equilibrium state transformations (like temperature under boosts) provides general theoretical tools applicable to non-equilibrium quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how the temperature of exotic cosmological fluids, particularly those with negative pressure, transforms under relativistic Lorentz boosts.

**Main result.** The transformation behavior differs significantly between barotropic fluids (where negative pressure suppresses temperature growth) and Chaplygin gases (which show a monotonic increase in transformed temperature with velocity).

**Method.** The analysis employs a covariant thermodynamic framework, deriving temperature transformations by applying Lorentz boosts to the energy-momentum tensor and using operational definitions of temperature.

**Model / system.** The study models effective cosmological fluids, specifically barotropic fluids ($p=who$) and generalized Chaplygin gases ($p=-A/ho^\alpha$). These models are used to represent dark energy or exotic matter components in cosmology.

**Key observables.** The transformed temperature ($T'$) and the ratio $T'/T$ as functions of relative velocity ($v$) and background energy density ($ho$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The equation-of-state parameter ($w$), the coupling constant ($A$), and the background energy density ($ho$) are crucial parameters determining the fluid's behavior.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes local thermodynamic equilibrium and adiabatic flow for the fluids considered, and acknowledges that temperature is an operationally derived quantity, not a fundamental Lorentz scalar.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates temperature suppression for barotropic fluids with negative $w$, while Figure 2 shows the monotonic increase of $T'/T$ with velocity for the Chaplygin gas.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the framework for barotropic fluids, deriving the transformation law for $T$. It then extends the analysis to the Chaplygin gas, contrasting its density-dependent behavior with the simpler barotropic case, and concludes by summarizing the implications for different cosmological regimes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the relativistic transformation of temperature for effective cosmological fluids with negative pressure within a covariant thermodynamic framework. The Lorentz transformation of temperature is derived for barotropic fluids with $p=wρ$ and for the (generalized) Chaplygin gas with $p=-A/ρ^α$. For barotropic fluids with constant negative equation-of-state parameter, we show that negative pressure suppresses the growth of temperature under relativistic boosts. In contrast, the Chaplygin gas exhibits a qualitatively different behavior due to its density-dependent pressure, leading to a monotonic increase of the transformed temperature with velocity. The dependence of the relativistic temperature on the relative velocity and background energy density is illustrated through representative examples.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically examines the stability of symmetry-breaking line defects embedded in a 3D O(N) critical bulk. Using advanced CFT and RG methods, the authors determine that stable symmetry breaking is only possible when the symmetry dimension N is greater than or equal to 3. This provides a quantitative criterion for defect stability in critical quantum matter.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous application of CFT and RG techniques to defect lines in critical systems provides powerful tools for understanding boundary physics, which is relevant to engineered quantum materials or interfaces in AMO physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates whether a line defect embedded in a 3D O(N) critical bulk can support a stable spontaneously symmetry-breaking (SSB) defect fixed point, contrasting with known instabilities in 1D systems.

**Main result.** A stable SSB defect fixed point is possible only for $N \gtrsim 3$, while for $N < 3$, domain wall proliferation prevents such stability.

**Method.** The study employs Defect Conformal Field Theory (dCFT) techniques, utilizing the fuzzy sphere method and Renormalization Group (RG) analysis via the g-function to test stability.

**Model / system.** The system models a line defect immersed in a 3D bulk tuned to the O(N) Wilson-Fisher critical point. The stability is analyzed by considering the effect of domain-wall operators and pinning fields.

**Key observables.** Scaling dimensions ($\Delta$) of primary operators, the defect $g$-function, and the relevance/irrelevance of the domain-wall operator.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionality $N$ of the symmetry group, and the critical exponents associated with the O(N) Wilson-Fisher universality class.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the fuzzy-sphere regularization technique for general N, and the stability criteria are based on the RG flow analysis of the defect operators.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically compares RG flow diagrams showing stable vs. unstable SSB fixed points based on the value of N relative to a critical value.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical framework by contrasting 1D vs. 3D defect stability, then applies the fuzzy sphere technique and RG analysis to the O(N) Wilson-Fisher model, deriving conditions for stable defect fixed points based on N.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

While spontaneous breaking of a discrete symmetry in one-dimensional classical systems with short-range interactions is absent, it is expected that a line defect embedded in a bulk criticality exhibits a stable discrete symmetry spontaneous breaking. Here, we investigate the behavior of a pinning-field line defect immersed in a 3D bulk that remains tuned to the $O(N)$ Wilson-Fisher critical point. Employing the fuzzy sphere technique, we provide convincing evidence of the existence of stable defect conformal fixed points, and we demonstrate their renormalization group stability by showing no relevant operator and less effective degrees of freedom than that at bulk fixed point via $g$-function. Moreover, we investigate the defect domain wall operator for various $N$, and we identify that it becomes irrelevance for $N\gtrsim 3$ but it is relevant for $N<3$.These evidence indicate that a one-dimensional defect coupled to a critical bulk cannot support a stable symmetry spontaneously broken defect fixed point due to domain wall proliferation for $N<3$ Wilson-Fisher universality, while in the case of $N \gtrsim 3$ a symmetry broken defect is possible.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work calculates the Casimir free energy for peptide films on silicon, showing that the film's stability depends critically on the substrate's electrical state. The study quantifies how water content and magnetic nanoparticles can tune this stability, offering insights for bioelectronic applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter interfaces, the calculation of fluctuation-induced forces (Casimir energy) is a fundamental topic relevant to understanding boundary conditions and interactions in nanoscale quantum systems, bridging quantum electrodynamics with materials science.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper calculates the Casimir free energy of thin peptide films deposited on silicon substrates, investigating how water content, nanoparticles, and substrate phase transitions affect film stability.

**Main result.** The film stability is highly dependent on the substrate state: the free energy is negative (stable) for thicker films on dielectric silicon but always positive (less stable) if the silicon is metallic.

**Method.** The calculation utilizes the Lifshitz theory of van der Waals and Casimir forces, requiring knowledge of frequency-dependent dielectric permittivities for the constituent materials.

**Model / system.** The system is a three-layer structure: vacuum/air, a peptide film (potentially containing water and nanoparticles), and a silicon substrate. The model incorporates the dielectric-to-metal phase transition in silicon.

**Key observables.** Casimir free energy ($F_p$) as a function of film thickness ($a$), and the borderline thickness ($a_0$) where $F_p=0$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Film thickness ($a$), volume fraction of water ($\Phi$), presence/type of nanoparticles (magnetic vs. nonmagnetic), and the dielectric state of silicon.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the use of the Lifshitz theory and employs approximations like the Clausius-Mossotti equation for mixing and treating the substrate as a semispace.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the magnitude and sign of the Casimir free energy versus film thickness for various water fractions on dielectric silicon, contrasting these results with the always positive energy found in the metallic state.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a structure covering the theoretical formalism, the impact of the metal transition, doping with nanoparticles, determining the zero-energy thickness, and concluding with applications in organic electronics and biomedicine.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Using the Lifshitz theory of the van der Waals and Casimir forces, we calculate the Casimir free energy of thin peptide films deposited on silicon substrates. The Casimir free energy is found as a function of film thickness for different fractions of water in the film, in the presence of either nonmagnetic or magnetic nanoparticles, and under the impact of irradiation of a silicon substrate with laser pulses or dopants resulting in the dielectric-to-metal phase transition. It is shown that for a dielectric silicon there is the borderline value of the film thickness, such that the Casimir free energy is negative and contributes to the film stability for thicker films, but is positive and makes the film less stable for thinner ones. According to our results, the borderline value of peptide film thickness decreases with increasing volume fractions of water and in the film. This decrease is more pronounced for the magnetic nanoparticles and becomes stronger with increasing their radius. The borderline value of peptide film thickness is found as a function of the fraction of water in the film. If the silicon substrate is in metallic state, the Casimir free energy of peptide coating is always positive, which makes it less stable. Possible applications of the obtained results in organic electronics and biomedicine are discussed.

</details>

**Authors:** Poramat Chianvichai, Poramet Pathumsoot, Naphan Benchasattabuse, Michal Hajdušek, Rodney Van Meter, Sujin Suwanna  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.03496v1>

**Summary.** This work analyzes how to efficiently extract multiple entangled Bell pairs from a Repeater Graph State (RGS) used in quantum repeaters. Through simulations, the authors demonstrate that the achievable entanglement yield is highly dependent on the network's connectivity, suggesting an optimal balance between sparse and dense connections. This guides the design of more resource-efficient quantum communication protocols.

**Why it may be interesting.** The findings provide concrete, topology-dependent guidelines for designing robust quantum communication networks, which is crucial for scaling quantum internet infrastructure.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates strategies to maximize the extraction of Bell pairs from a Repeater Graph State (RGS), addressing a known bottleneck where only one pair can be extracted from a complete bipartite graph state.

**Main result.** The maximum Bell pair yield depends critically on the RGS connection topology, showing that optimal yield occurs at low to moderate edge densities, and higher inner-qubit connectivity is needed for longer network hops.

**Method.** The study uses simulations to calculate the expected number of Bell pairs by analyzing the Pauli measurements performed on the RGS structure, relating the yield to the graph's connectivity.

**Model / system.** The physical model is the quantum repeater utilizing the Repeater Graph State (RGS) architecture, which is a memory-less alternative for long-distance quantum communication. The RGS is defined on a graph G(V, E) and its properties are analyzed via graph state measurements.

**Key observables.** Bell-pair yield ($\langle n_{	ext{bell}} angle$), which is the primary observable, and the required inner-qubit connectivity.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Edge density, number of network hops, and the parameters of the Gamma distribution used to model edge counts.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The RGS structure is assumed to provide protection against photon loss by embedding Quantum Error Correction (QEC) through tree-graph-encoded logical qubits.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate graph state representations, measurement procedures, and heat maps showing the expected Bell pair yield across various RGS topologies and edge densities, demonstrating the optimal connectivity.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the need for RGSs in quantum repeaters, details the measurement formalism using Pauli measurements, and then simulates the Bell-pair yield, concluding with an analysis of how optimal connectivity shifts with increasing network hop count.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum repeaters are essential for establishing long-distance quantum communication to overcome the exponential decay of entanglement due to photon loss. Traditional repeater architectures rely on physical quantum memory, which introduces decoherence and poses significant practical implementation challenges. The repeater graph state (RGS) architecture offers a promising memory-less alternative that is inherently resilient to photon losses. A key challenge in implementing RGS lies in the requirement for highly efficient graph state generators and complex qubit measurement. In this work, we aim to investigate the strategies for extracting the maximum number of Bell pairs from the RGS structure via its qubit connection to resolve the well-known bottleneck problem of RGS in which only a single Bell pair can be extracted from a complete bipartite graph state. From simulations, we observe that the maximum number of extracted Bell pairs depends on its connection topology, where the Bell-pair yield tends to be maximal at low to moderate edge densities. As the number of network hops increases, the RGS must be equipped with higher inner-qubit connectivity to maintain a sufficient yield of extractable Bell pairs. Thus, the expected resource requirement shifts toward the use of RGSs with higher inner-qubit connectivity.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Approximate Maximum-Likelihood Decoding (AMLD), a novel, efficient framework for decoding quantum error-correcting codes. AMLD estimates the true likelihood by using truncated free energies derived from candidate samples, overcoming the computational barrier of exact Maximum-Likelihood Decoding. Benchmarks show that AMLD significantly improves the error threshold compared to standard Minimum-Weight Decoding across various quantum codes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, computationally tractable method to estimate the true performance limits of quantum error correction codes by leveraging statistical physics tools (free energy estimation) to overcome NP-hard decoding problems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the computational hardness of Maximum-Likelihood Decoding (MLD) for quantum error-correcting codes, which is necessary for determining the minimum logical error rate.

**Main result.** The proposed Approximate Maximum-Likelihood Decoding (AMLD) framework significantly closes the performance gap between the standard Minimum-Weight Decoding (MWD) and the optimal MLD, showing up to an 83% improvement on benchmark codes.

**Method.** AMLD is a black-box framework that estimates the free energy for each logical class by recycling candidate samples discarded by stochastic inner decoders, providing a provable bound between the exact free energy and the empirical minimum weight.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on quantum error-correcting codes, specifically stabilizer codes like the Toric and color codes, which are analyzed under i.i.d. Pauli noise (bit-flip or depolarizing). The problem is mapped to finding the ground state of a disordered Ising model.

**Key observables.** Logical error rate ($P_L$), threshold gap closure percentage, and the ratio $\lambda = p_L(	ext{AMLD}) / p_L(	ext{MWD})$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Noise type (bit-flip, depolarizing), code structure (e.g., $6.6.6$ color code), and the number of decoding trials ($N_{	ext{total}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes that the free energy estimation is bounded by the empirical minimum weight and the exact free energy, and that the stochastic solvers provide a sufficiently diverse pool of candidate samples.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the logical error rate ratio ($\lambda$) across different codes and noise models, and show $P_{	ext{fail}}$ vs. $p$ curves demonstrating AMLD's superior performance relative to MWD.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical gap between MLD and MWD. It then introduces the AMLD framework using truncated free-energy estimators. Finally, it benchmarks AMLD against MWD on various codes (Toric, color, etc.) under different noise models to quantify the performance improvement.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Maximum-likelihood decoding (MLD) achieves the minimum logical error rate of stabilizer codes under known i.i.d. Pauli noise, but its exact evaluation is \#P-hard. Practical pipelines therefore approximate MLD by minimum-weight decoding (MWD), retaining only the lowest-weight recovery per syndrome and discarding the coset degeneracy. The minimum-weight search is in turn implemented by stochastic solvers. We introduce approximate maximum-likelihood decoding (AMLD), a black-box framework that recycles the candidate samples discarded by stochastic inner decoders into a per-class truncated free-energy estimator. For every logical class represented in the candidate pool, the estimator is provably bounded below by the exact free energy and above by the empirical minimum weight. AMLD returns the logical class minimizing the estimated free energy with linear classical overhead. In SA-based Ising-decoder benchmarks, AMLD closes up to $83\%$ of the MWD--MLD threshold gap across the toric and color codes under bit-flip and depolarizing noise. The largest threshold improvement, from $17.28\%$ to $18.62\%$, occurs on the $6.6.6$ color code under depolarizing noise. We further demonstrate AMLD on the $[[144,12,12]]$ bivariate-bicycle code, whose bit-flip decoding problem has a hypergraph structure. This application requires neither matching-based enumeration nor code-specific tensor-network contraction. At $p=0.05$, AMLD reduces the logical error rate by $13\%$ relative to MWD evaluated on the same BP-OSD candidate pool.

</details>

**Authors:** Rahul Sahay, David M. Long, Vedika Khemani

(a) Thus, a sheet-like logical Z in the 4D toric code (shown in a cross-section at fourth coordinate w = 0) encodes the implementation of (b) an ad- dressable logical CZ gate between two toric codes TC and TC. Finally, synthesize the gate by converting the chain map to a physical quantum cir- cuit.</sub>

**Summary.** This paper introduces the chain map hierarchy, a novel mathematical framework that unifies the description of all logical quantum gates (Pauli, Clifford, non-Clifford) for qLDPC codes. By mapping logical operations to homology classes in a chain complex, the authors provide a systematic method to discover and implement complex gates, such as the full Clifford group, on physical quantum hardware models like the toric code.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful, algebraic tool (homological algebra) to systematically analyze and predict the feasibility of implementing complex quantum gates on physically relevant quantum error-correcting codes.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a unified and intuitive framework for performing logical computation using quantum low-density parity check (qLDPC) codes, treating Pauli, Clifford, and non-Clifford operations equally.

**Main result.** The authors introduce the chain map hierarchy, which allows for the discovery of constant-depth unitary implementations for the full logical Clifford group on 2D toric codes and addressable CCZ gates on 3D toric codes.

**Method.** The core method involves defining the chain map hierarchy—a family of chain complexes—whose homology classes encode logical unitary operations, generalizing the existing formalism for Pauli logicals.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on qLDPC codes, specifically analyzing the 2D and 3D toric codes, and extending the framework to non-manifold codes.

**Key observables.** Homology classes (encoding logical operators) and boundaries (encoding stabilizers) of the associated chain complex.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The level of the Clifford hierarchy ($\ell$), the number of code blocks (e.g., any number of blocks of the 2D toric code).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes that logical operations can be mapped onto homology classes within the defined chain map structure.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the chain map hierarchy, extending from Pauli logicals to Clifford and non-Clifford gates. Figure 2 shows applications, including realizing the full Clifford group on the 2D toric code and finding non-Clifford gates in the 3D toric code.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds the chain map hierarchy by defining chain complexes that encode logical operations, demonstrating how this structure unifies the description of Pauli, Clifford, and non-Clifford gates, and then applies this framework to discover specific, high-fidelity logical gates on various toric and fracton codes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a framework for logical computation with qLDPC codes that places logical Pauli, Clifford, and non-Clifford operations on the same footing. This brings the simple homological description of Pauli logicals to the patchwork landscape of logical Clifford and non-Clifford operations, providing a tool for the discovery of new logical gates. In particular, we define the chain map hierarchy: a family of chain complexes whose homology classes encode logical unitary and code surgery operations at any level of the Clifford hierarchy, precisely analogous to the chain complex description of Pauli logicals. Consequently, intuition for Pauli logicals can be leveraged to discover new logical operations on qLDPC codes. For instance, the familiar ability to deform Pauli logicals with stabilizers---i.e. boundaries of the chain complex---becomes a way to search for constant-depth unitary implementations of (non-)Clifford logical gates. Using this strategy, we discover constant-depth unitary implementations of the full logical Clifford group on any number of blocks of the 2D toric code, including within a single block, and addressable logical CCZ gates on any triple of logical qubits on any number of blocks of the 3D toric code. Beyond manifold codes, we find addressable non-Clifford gates on codes with many encoded qubits. The chain map hierarchy naturally encompasses and extends other constructions of logical gadgets, for instance providing a universal parameterization of cup products. As such, our work provides a unified, useful, and intuitive language for computing with qLDPC codes.

</details>
