**Summary.** This theoretical paper investigates cusped defects within Conformal Field Theories, establishing that cusp scaling operators behave like primary operators under conformal transformations. By developing the Cusp Operator Expansion, the authors provide a systematic way to analyze complex defects and derive fundamental analytic constraints on their associated physical data. This work advances the understanding of defect physics in highly symmetric quantum field theories.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly specialized in CFT, the rigorous treatment of singular boundaries (cusps) and the use of operator expansions and bootstrap methods are general theoretical tools relevant to understanding boundary effects in quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper analyzes the general properties of cusped defect lines embedded within Conformal Field Theories (CFTs). It aims to understand how these defects behave under conformal transformations and how they can be systematically expanded.

**Main result.** The authors prove that suitably defined cusp scaling operators transform like primary operators under general conformal transformations, and they derive analytic constraints on the dynamical cusp data using a crossing equation.

**Method.** The primary methods involve developing the Cusp Operator Expansion (COE) to represent general defects, deriving transformation properties using conformal symmetry, and applying crossing equations to constrain parameters.

**Model / system.** The study is set in the context of generic Conformal Field Theories (CFTs) featuring one-dimensional defect lines that possess singular points, specifically cusps. The analysis uses techniques from path integrals and radial quantization around these cusps.

**Key observables.** Cusp scaling operators, correlation functions of cusped defects, and the cusp anomalous dimension ($\Gamma$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The cusp angle ($\phi$), the small cutoff parameter ($\epsilon$), and the coupling constant ($\lambda$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the defect expectation value is invariant under conformal transformations if the submanifold is smooth, and that the defect is reflection positive to ensure a positive definite scalar product.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates a line defect with a cusp formed by the intersection of two straight lines. Figure 2 shows state preparation via path integral inside a sphere with boundary conditions specified at the UV ball.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by defining the Hilbert space and the COE, proving the transformation rules for cusp operators, providing worked examples (like the magnetic field defect), deriving analytic constraints via crossing equations, and discussing bootstrap applications.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We analyze general properties of cusped defect lines embedded in generic conformal field theories (CFTs). We prove that suitably defined cusp scaling operators transform like primary operators under general conformal transformations. We show that, via the cusp operator expansion (COE), defects of general shape can be expanded in a basis of cusped defects. These findings are checked and exemplified in the case of the localized magnetic field defect. Finally, we derive analytic constraints on dynamical cusp data by making use of a crossing equation, and discuss possible numerical bootstrap applications of our results.

</details>

**Summary.** This work establishes 'distinctness' as the fundamental necessary threshold for a unitary ensemble to be computationally pseudorandom. The authors demonstrate that achieving this pseudorandomness does not require the ensemble to possess the stronger property of statistical pseudorandomness (like being a unitary 2-design). They provide new constructions and resource bounds based solely on this distinctness criterion.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of 'distinctness' provides a novel, necessary, and potentially weaker resource constraint for quantum pseudorandomness than traditional design theory, which is highly relevant for designing efficient quantum protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the poorly understood relationship between two forms of quantum pseudorandomness: statistical (unitary designs) and computational (pseudorandom unitaries, PRUs).

**Main result.** It proves that statistical pseudorandomness is not necessary for PRUs, introducing 'distinctness' as a necessary threshold property that characterizes PRUs.

**Method.** The authors define and characterize 'distinctness' using an entangled analogue of anticoncentration, comparing it to established concepts like unitary designs.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on constructing and analyzing ensembles of unitary operators acting on $n$ qubits, particularly in the context of the Parallel Forward-Query (PFC) framework.

**Key observables.** Distinctness, Entangled Anticoncentration ($\delta$-EAC), Imaginarity ($	ext{Ip}(U)$), and Coherence ($	ext{Cp}(U)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The required distinctness level is $	ext{negl}(n)$, and the equivalence theorems hold in the computationally bounded regime where query depth $t = 	ext{poly}(n)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The equivalence between distinctness and EAC relies on the assumption of a computationally bounded query depth ($t = 	ext{poly}(n)$).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the concepts of distinctness and EAC, proves their equivalence under certain conditions, uses this property to construct new PRU ensembles that bypass the need for full unitary designs, and applies the concept to constrain existing conjectures.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Pseudorandomness is increasingly recognized as a key property of ensembles in quantum information theory, statistical mechanics, and quantum many-body physics. Yet it appears in two conceptually different forms: statistical pseudorandomness, embodied by unitary designs, and computational pseudorandomness captured by pseudorandom unitaries (PRUs). The relationship between these two forms of pseudorandomness remains surprisingly poorly understood. Existing PRU constructions reveal this interplay where a statistically randomizing ingredient, a unitary design, is combined with classical cryptographic primitives to produce computational pseudorandomness. We show that statistical pseudorandomness is not necessary for computationally pseudorandom unitaries. We do this by replacing the unitary $2$-design layer in the existing constructions with ensembles that are not even state $1$-designs, yet are sufficiently {\em distinct}, a property we identify to be necessary for any PRU. This yields new non-adaptively secure PRU ensembles whose computational pseudorandomness is obtained without an underlying statistically pseudorandom quantum ensemble, such as a $2$-design. We characterize distinctness via an entangled analogue of anticoncentration and use it to show that distinctness already captures constraints on coherence and imaginarity of PRUs, while identifying broad classes of inputs for which the latter obstruction disappears, enabling real-valued PRUs even for certain (maximally) entangled states. As an application, we use lack of distinctness to constrain the conjectured pseudorandomness of the random phase-Hadamard ensemble to form a PRU.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a polynomial classical algorithm to analyze the reduced Dynamical Lie Algebra (DLA) for QAOA, specifically when applied to tree graphs. By leveraging graph structure properties like shortest paths and degree parities, the authors can determine the quantum dynamics' expressibility. This allows researchers to theoretically guide and understand quantum algorithms using only classical graph theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful, purely classical structural tool to diagnose the fundamental quantum limitations (expressibility) of variational quantum algorithms before running them on expensive quantum hardware.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of determining the expressive power and controllability of quantum circuits, specifically for the QAOA algorithm, whose underlying dynamics are captured by the Dynamical Lie Algebra (DLA).

**Main result.** For QAOA on tree graphs, the authors developed a polynomial classical algorithm that can determine the reduced DLA structure, certifying maximal expressibility or providing rigorous lower bounds on the DLA dimension.

**Method.** The approach involves developing a classical, polynomial-time algorithm that recursively partitions graph vertices using shortest-path distances and degree parities to analyze the structure of the reduced DLA.

**Model / system.** The model is the Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAOA) applied to combinatorial optimization problems like MaxCut. The dynamics are analyzed via the Dynamical Lie Algebra (DLA) generated by the problem and mixer Hamiltonians.

**Key observables.** The structure and dimension of the reduced Dynamical Lie Algebra (DLA), and the containment of single-site Pauli operators within this algebra.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The graph structure ($\Gamma$), particularly when it is a tree (acyclic), which simplifies the Lie-algebraic analysis.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core results are established under the assumption that the underlying graph $\Gamma$ is connected and acyclic (a tree).

**Figures summary.** The notes reference figures illustrating the symmetry-reduction framework (fixing a reference vertex $v$) and the general structure of the QAOA circuit.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the general problem of DLA construction to a specific, tractable case: QAOA on trees. It introduces graph-theoretic diagnostics (distance layers, parity patterns) to derive algebraic containment results for the reduced DLA.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Classically equivalent formulations of an optimization problem need not lead to equivalent quantum algorithms. For MaxCut, fixing the value of a single vertex removes a simple global symmetry without changing the underlying optimization problem, yet it can substantially alter the quantum dynamics generated by the Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAOA). These dynamics are captured by the circuit's dynamical Lie algebra (DLA), whose direct construction can become exponentially expensive. Here we show that, for symmetry-reduced QAOA on trees, substantial information about the reduced DLA can instead be obtained efficiently from the graph alone. We introduce a polynomial classical algorithm that recursively distinguishes vertices using shortest path structure and degree parity. When all vertices are resolved individually, the method determines the complete reduced DLA and, under the corresponding graph conditions, certifies maximal expressibility of the reduced QAOA ansatz. Even when full resolution is not achieved, the algorithm identifies embedded subalgebras, provides rigorous lower bounds on DLA dimension, and certifies controllable subsystems. Our theoretical and experimental results show how classical graph structure can be used to diagnose and potentially guide the quantum dynamics of variational algorithms before running them on quantum hardware.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously characterizes the geometric constraints governing the entanglement structure of three-qubit pure states. It moves beyond simple upper bounds by deriving necessary and sufficient semi-algebraic conditions for the coexistence of pairwise concurrences and the three-tangle. The results provide exact formulas to determine the possible range of a missing entanglement component given the others.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous mathematical tools to map out the precise boundaries of entanglement resource spaces, which is crucial for understanding the limits of quantum correlations in multi-partite systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To determine the fundamental, necessary, and sufficient constraints governing the coexistence of multiple entanglement measures (pairwise concurrences and three-tangle) in a three-qubit pure state.

**Main result.** The paper provides a necessary-and-sufficient semi-algebraic characterization for the entire achievable entanglement region ($\Omega_3$) and derives exact, parameter-free intervals for missing bipartite entanglement components.

**Method.** The authors use supporting geometry, canonical state parametrization (Ac´ın form), and algebraic elimination techniques to characterize the boundary of the entanglement region.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on three-qubit pure quantum states, $|\psiangle_{ABC}$, and the entanglement measures associated with them, such as pairwise concurrences ($C_{ij}$) and the three-tangle ($	au_3$).

**Key observables.** Pairwise concurrences ($C_{AB}, C_{AC}, C_{BC}$) and the three-tangle ($	au_3$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The state is analyzed in a four-dimensional compatibility body ($\Omega_3$), and the analysis involves auxiliary parameters ($s$) and transformed variables ($X, Y, Z$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The final characterization relies on the Ac´ın standard form parametrization and the existence of an auxiliary parameter $s$ to define the boundary conditions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate cross-sections of the three-qubit entanglement region in the space of squared concurrences for varying $	au_3$, showing how GHZ correlations constrain pairwise entanglement.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing initial global supporting linear constraints (Theorem 1) to deriving the full, non-linear, necessary-and-sufficient semi-algebraic characterization (Theorem 2), culminating in the exact determination of completion intervals for missing entanglement components.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a permutation-symmetric supporting relation between the pairwise concurrences and the three-tangle in three-qubit system, whose extrema are attained only by the symmetric $W$ and GHZ local-unitary classes. Furthermore, by introducing the concept of a four-dimensional compatibility body, we show that our proposed global relation and the previous CKW relation can be uniformly viewed as different supporting directions of the same achievable set. Subsequently, we provide a necessary-and-sufficient semi-algebraic characterization of the complete four-dimensional compatibility of a three-qubit pure state. Based on this, we can conversely describe the compatibility between entangled components: given the remaining entangled components, the missing two-body entangled component can only take values within a precise parameter-free interval. We demonstrate that the above construction also applies to arbitrary concurrence-generated entanglement measures.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates that the material KFeF3 undergoes a low-temperature phase transition driven by a hidden magnetic octupole order, rather than conventional structural distortion. The key mechanism involves spin-orbit coupling stabilizing this higher-rank magnetic order, which is responsible for observed weak ferromagnetism. The study establishes KFeF3 as a model system for understanding multipolar magnetism driven by competing electronic energy scales.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of hidden, multipolar order driven by SOC is highly relevant to understanding exotic magnetic states in strongly correlated materials, which often exhibit complex, non-collinear magnetic ground states.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates phase transitions driven by 'hidden order'—magnetic or structural instabilities lacking conventional symmetry-breaking signatures.

**Main result.** The low-temperature transition in KFeF3 is driven by a hidden magnetic octupole order parameter, which is stabilized by spin-orbit coupling (SOC) and accounts for weak ferromagnetism.

**Method.** The research combines high-resolution powder diffraction, magnetometry, symmetry-based Landau analysis, and first-principles DFT calculations.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the 3d perovskite, KFeF3, which exhibits multiple magnetic and structural transitions between cubic, rhombohedral, and monoclinic phases.

**Key observables.** Weak ferromagnetism (wFM), symmetry-breaking strain, magnetic octupole order parameter, and the evolution of the effective magnetic moment ($\mu_{	ext{eff}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The competition between spin-orbit coupling (SOC) and orbital degeneracy, which dictates the emergence of higher-rank magnetic multipolar degrees of freedom.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on symmetry-based Landau theory and DFT calculations to model the coupling between different magnetic multipolar orders.

**Figures summary.** Figures show magnetic susceptibility (M(T), M(H)), variable temperature neutron diffraction data confirming structural transitions, and theoretical diagrams illustrating the coupling of the octupole order parameter to strain and weak ferromagnetism.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from characterizing conventional transitions (90 K) to identifying the hidden instability (40 K). It uses symmetry analysis (Landau theory) to pinpoint the octupole order, and DFT calculations confirm the role of SOC in suppressing competing instabilities and stabilizing the multipolar state.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Phase transitions are typically driven by symmetry-breaking structural distortions that lift electronic degeneracies, yet in some cases, these transitions may be driven by a hidden order without conventional structural signatures. Here, we demonstrate that the 3$d$ perovskite, KFeF$_3$, hosts such a hidden instability. Using a combination of high-resolution powder diffraction, magnetometry, symmetry-based analysis and first principles calculations, we reveal that while the 90 K cubic to rhombohedral transition arises from conventional magnetostriction accompanying antiferromagnetic order, a second transition at 40 K lowers the symmetry to monoclinic without any detectable Jahn-Teller distortion or translational symmetry-breaking. Symmetry-based Landau analysis supports a hidden zone centered magnetic octupole order parameter, whose improper coupling accounts for the weak ferromagnetism and pronounced symmetry-breaking strain. Density functional calculations show that spin-orbit coupling suppresses the competing Jahn-Teller instability, and thereby favors higher rank magnetic multipolar degrees of freedom. These findings establish KFeF$_3$ as a model system in which the competing energy scales between spin-orbit coupling and orbital degeneracy result in the emergence of hidden, multipolar order. We show that the control of such magnetic, multipolar order could, in principle, provide a route to induce altermagnetism.

</details>

**Authors:** Fabio Deelan Cunden, Jakub Czartowski, Giovanni Gramegna, Marilena Ligabò  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.04057v1>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper analyzes the limiting behavior of stochastic semigroups derived from Gibbs distributions as temperature approaches zero. It proves that the limit of the semigroups does not generally commute with taking the semigroup of the limit distribution. The resulting structure is characterized by block-upper-triangular matrices, defining a novel mathematical concept called Block-Triangular majorisation.

**Why it may be interesting.** The non-commutativity result is crucial for understanding how thermodynamic limits affect the dynamics of open quantum systems, suggesting that the path taken to the limit matters significantly.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the mathematical structure of the limit of stochastic semigroups associated with probability vectors $\gamma(eta)$ as the inverse temperature $eta$ approaches infinity.

**Main result.** The limiting semigroup is generally not the semigroup preserving the limiting distribution, but rather a subsemigroup characterized by block-upper-triangular stochastic matrices, leading to a new concept called Block-Triangular majorisation.

**Method.** The authors develop a general framework involving multiscale decomposition and analyzing the Kuratowski limit of the semigroups, utilizing majorization theory and combinatorial counting.

**Model / system.** The model involves stochastic matrices whose invariant distributions are Gibbs vectors $\gamma(eta) \propto e^{-eta E_i}$, arising in the context of statistical mechanics at varying temperatures.

**Key observables.** Stochastic matrices, semigroups, Block-Triangular majorisation, and Rényi $\alpha$-entropies.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The inverse temperature $eta$, particularly its limit $eta 	o \infty$ (zero-temperature limit).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often relies on the exponential separation of scales in the Gibbs case and the existence of a limiting temperature $eta_0$ for certain inequalities.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the non-commutativity of limit operations using different asymptotic trajectories and provide flowcharts detailing the extreme points of the stochastic semigroups for small systems ($n=3, 4$).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes a general framework for limiting semigroups, specializes this to the Gibbs case using multiscale profiles, characterizes the resulting block-upper-triangular structure, and proves the equivalence between this structure and the Block-Triangular majorisation preorder.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate limits of semigroups of stochastic matrices defined by their invariant distribution. Given probability vectors $γ(β)$ depending on a parameter $β$, we introduce a notion of convergence as $β\to\infty$ for the corresponding semigroups of $γ(β)$-preserving stochastic matrices and investigate the structure of the resulting limit. In general, the limiting semigroup differs from the semigroup preserving the limiting distribution, showing that these two operations do not commute. We develop a general framework for such limiting semigroups and study in detail the case in which the invariant distributions are Gibbs vectors at the inverse temperature $β$. We show that the limiting semigroup consists of block-upper-triangular stochastic matrices subject to additional substochasticity constraints. We characterise and enumerate their extremal elements and determine the preorder on probability vectors induced by the action of the semigroup. The resulting notion of Block-Triangular majorisation interpolates between ordinary majorisation and upper triangular (aka unordered) majorisation. We show that it is completely characterised by a finite family of monotones and analyse the corresponding behaviour of Rényi $α$-entropies as $β\to\infty$.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously proves that the separable quantum optimal transport cost, derived using the Hilbert-Schmidt cost, satisfies the triangle inequality, thereby establishing it as a true metric. The authors achieve this by employing sophisticated tools from convex analysis, such as convex-roof duality, to overcome known mathematical obstructions in the field. This result provides a fundamental geometric structure for quantifying distances between quantum states.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous mathematical proof of metricity for quantum transport distances is crucial for developing robust geometric tools in quantum information theory, providing a quantitative measure of distinguishability between quantum states.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to prove that the separable quantum optimal transport cost, $d_{sep}(ho, \sigma)$, associated with the Hilbert-Schmidt cost, defines a genuine metric on the space of density matrices.

**Main result.** The core result is the establishment of the triangle inequality for $d_{sep}(ho, \sigma)$, confirming it is a true distance metric. Furthermore, it shows that the separable SWAP fidelity yields a metric on the state space.

**Method.** The proof relies heavily on advanced convex analysis techniques, specifically replacing a failing 'gluing argument' with convex-roof duality and utilizing a dimension-independent interpolation result for Hermitian operators.

**Model / system.** The system involves quantum states represented by density matrices ($ho, \sigma$) in a finite-dimensional Hilbert space ($\mathcal{H}^n$). The analysis focuses on the quantum optimal transport framework restricted to separable quantum couplings.

**Key observables.** The separable quantum optimal transport cost, $d_{sep}(ho, \sigma)$, and the associated separable SWAP fidelity, $F_{S,sep}(ho, \sigma)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The cost function is derived from the Hilbert-Schmidt distance between pure states, $c(x, z) = rac{1}{2}(1 - |\langle x, z angle|^2)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The proof requires establishing a key interpolating Hermitian operator (Lemma 2.3) to circumvent the 'quantum marginal obstruction' that invalidates standard gluing arguments.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes $d_{sep}$ as a semidistance, then derives the dual formulation for the transport cost using convex analysis (convex-roof duality), and finally proves the main theorem by establishing the triangle inequality.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We prove that the square root of the separable quantum optimal transport cost associated with the orthogonal projection onto the antisymmetric subspace defines a genuine distance between density matrices. Equivalently, this establishes the triangle inequality for the order-two Beatty-França quantum optimal transport construction induced by the Hilbert-Schmidt distance between pure states. The result also proves metricity of the corresponding distance derived from separable SWAP fidelity. The proof replaces the unavailable gluing argument by convex-roof duality and a dimension-independent interpolation result for Hermitian operators.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a multi-body quantum teleportation protocol using scrambling, motivated by holographic wormhole geometries. By extending the standard two-party setup to three parties, the authors demonstrate that the protocol can probe spatial information spreading, revealing features like holographic causal shadows. The results offer a new diagnostic tool for understanding information transfer in complex quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, testable quantum information protocol whose diagnostics (like spatial resolution) are directly linked to deep concepts in quantum gravity and holography, making it highly relevant for theoretical quantum information scientists.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper generalizes quantum teleportation to a multi-body setting using quantum scrambling, drawing motivation from holographic models of traversable wormholes.

**Main result.** The three-party setup reveals spatial or subsystem resolution of information spreading, and the fidelity and required coupling exhibit dependencies on distance reminiscent of holographic causal shadows.

**Method.** The analysis involves studying the protocol's success probability using both analytical techniques and numerical simulations employing random quantum circuits.

**Model / system.** The system is a three-party quantum teleportation protocol involving three qubit systems (A, B, C), initialized with EPR pairs analogous to a three-boundary holographic state.

**Key observables.** Teleportation fidelity, minimum required coupling, and the dependence of these on distance from the injection site.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionality of dynamics (one-dimensional vs. all-to-all) and the number of parties (three).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis uses a single-qubit message and often relies on the infinite-temperature limit for holographic comparisons.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the two-sided protocol, the pair of pants geometry for the three-party state, and the causal structure including a causal shadow.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the standard two-party protocol to a three-party generalization, analyzing dynamics under 1D and all-to-all coupling, and comparing results to predictions from multi-boundary wormhole holography.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Unlike standard quantum teleportation, many-body teleportation uses scrambling to transmit quantum information. In this protocol, initially localized information spreads over many degrees of freedom and is later refocused at the receiver by a simple coupling between the systems, followed by further many-body evolution. The protocol was developed from models of traversable wormholes in holography and has become a useful probe of scrambling. In particular, it can distinguish genuine scrambling from decoherence or noise when out-of-time-order correlators fail, and can reveal signatures of different scrambling mechanisms, including the distinctive behavior expected in holographic systems. Holography predicts that related protocols can transmit information between selected boundaries of multi-boundary wormhole geometries. Motivated by this setting, we study single-qubit many-body teleportation among three systems of qubits. The initial state consists of EPR pairs distributed among them, providing a simple analogue of an infinite-temperature three-boundary holographic state. We analyze the protocol with one-dimensional and all-to-all dynamics, both analytically and numerically using random circuits. We find that the third system suppresses teleportation once the spreading message reaches the region of qubits that are entangled with it. In one dimension, both the minimum coupling required for successful teleportation and the fidelity depend on the distance from the injection site to this region, a feature reminiscent of holographic causal shadows. For all-to-all dynamics, successful teleportation is instead restricted to early times and to sufficiently few qubits entangled with the third system. A third system therefore provides spatial, or subsystem, resolution of information spreading that is absent from the two-sided protocol. Our results offer a step toward many-body teleportation networks.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a resource theory framework to quantify 'non-local magic' in bipartite quantum states. The authors derive an exact, closed-form solution for this magic measure using the state's Schmidt spectrum. Crucially, they establish that this non-local magic is equivalent to the minimum magic achievable through the purification process, offering both an efficient calculation and a deep physical interpretation.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous, analytically tractable measure (non-local magic) for quantifying non-local quantum correlations, linking it to established resource theories like purification.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to provide an efficient analytical characterization and resource-theoretic interpretation of 'non-local magic,' a measure quantifying the non-stabilizerness of bipartite quantum states.

**Main result.** They prove that the non-local magic, specifically for the log-stabilizer fidelity, coincides with the minimum magic of purification along the unitary orbit of the reduced density operator.

**Method.** The authors derive an exact closed-form solution for the log-stabilizer fidelity based on the Schmidt spectrum and introduce the 'magic of purification' as a resource-theoretic extension.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on bipartite quantum states ($ho_{AB}$ or $|\psiangle_{AB}$), analyzing their non-local magic relative to stabilizer states ($	ext{STAB}_1, 	ext{STAB}_0$) and the concept of state purification.

**Key observables.** Non-local magic, log-stabilizer fidelity ($D_{	ext{NL stab}}$), magic of purification ($D_{	ext{pur stab}}$), and the Schmidt spectrum.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The Schmidt coefficients ($	heta_i$) of the bipartite state are crucial as they determine the closed-form solution.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is tractable by focusing on the log-stabilizer fidelity, as the general evaluation of non-local magic requires difficult optimization over local unitaries.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining non-local magic, deriving the closed-form solution for the log-stabilizer fidelity, introducing the magic of purification, and finally proving the equivalence between the two measures via the unitary orbit of the reduced density operator.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Non-local magic quantifies the non-stabilizerness of a bipartite quantum state that cannot be removed by local unitary transformations. Despite its natural definition, its evaluation generally requires a difficult optimization over local unitaries. Here, we show that for the log-stabilizer fidelity this optimization admits an exact closed-form analytic solution depending only on the Schmidt spectrum. We then introduce the magic of purification, defined as the minimum pure-state magic over all purifications of a mixed state, and show that it naturally induces a resource theory whose free states are normalized stabilizer-code projectors. For the log-stabilizer fidelity, the magic of purification admits a distance-based formulation in terms of the Uhlmann fidelity. Remarkably, we prove that non-local magic coincides with the minimum magic of purification along the unitary orbit of the reduced density operator. Our results provide both an efficient analytical characterization and a mixed-state resource-theoretic interpretation of non-local magic.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper analyzes the geometric structure of the stabilizer polytope in high-dimensional quantum systems. It derives quantitative bounds on the polytope's size and proves a sharp phase transition for when random quantum states are likely to be 'magic.' The results show that the polytope has extremely high combinatorial complexity, suggesting that its exact description requires an exponentially large number of constraints.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous geometric characterization of the boundary between 'magic' and 'non-magic' states provides fundamental insights into the structure of quantum correlations, which is crucial for understanding quantum error correction and resource theories.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper characterizes the geometry and typicality of 'quantum magic' states—states that are convex mixtures of pure stabilizer states—in high-dimensional quantum systems.

**Main result.** It establishes sharp bounds on the geometric complexity (e.g., doubly exponential number of facets) of the stabilizer polytope and determines a sharp phase transition scale for when random induced states are likely to be magic.

**Method.** The analysis relies heavily on advanced geometric techniques from convex geometry, including bounding the Hilbert-Schmidt inradius and volume radius of the polytope, and applying Gaussian approximation theorems to random states.

**Model / system.** The system is an $n$-qubit system with dimension $d=2^n$. The focus is on the stabilizer polytope ($	ext{STAB}_n$), which contains all mixed stabilizer states, and random states obtained by tracing out a subsystem from a Haar-random pure state.

**Key observables.** Purity ($	ext{Tr}(ho^2)$), Hilbert-Schmidt inradius ($	ext{rin}(K_n)$), Volume radius ($	ext{vrad}(K_n)$), and the transition dimension $k_\star$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The system dimension $d=2^n$, the environment dimension $k$, and the critical purity threshold $a^* \approx 0.458327$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis uses the assumption of Haar-random pure states and relies on asymptotic convex geometry tools like the Bourgain-Milman inequality.

**Figures summary.** No figures were explicitly summarized, but the notes detail the derivation of bounds for the inradius, volume radius, and the transition scale $k^*(d)$ using various inequalities.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first establishing purity bounds related to the inradius (Theorem 1), then bounding the volume radius (Theorem 2), followed by determining the sharp phase transition scale for magic typicality (Theorem 3), and finally quantifying the combinatorial complexity via the facet count (Theorem 4).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We prove that, for an $n$-qubit system of dimension $d=2^n$, every state satisfying $\operatorname{Tr}(ρ^2)\le 1/(d-a_\ast)$, with $a_\ast=0.458327\cdots$, lies inside the stabilizer polytope and is therefore magic-free. Combining this result with general geometric properties of high-dimensional polytopes, we establish quantitative estimates for the Hilbert--Schmidt inradius and volume radius of the stabilizer polytope, and use them to characterize the typicality of magic in random induced states obtained by tracing out a $k$-dimensional subsystem from a $d\times k$-dimensional Haar-random pure state. We prove a sharp phase transition in the probability of such states having magic, whose transition dimension $k_\star$ is bounded between $Ω(d^2/\log^2d)$ and $\mathcal{O}(d^2)$. We further prove that the number of facets of the stabilizer polytope lies between $\exp[Ω(d^2/\log^2 d)]$ and $\exp[\mathcal{O}(d^2\log^2 d)]$, substantially improving upon the previous quasipolynomial lower bound and implying that any exact description of the magic-free region requires a doubly exponential number of linear inequalities in the number of qubits. Overall, our results show that the stabilizer polytope exhibits near-maximal geometric complexity allowed for a high-dimensional polytope with a certain number of vertices.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a Projected Riemannian Gradient Descent algorithm to compute the Bures-Wasserstein barycenter of quantum states represented by positive definite matrices. By introducing a novel, non-expansive projection based on eigenvalue clipping, the authors achieve dimension-independent linear convergence at the computationally desirable unit step size. This provides a significant algorithmic improvement over existing methods in quantum information geometry.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a highly efficient, theoretically robust numerical tool for averaging quantum states or covariance matrices, which is fundamental in quantum estimation and resource theories.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the computational challenge of finding the Bures-Wasserstein (BW) barycenter of an ensemble of positive definite matrices and solving related invariant matrix projection problems.

**Main result.** It proposes a Projected Riemannian Gradient Descent (RGD) algorithm that achieves dimension-independent linear convergence at unit step size, resolving a known theoretical dichotomy in the field.

**Method.** The core method is a Projected RGD algorithm that incorporates a novel, exact, non-expansive projection based on eigenvalue clipping to constrain iterates within a spectral interval.

**Model / system.** The analysis is conducted on the manifold of positive definite matrices (PD(d)), using the Bures-Wasserstein distance, which is relevant for quantum information tasks like Bayesian quantum tomography.

**Key observables.** Convergence rate, which is shown to be linear with a rate of $(1 - \kappa^{-3/2})$, and the condition number $\kappa = eta/\alpha$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The spectral bounds $\alpha$ and $eta$ define the constrained set $[\alpha I, eta I]$, and the step size is fixed at unity ($\eta=1$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The key assumption enabling the result is the novel Projection Lemma, which proves that eigenvalue clipping is the closed-form, non-expansive BW-metric projection onto the constrained set.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1(a) compares the convergence speed of unit-step vs. small-step RGD, showing the proposed method achieves convergence in $\approx 16$ iterations versus $\approx 3300$ iterations for small steps.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the scientific problem (BW barycenter/invariant projection), then introduces the Projected RGD method, proves the crucial Projection Lemma, and finally analyzes the convergence rate and its generalization to related submanifolds.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The computation of the Bures-Wasserstein (BW) barycenter of an ensemble of positive definite matrices arises throughout machine learning, optimal transport, and quantum information. Riemannian gradient descent (RGD) at unit step size -- the fixed-point iteration used in practice -- converges rapidly, yet existing analyses present a dichotomy: unit-step guarantees carry worst-case exponential dependence on the dimension, while dimension-independent guarantees require small step sizes that forfeit the empirical speed. We resolve this dichotomy, not by improving the guarantees for unit-step RGD, but by proposing a Projected RGD algorithm that achieves dimension-independent linear convergence at unit step size. The achieved rate, $(1 - κ^{-3/2})$, where $κ$ is the condition number of the ensemble, also polynomially improves on the best small-step guarantee ($κ^{3/2}$ versus $κ^{5/2}$ iteration complexity). The crux is a novel Projection Lemma: clipping the eigenvalues of a positive matrix to an interval $[α, β]$ is the closed-form, non-expansive (1-Lipschitz) BW-metric projection onto the set $\{S : αI \leq S \leq βI\}$ -- a statement which, unlike its known one-sided counterpart, does not follow from convexity. The projection is moreover free: it reuses an eigendecomposition the next iteration must perform in any case, so the projected and unprojected iterations cost the same per step. The same analysis covers the invariant matrix projection problem of Brahmachari et al. (2025), whose fixed-point algorithm we identify as unit-step RGD on a totally geodesic submanifold, thereby extending the dimension-independent guarantee to that setting verbatim.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Q-Edge, a novel quantum framework designed to overcome the exponential complexity barrier in simulating high-dimensional structured extreme dependence. It achieves this by mathematically exploiting inherent symmetries to map the vast dependence space onto a much smaller, compact orbit space. This results in a quantum simulation that requires significantly fewer qubits while maintaining full expressive power for complex, rare events.

**Why it may be interesting.** The technique of transferring symmetry into the data representation rather than constraining the quantum circuit is a powerful general principle for designing resource-efficient quantum algorithms for complex physical systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is the combinatorial complexity of simulating high-dimensional multivariate extremes, which limits current quantum simulation methods.

**Main result.** The authors introduce Q-Edge, a framework that uses symmetry to map the exponentially large dependence space onto a compact, symmetry-reduced orbit space, drastically lowering the required quantum resources.

**Method.** The approach involves exploiting the mathematical principle that symmetry allows for a lossless orbit-space representation, which is then used to guide an unconstrained quantum generative model.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on simulating structured extreme dependence (ESD) in high-dimensional random variables, treating the dependence structure via its angular distribution on a simplex.

**Key observables.** The number of reachable symmetry-constrained orbits ($	ext{OG}(	ext{QN})$) and the reduction in required qubits (e.g., 21 qubits down to 8 for a 30D problem).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionality ($D$), the resolution ($N$), and the specific symmetry group structure (e.g., related to Dirichlet distributions).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that the structured dependence admits a lossless orbit-space representation that can be utilized by quantum generative models.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 conceptually illustrates the flow: structured extremes $ightarrow$ orbit-space reduction $ightarrow$ reduced representation $ightarrow$ quantum model $ightarrow$ reconstruction.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from identifying the complexity bottleneck in high-dimensional simulation, deriving the mathematical framework for orbit-space reduction using symmetry principles (Burnside's Lemma), and finally proposing the Q-Edge quantum implementation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

High-dimensional simulation of multivariate extremes is fundamentally limited by the combinatorial complexity of dependence, often more than by the scarcity of extreme observations. We show that symmetry admits a lossless orbit-space representation that preserves structured extreme dependence while replacing an exponentially large dependence space with a compact set of symmetry classes. Based on this principle, we develop Q-Edge (Quantum Extreme Dependence Engine), a symmetry-reduced quantum framework that operates directly in orbit space, enabling scalable simulation and digital twins of structured extreme systems. By transferring symmetry into the data representation rather than the quantum circuit, Q-Edge allows unconstrained quantum generative models to exploit dramatically reduced state spaces. For a 30-dimensional problem, approximately 1.6 million angular states collapse to 256 orbit states, reducing the required quantum representation from about 21 qubits to 8. Our results establish a general computational principle for scalable quantum simulation of structured extreme dependence.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel algebraic framework to define abstract quantum data types, extending classical universal algebra techniques to the quantum domain. It formalizes the 'quantization' of data type specifications, providing a rigorous mathematical tool for quantum programming. This work is significant for building robust, abstract quantum algorithms independent of specific hardware implementations.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous mathematical formalism for handling data structures in quantum computation, which is crucial for developing hardware-agnostic quantum algorithms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To establish an algebraic foundation for data abstraction in quantum programming by defining and quantizing abstract data types.

**Main result.** The paper formally defines abstract quantum data types using universal algebra and proves that classical data type specifications can be soundly lifted to the quantum setting.

**Method.** The approach uses universal algebra to define quantum signatures and algebras, modeling quantum terms as quantum circuit formulas evaluated on Hilbert spaces.

**Model / system.** The model is an abstract quantum algebraic framework where data types are represented by quantum sorts, and operations are represented by unitary gates acting on tensor product Hilbert spaces.

**Key observables.** State overlaps, inner products, and the equality of quantum circuit evaluations (LHS = RHS) are used to verify algebraic identities.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The structure of the quantum gates (unitary operators) and the initial quantum states (pure states) are central.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes that quantum operations are unitary and that the focus is on pure states, though generalizations are mentioned.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the quantum algebraic structure, then defining quantum terms and equations, and finally proving the validity of structural preservation (embeddings, isomorphisms) by verifying key equations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this paper, we introduce a notion of abstract quantum data type within the framework of universal algebra. This notion provides an algebraic foundation for describing data abstraction in quantum programming. We formally define a quantisation of classical data types and show that their equational specifications can be soundly lifted to the quantum setting. Two standard quantisation methods for classical functions, namely the bit oracle and the phase oracle, arise as special cases of this general construction. We illustrate the framework with applications to quantum arrays and quantum error-correcting codes, showing how they can be understood through the lens of data-type quantisation. We further establish conditions under which quantisation preserves structural relationships and constructions of classical data types, including embeddings, isomorphisms, and products.

</details>

**Summary.** This work determines the classical communication cost needed to simulate quantum communication and Bell nonlocality for arbitrary dimensions. It finds that while qubits and qutrits can be simulated with finite classical bits (though the cost for qutrits is high), any system with dimension four or higher requires an infinite amount of classical communication for exact simulation.

**Why it may be interesting.** The finding that quantum correlations require infinite classical resources above a certain dimension is a fundamental statement about the non-classical nature of quantum information, relevant to understanding the resource theory of quantum communication.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the fundamental limits on simulating quantum communication and Bell nonlocality using only finite classical communication.

**Main result.** It establishes that for quantum dimensions $d_Q \geq 4$, no finite classical communication can simulate the quantum correlations, revealing a qualitative transition at $d_Q=4$.

**Method.** The authors compare quantum probability distributions (derived from quantum measurements) against classical probability distributions achievable through finite classical encoding and shared randomness.

**Model / system.** The study models quantum communication using states $ho$ of dimension $d_Q$ and local measurements $M$, and analyzes the simulation cost $C_{cl}(d_Q)$ required to match quantum statistics classically.

**Key observables.** The required classical communication dimension $d_C$, and the comparison between quantum and classical probability averages $p_Q$ vs $p_C$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Quantum dimension $d_Q$ (e.g., 2 for qubits, 3 for qutrits, $\geq 4$ for ququarts), and the required classical bits $d_C$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The simulation model assumes a single round of classical communication (prepare-and-measure model).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the setup for classical simulation (prepare-and-measure model) and show the comparison of quantum vs. classical averages, particularly highlighting the divergence for ququarts.

**Paper structure.** The paper first addresses the general simulation problem, solving it for qubits ($d_Q=2$) and constructing an explicit, large protocol for qutrits ($d_Q=3$). It then proves that for $d_Q \geq 4$, the simulation cost is infinite, marking a sharp transition.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A quantum system of any fixed dimension can be prepared in a continuum of states, yet it cannot be used to transmit an unlimited amount of classical information. Similarly, the correlations observed between measurement outcomes on separate parts of a shared quantum system can be stronger than classical correlations, but they cannot transmit information. These fundamental limitations suggest that the statistics observed from quantum communication and quantum correlations may admit a simulation using a finite amount of classical communication. This expectation is confirmed in the smallest nontrivial quantum dimension, with two classical bits being necessary and sufficient to exactly simulate qubit communication and all correlations between qubits. Despite significant efforts during the previous decades, this remained the only solved case. Here we resolve both problems for every quantum dimension. The solution reveals an unexpected qualitative transition starting at dimension four: no finite amount of classical communication can exactly simulate ququart communication nor all quantum correlations of two entangled ququarts, even with unlimited shared randomness. One might have expected this transition, if it existed, to appear already for qutrits. Instead, we construct an explicit protocol that exactly simulates qutrit communication using $357$ classical bits, and consequently, all correlations of two entangled qutrits.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the difficult problem of decoding native GKP quantum error-correcting codes by co-designing the code structure with its decoder. The authors leverage classical Low-Density Lattice Codes (LDLCs) to build quantum analogues (qLDLCs). They demonstrate that using specialized, fully analog message-passing decoders allows these codes to achieve error correction performance comparable to leading hybrid qubit-analog methods.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work directly addresses fault-tolerance in bosonic quantum computing platforms, providing a concrete, computationally tractable path forward for implementing high-performance quantum error correction.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is constructing and efficiently decoding native Gottesman-Kitaev-Preskill (GKP) codes, which encode qudits into infinite-dimensional bosonic modes.

**Main result.** The authors propose a co-design strategy using classical Low-Density Lattice Codes (LDLCs) to construct quantum versions (qLDLCs) whose performance approaches state-of-the-art hybrid decoders.

**Method.** They adapt classical message-passing decoding algorithms, originally developed for LDLCs, to the quantum lattice setting, requiring the co-design of the code structure and the decoder.

**Model / system.** The model involves GKP codes, which are bosonic quantum error-correcting codes based on mathematical lattices. The underlying mathematical structure is derived from classical LDLCs.

**Key observables.** Error rates, code properties (e.g., code distance, logical dimension), and the convergence of message-passing variances.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionality reduction factor and the convergence parameter $\alpha$ governing the asymptotic behavior of the iterative decoder.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach assumes that efficient decoding requirements can dictate the structure of the quantum error-correcting code, bypassing the direct, hard lattice problems.

**Figures summary.** Not specified by the notes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the difficulty of native GKP decoding to proposing a co-design strategy based on classical LDLCs, detailing the use of analog message-passing decoders, and finally presenting results showing comparable performance to existing state-of-the-art codes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Gottesman-Kitaev-Preskill (GKP) codes provide a family of promising schemes for encoding discrete quantum information (qudits) into infinite-dimensional bosonic modes based on mathematical lattices. While such codes, when concatenated with discrete-variable codes, are relatively well studied, the construction and decoding of native GKP codes has largely remained open due to the computationally hard problems encountered. To address this challenge, we advocate a strategy of co-designing the decoder and the quantum error-correcting code itself by constructing lattices for which decoding is feasible: The requirement of efficient decoding effectively determines the quantum error-correcting code. This construction is built on classical low-density lattice codes (LDLCs), a lattice analogue of low-density parity-check codes, here lifted to families of GKP codes. Concretely, we introduce quantum versions of classical, randomly constructed LDLCs. We show that after suitable dimensionality reduction these codes have code properties comparable to or better than concatenated GKP-surface codes of equal number of modes. However, the GKP-LDLCs constructed here do not have a strictly sparse parity check matrix, which motivates our study of the performance of natively analog message-passing decoders originally developed for LDLCs when applied to concatenated GKP-LDPC codes. We show that the fully analog, linear-time decoder achieves performances close to state-of-the-art hybrid qubit-analog decoders. To facilitate future research on the structure and performance of general GKP codes, the relevant source code will be released in open-source Julia packages LatticeDecoder.jl and SymplecticGKP.jl.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Quantum Quasi-Monte Carlo (QqMC), a method that merges low-discrepancy point sets with quantum amplitude estimation for numerical integration. It proves that while no asymptotic speedup over classical qMC is achieved, a measurable 'pre-asymptotic advantage' exists in finite query regimes. This makes the technique relevant for near-term quantum hardware where the number of available queries is limited.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on numerical integration, the core concept of achieving computational speedups by exploiting finite-size quantum resources (the pre-asymptotic regime) is highly relevant to quantum algorithms for physical simulations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the computational bottleneck of numerical integration using Monte Carlo methods, specifically aiming to improve upon classical Quasi-Monte Carlo (qMC) techniques.

**Main result.** It demonstrates a 'pre-asymptotic quantum advantage' for Quantum Quasi-Monte Carlo (QqMC) by showing that significantly fewer quantum queries can achieve the same accuracy as classical methods in finite regimes.

**Method.** The approach combines low-discrepancy nets (Sobol' sequences) with Quantum Amplitude Estimation (QAE) to estimate high-dimensional integrals.

**Model / system.** The model is the numerical integration of a function $f$ over the unit hypercube $[0,1)^d$. The quantum computation utilizes the coherent preparation of a superposition over Sobol' points.

**Key observables.** The primary observables are the error bounds ($\epsilon_{qMC}$ vs $\epsilon_{QqMC}$) and the required number of function queries ($T$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Key parameters include the dimension $d$, the resolution parameters $q$ and $Q$, and the query budget $T$. The advantage window is controlled by these parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The advantage is strictly pre-asymptotic, not asymptotic. The analysis relies on the error separating into a discretization error (from the finite net) and a quantum estimation error.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 provides an overview contrasting classical qMC vs QqMC error bounds, while later figures compare the quality parameter $	ilde{t}(q)$ against the true finite-resolution value $t_{	ext{finite}}$ across various dimensions.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from classical limitations (MC/qMC) to the novel QqMC algorithm, deriving the error decomposition, establishing the pre-asymptotic advantage window, and concluding with asymptotic analysis of the required query threshold.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Numerical integration with Monte Carlo methods is a central computational task in many scientific and industrial applications, including financial derivative pricing and risk management. Classical Monte Carlo algorithms are computationally demanding: achieving an accuracy $ε$ typically requires a number of function evaluations scaling as $O(1/ε^2)$. Quantum-accelerated Monte Carlo methods based on quantum amplitude estimation can in principle quadratically improve this dependence. However, \textit{quasi}-Monte Carlo methods have not been explored in the quantum context. In this work, we introduce a quantum quasi-Monte Carlo algorithm that combines low-discrepancy nets with quantum amplitude estimation. The proposed method prepares the quasi-random point set coherently in superposition. The method does not yield an asymptotic improvement over classical quasi-Monte Carlo, since the total error separates into a discretization error, determined by the finite net, and a quantum estimation error. Instead, we explore a pre-asymptotic advantage window: for a target accuracy that would classically require $2^q$ low discrepancy points, one can prepare a higher-resolution net of size $2^Q$, with $Q>q$, in superposition and reach the same accuracy using significantly fewer function queries. This window can be controlled by tuning the circuit resolution and amplitude-estimation parameters, making the approach relevant for practical regimes where the number of queries is finite rather than asymptotically large.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper establishes a quantum query lower bound for finding a Tarski fixed point on a high-dimensional grid. It introduces the novel Tree–Filtration Adversary Method, which provides a combinatorial tool for proving quantum complexity lower bounds. The main result shows the complexity is $\Omega(k \log n)$, advancing the understanding of quantum computation in order theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the core topic is algorithmic complexity, the methodology—developing a general framework for quantum lower bounds based on tree structures—is highly relevant to understanding the computational limits of quantum algorithms in various physical models.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper determines the quantum query complexity required to find a Tarski fixed point of a monotone function defined on a high-dimensional grid lattice.

**Main result.** The quantum lower bound for this problem is established as $\Omega(k \log n)$, which improves upon previous classical lower bounds in certain regimes.

**Method.** The authors develop and apply the Tree–Filtration Adversary Method, which provides a combinatorial interpretation of the nonnegative spectral adversary method for deriving quantum query lower bounds.

**Model / system.** The problem is modeled in the value-oracle setting, where the input space is the $k$-dimensional grid lattice $[n]^k$, and the function $f$ is assumed to be monotone.

**Key observables.** Quantum query complexity $Q_\epsilon(	ext{Tarski}(n, k))$, and the spectral norm of the constructed adversary matrix $\Gamma$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensions $n$ and $k$ of the grid lattice, and the error parameter $\epsilon$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on constructing a suitable tree structure for the hard instances and defining a random walk on this tree that satisfies specific anti-concentration and geometric-indistinguishability properties.

**Figures summary.** Not specified.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the problem and the general framework of the adversary method. It then details the construction of the Tree–Filtration Adversary Method using a top-down random walk on a filtration tree. Finally, it applies this method to derive the $\Omega(k \log n)$ lower bound for the Tarski fixed point problem.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Knaster-Tarski fixed-point theorem states that every monotone function over a complete lattice has a fixed point. Beyond its fundamental role in order theory, the theorem and its algorithmic variants have found broad applications in areas such as economics, game theory, and programming languages. While the query complexity of finding a Tarski fixed point has been extensively studied in classical models, comparatively little is known in the quantum setting.   We prove an $Ω(k\log n)$ quantum query lower bound for finding a fixed point of a monotone function on $[n]^k$, using the nonnegative spectral adversary method. In the two extremal regimes $n = 2$ and $k = 1$, our quantum lower bound matches the previous classical lower bounds $Ω(k)$ and $Ω(\log n)$, respectively. For $n, k\geq 2$, our bound improves the best previous classical lower bound when $n < k$ and is within a factor of $\log n / \log k$ compared to the known classical lower bound when $n \geq k$. To construct the adversary matrix, we develop the Tree--Filtration Adversary Method. Besides yielding our lower bound, the method offers a more transparent combinatorial interpretation of the nonnegative spectral adversary method. When the hard instances of a problem admit a tree-like organization and suggest an intuition analogous to classical decision-tree lower bounds, our method provide a promising approach to establishing quantum complexity lower bounds.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes the fundamental limits of learning in tunable mechanical networks by incorporating Maxwell-Betti reciprocity. It proves that this physical symmetry restricts the network's reachable response space, creating a quantifiable error floor that no passive training rule can overcome. The findings are crucial for designing physical machine learning hardware by setting realistic performance expectations.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of an inherent, physically imposed error floor in a learning system, derived from fundamental symmetries like reciprocity, provides a powerful theoretical constraint applicable to any physical realization of a neural network.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper determines the true capacity of tunable mechanical networks by accounting for physical symmetries, specifically Maxwell-Betti reciprocity, which constrains the reachable response space.

**Main result.** Reciprocity forces the reachable set into a subspace of codimension $p(p-1)/2$, establishing a computable error floor that limits the learning capability of passive networks.

**Method.** The authors derive theoretical bounds using linear algebra and analyze the system's response block using the Jacobian, comparing theoretical constraints with numerical simulation results.

**Model / system.** The system is a passive, linear elastic mechanical network with tunable stiffnesses, modeled by a bond force law. The analysis focuses on the response block $R(	heta)$ derived from the compliance matrix.

**Key observables.** The codimension of the reachable set, the error floor (related to the antisymmetric part of the target), and the rank of the Jacobian matrix.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The overlap $p$ (degrees of freedom both driven and read out), the number of bonds, and the stiffness parameters $	heta$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a fixed graph with a finite set of tunable stiffnesses, and the core results rely on the validity of Maxwell-Betti reciprocity for passive systems.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the mechanical setup, showing how reciprocity constrains the response block to a lower-dimensional subspace, quantified by $p(p-1)/2$ lost dimensions.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical model, derives the theoretical constraint imposed by reciprocity, calculates the resulting error floor, and then tests these theoretical limits using numerical simulations and optimization routines.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Tunable mechanical networks are being developed as materials that learn in place. Capacity is estimated by counting tunable parameters against target constraints, ignoring Maxwell-Betti reciprocity, the symmetry every passive, linear elastic network obeys by construction. When p degrees of freedom are both driven and read out, every reachable response block lies in a subspace of codimension p(p-1)/2, whatever the size, topology and stiffnesses; at full overlap nearly half the target space is unreachable. The consequence for training is a number: any learning rule leaves an error at least the norm of the target's antisymmetric part on the shared degrees of freedom, computable before training, and positive definiteness adds an orthogonal second term. A second-order optimiser with the exact Jacobian reaches that floor within 1% in 143 of 144 runs, a bond-local contrastive rule within 0.1% in 22 of 24, and odd couplings restore the lost directions at the price of an external torque source: the network becomes active. Prescribed-displacement drives obey a companion law we prove. The symmetry is classical; its consequence on a fixed graph with finitely many tunable stiffnesses at partial overlap is new. For a published robotic metamaterial it shows that no symmetric positive-definite stiffness matrix meets both targets in the linear model their deposited data integrate.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the critical challenge of fault-tolerant quantum computation in distributed systems where communication links are noisy. The authors develop advanced protocols that adapt error correction to resource constraints, notably showing significant reductions in required quantum resources by integrating decoding steps. This work provides a pathway toward realizing large-scale quantum computers by optimizing error handling across physically separated modules.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the engineering bottleneck of scaling quantum computation by providing concrete, resource-aware protocols for error correction in physically realistic, distributed hardware architectures.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is developing distributed quantum error correction (DQEC) and logical gates for large-scale quantum computers where module interaction is limited to significantly noisy shared Bell pairs.

**Main result.** The authors derive resource-adaptive strategies, showing that integrating decoding halves the required distillation code distance and significantly reducing the required number of Bell pairs compared to context-free methods.

**Method.** The work extends fault tolerance by construction to noisy distributed settings, synthesizing efficient circuits for distributed stabilizer measurements using techniques like ZX-calculus and analyzing resource trade-offs.

**Model / system.** The model involves distributed quantum computing architectures composed of multiple QPU modules connected by long-range interconnects that introduce high noise into shared Bell pairs. The analysis focuses on surface and color codes for memory and lattice surgery.

**Key observables.** Logical error rates, required distillation code distance, and the number of necessary Bell pairs.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interconnect noise strength ($p_{	ext{Bell}}$) relative to on-chip gate noise ($p$), and resource constraints like Bell pair generation rate.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the process of Bell pair generation and transduction is a black box with a higher error rate than on-chip operations, and it focuses on modifying on-chip circuits given pre-distilled pairs.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the general noise model showing QPU modules and interconnects, and one figure depicts logical state teleportation using lattice surgery.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the general framework for handling noisy interconnects, then developing synthesis techniques for distributed stabilizer measurements, and finally benchmarking these circuits on specific codes like the surface code to quantify resource savings.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Distributed architectures have been proposed as a pathway to large-scale quantum computers. Combined with the need for fault-tolerance, such architectures require distributed quantum error correction (DQEC) and distributed logical gates. An important challenge is how to realize DQEC primitives in the setting where interaction between modules is restricted to shared Bell pairs that are significantly noisier than on-chip operations. We extend the framework known as fault tolerance by construction to this setting, deriving different strategies for handling the additional noise. Through fault-improvement we recover conventional entanglement distillation, and also find more dynamical protocols that enable space-time trade-offs. We show that integrated decoding halves the required distillation code distance compared to entanglement distillation implemented using separate decoding, thus requiring significantly fewer Bell pairs. As a main focus of the work, we synthesize efficient circuits for an important primitive in distributed fault tolerance: distributed stabilizer measurements. These circuits can be adapted to resource constraints, e.g. on the Bell pair generation rate or the space available for on-chip auxiliary qubits. Noting that full local fault-tolerance is not always needed to preserve the correct scaling of logical error rates, we further optimize the circuits depending on the surrounding context. We consider in particular the surface code and the color code, both as distributed memories and in the case of lattice surgery across separate modules. Here, robustness to certain hook and readout errors reduces the number of required Bell pairs even further, compared to the context-free setting. We numerically benchmark the resulting implementations under circuit level noise with additional interconnect noise.

</details>

**Authors:** Shutao Liu, Shuai Yang, Jie Lou, Yan Chen  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.03465v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

**Summary.** This paper theoretically investigates line defects embedded in a Deconfined Quantum Critical Point (DQCP) bulk using fuzzy-sphere regularization. By analyzing the defect operator spectrum across the renormalization group flow, the authors characterize the universal properties of the defect's conformal fixed point. The results confirm the existence of a stable defect critical phase, demonstrating the robustness of this physics across different underlying symmetries (SO(5) and O(4)).

**Why it may be interesting.** The study provides a rigorous theoretical framework for understanding how topological defects modify critical behavior in strongly correlated quantum matter, which is relevant to understanding boundary effects in quantum materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the universal properties of one-dimensional line defects embedded within a two-dimensional Deconfined Quantum Critical Point (DQCP) bulk.

**Main result.** The numerical analysis establishes the first characterization of line-defect conformal data at DQCP, suggesting the existence of a stable, non-trivial defect conformal fixed point.

**Method.** The authors employed fuzzy-sphere regularization and numerical exact diagonalization to analyze the Renormalization Group (RG) flow of the defect operator spectrum.

**Model / system.** The system models a (0+1)-dimensional pinning-field defect coupled to a (2+1)-dimensional DQCP bulk, which is related to an SO(5) symmetry. The analysis is extended to an O(4) symmetry case to test universality.

**Key observables.** Defect operator spectrum, defect g-function, scaling dimensions of defect-changing/creating operators, and correlation functions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The defect coupling strength ($h_d$), which is varied from 0 (bulk fixed point) to $\infty$ (defect fixed point).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the fuzzy-sphere regularization framework, and the DQCP realizations are assumed to be weakly first-order.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the evolution of excitation states along the RG flow, show finite-size extrapolation of scaling dimensions, and display the resulting defect conformal multiplets.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the RG flow from the bulk fixed point to the defect fixed point for the SO(5) DQCP, extracts universal conformal data, and then confirms the robustness of these findings by repeating the analysis for an O(4) symmetric DQCP.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The interplay between bulk critical fluctuations and nontrivial topology can enrich defect physics and give rise to novel defect universality classes. Understanding the fate of such defects therefore constitutes an important open problem. In this work, we studied a particularly simple setting: a (0+1)-dimensional pinning-field defect coupled to a (2+1)-dimensional deconfined quantum critical bulk. Using the fuzzy-sphere regularization, we numerically investigated the defect operator spectrum and extracted several universal quantities characterizing the defect conformal fixed point, including the scaling dimensions of defect-changing(creating) operators and the defect \(g\)-function. These results establish the first numerical characterization of line-defect conformal data at deconfined quantum criticality and may stimulate further investigations of defect critical phenomena in topological quantum critical matter.

</details>
