**Summary.** This theoretical paper determines the optimal, or 'stealthiest,' arrangement of point particles in high-dimensional Euclidean spaces. It proves that the $E_8$ and Leech lattices achieve the maximum possible stealthiness in dimensions 8 and 24, respectively. The work employs advanced tools from geometric measure theory and lattice theory to establish sharp bounds on particle arrangement order.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of 'stealthiness' relates to the structure factor and diffraction patterns, which are fundamental tools in characterizing the order and correlations in many-body quantum systems, particularly in condensed matter physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper seeks to determine the 'stealthiest' arrangement of identical classical point particles in Euclidean space ($\mathbb{R}^d$) given a fixed particle density.

**Main result.** The $E_8$ lattice is the unique stealthiest process in $\mathbb{R}^8$, and the Leech lattice is the unique stealthiest process in $\mathbb{R}^{24}$.

**Method.** The analysis uses the framework of isometry-invariant point processes and relies on establishing sharp upper bounds for the stealthiness parameter using auxiliary functions derived from sphere packing theory.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled by a stationary, locally square-integrable point process ($\eta$) representing particle configurations in $\mathbb{R}^d$. The analysis extends to sphere packing problems in dimensions 8, 20, and 24.

**Key observables.** Stealthiness ($	ext{stealth}(\eta)$), measured by the gap between the origin and the nearest Bragg peak in the diffraction pattern, and the reduced covariance measure ($\kappa_\eta$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Particle density (fixed at 1 in the main theorems), and the dimension $d$ (specifically $d=8$ and $d=24$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the point process is stationary and locally square-integrable. The conclusion that the optimal configuration must be a lattice is noted as a potential limitation.

**Paper structure.** The paper defines stealthiness using point processes, reviews the pair correlation formalism, proves the main theorem establishing the $E_8$ and Leech lattices as optimizers, and concludes by constructing a stealthier periodic configuration in $\mathbb{R}^{20}$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that the $E_8$ and Leech lattices generate the stealthiest isometry-invariant, locally square-integrable point processes of intensity $1$ in $\mathbb{R}^8$ and $\mathbb{R}^{24}$, respectively, and that they are the unique stealthiest processes. We also apply the proof technique to sphere packing and energy minimization, and we construct a $20$-dimensional periodic configuration that is stealthier than any known $20$-dimensional lattice with the same particle density. This periodic configuration is a formal dual of Vardy's $20$-dimensional sphere packing.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work tackles the paradox of irreversibility in unitary quantum systems by showing that the apparent loss of entropy is due to the failure of hidden microscopic information to organize into restorative macroscopic currents. The authors develop exact mathematical tools to separate entropy generation from these return currents, providing a deep, quantum-mechanical understanding of the Second Law.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous, quantum mechanical framework for understanding the Second Law of Thermodynamics in isolated systems by quantifying the role of coherence and hidden degrees of freedom in macroscopic irreversibility.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the origin of thermodynamic irreversibility in an isolated quantum system whose underlying dynamics are unitary and information-preserving.

**Main result.** Irreversibility is shown not to be the loss of microscopic information, but rather the failure of that hidden information to organize the quantum currents necessary to restore macroscopic order.

**Method.** The authors derive an exact entropy rate by splitting the state into a macroscopic 'record' part and an unresolved 'hidden' part, analyzing the resulting currents via spectral decomposition.

**Model / system.** The analysis considers an isolated quantum system evolving unitarily, focusing on the dynamics observed through a fixed projective record. The theory is tested using models like hard-core and Bose-Hubbard chains.

**Key observables.** Thermodynamic Entropy Rate ($\dot{S}_R$), Observational Deficit ($I_R$), and 'hidden currents' ($J_{RR'}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis is restricted to finite observation windows and specific projective records; the thermodynamic limit is not fully established.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to the specified records and observation windows; heat and work require additional boundary specifications.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds by first defining the information deficit, then deriving the exact entropy rate by separating spreading from return currents, and finally comparing the finite-step evolution to the orbit-average evolution to establish the physical meaning of irreversibility.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Microscopic unitary dynamics preserves all fine information, yet an isolated finite system can show a robust thermodynamic window in which the entropy associated with a restricted record rises. We ask why information hidden from the current record usually fails to rebuild a low-entropy macrostate. For a fixed projective record, every finite step separates exactly into the evolution predicted from the record alone and an exact correction carried by unresolved microscopic structure. The record-only contribution starts only at second order in time, so all instantaneous change of the record comes from hidden currents between macrostates. We derive the exact entropy rate, separate entropy-spreading from return-oriented currents, and resolve those currents into energy-gap amplitudes. Transitions with the same gap add coherently, revealing how the Hamiltonian and the microscopic state organize hidden information for return. In interacting mixing dynamics the current power is spread over many frequencies; free and deliberately commensurate controls progressively concentrate it, and the engineered dynamics reconstructs a low-entropy macrostate. An independent distribution-level test separates hidden dynamical activity from finite-step return, and an exact classical measure-preserving counterpart shows which parts of the construction are not uniquely quantum. Within the specified record and observation window, irreversibility is therefore not loss of microscopic information, but the failure of that information to organize currents that restore macroscopic order.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a specific, resource-efficient quantum circuit ensemble that approximates Haar randomness by achieving $\epsilon$-approximate projective 2- and 3-designs. By exploiting the commuting structure of the phase gates, the authors demonstrate that high-fidelity random states can be generated in remarkably shallow depth. This has significant implications for quantum metrology and quantum algorithm design by providing a blueprint for resource-constrained quantum simulation.

**Why it may be interesting.** The demonstration that precise Haar-like statistics can emerge from sparse, structured dynamics with remarkably low quantum resources is highly relevant for designing efficient quantum algorithms and characterization protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is generating quantum random states that approximate Haar randomness with high precision (relative error) while minimizing the required quantum resources, especially achieving constant circuit depth.

**Main result.** The authors prove that a specific sparse commuting circuit ensemble can generate $\epsilon$-approximate projective 2- and 3-designs in relative error, achieving constant depth (depth seven) or $O(\log(n/\epsilon))$ depth without ancillas.

**Method.** The analysis relies on bounding the relative error of quantum moments ($\mathcal{M}^{(k)}$) by exploiting the commuting structure of the phase gates and employing advanced combinatorial techniques like block decomposition and the bishop method.

**Model / system.** The model is an ensemble of random unitaries acting on $n$ qubits, structured as a product of a sparse Clifford phase layer, a sparse controlled-Z layer, and independent single-qubit Clifford gates.

**Key observables.** Relative error ($\epsilon$), projective $k$-design property (for $k=2, 3$), and quantum circuit depth.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Sparsity parameter ($\gamma$), relative error ($\epsilon$), and system size ($n$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the relative error can be controlled by analyzing the operator-norm distance to the Haar moment, and the results are highly dependent on the specific commuting structure of the phase circuits.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1(a) schematically compares the shallow phase-circuit construction to generic shallow random circuits, while Figure 1(b) illustrates how moment analysis makes the deviation from Haar randomness transparent.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the general framework of relative error bounds, then analyzing the second and third moments using local Clifford twirling to suppress errors, and finally employing advanced combinatorial arguments (like the bishop method) to rigorously prove the vanishing error for the third moment.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Random quantum objects are powerful resources for quantum information processing, yet exact Haar randomness is costly and typically unnecessary. We introduce an explicit sparse commuting circuit ensemble on $n$ qubits that reproduces low-order Haar moments in the stringent relative-error sense. The circuit consists of a sparse Clifford phase layer followed by independent single-qubit Clifford gates. Acting on a simple product state, the resulting ensemble forms $ε$-approximate projective $2$- and $3$-designs in relative error, with the required logarithmic interaction degree being asymptotically optimal within this circuit family. It admits an ancilla-free implementation of quantum depth $O(\log(n/ε))$ on an all-to-all architecture, as well as an adaptive constant-depth implementation---in fact, depth seven---using $O(n\log(n/ε))$ ancilla qubits. Departing from existing shallow-design paradigms, our analysis exploits the intrinsic moment structure of commuting phase circuits; at third order, this requires a new block decomposition and combinatorial analysis that also suggests a route toward higher-order shallow designs. Our results show that precise Haar-like statistics can emerge from sparse commuting dynamics with remarkably low quantum resources, with applications to randomized characterization, quantum metrology, quantum algorithms, and many-body physics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proves that finding the minimum CNOT circuit to realize a target quantum transformation is NP-hard in the most basic 'vanilla' setting. The authors achieve this by reducing the Hamiltonian path problem on grid graphs to the CNOT synthesis problem. This result establishes a fundamental computational complexity limit for quantum circuit design.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work establishes a fundamental computational barrier (NP-hardness) for a core primitive of quantum computation—the exact synthesis of gates—even in the simplest possible connectivity model.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the computational complexity of finding the minimum number of CNOT gates required to implement a specified invertible linear transformation (parity matrix) in the most basic, or 'vanilla,' setting.

**Main result.** The decision version of this vanilla exact CNOT synthesis problem is proven to be NP-complete, and consequently, the optimization version is NP-hard.

**Method.** The proof establishes the hardness via a polynomial-time reduction from the known NP-complete Hamiltonian-path problem on grid graphs.

**Model / system.** The model is quantum computation using CNOT gates to realize transformations over $\mathbb{F}_2$. The 'vanilla' setting assumes all-to-all connectivity, identity input, and no ancillas, making the result fundamental.

**Key observables.** The minimum number of CNOT gates required to achieve the target parity matrix $A \in GL(n, 2)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The size of the system ($n$ qubits) and the target transformation matrix $A$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The proof overcomes the challenge that CNOT synthesis only constrains the final parity matrix by introducing extra 'recorder qubits' to enforce intermediate path visits.

**Figures summary.** The notes mention Table 1 summarizing hardness results for various synthesis models, and Figure 1 illustrating a grid graph and an example Hamiltonian path.

**Paper structure.** The paper first defines the vanilla exact CNOT synthesis problem, then details the reduction from the Grid-HP problem, utilizing isometric embedding into a hypercube and recorder qubits to enforce path constraints, and finally discusses implications for related problems like phase polynomial synthesis.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Exact CNOT synthesis asks for a minimum-size CNOT circuit implementing an invertible linear transformation. Although several related synthesis models have been shown to be computationally hard, their hardness proofs rely on additional structure such as restricted qubit connectivity, encoded inputs, or unrestricted intermediate variables. The complexity of the most basic setting---identity input, a fixed number of labelled qubits, no ancillas, and all-to-all CNOT connectivity---had remained unresolved. In this work, we prove that the decision version of this vanilla exact CNOT synthesis problem is NP-complete, and consequently that its optimization version is NP-hard. Our proof gives a polynomial-time reduction from the Hamiltonian-path problem on grid graphs in two steps. First, we isometrically embed the grid graph into a hypercube via a unary encoding map. We then encode this hypercube Hamiltonian path problem into vanilla exact CNOT synthesis. The main challenge is that CNOT synthesis specifies only the final parity matrix and cannot directly enforce the intermediate vertex visits required by a Hamiltonian path. To overcome this difficulty, we introduce extra recorder qubits that encode the required intermediate vertex visits into the final transformation, forcing any CNOT circuit implementation to realize the intended path structure. Beyond CNOT synthesis, our result directly implies hardness for several related problems, including the shortest word problem over $\mathrm{GL}(n,2)$, distance computation on Cayley graphs over $\mathrm{GL}(n,2)$, minimization of sequential XOR programs, and exact synthesis of phase polynomial circuits.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the geometric properties of the unitary group PU(D) using specialized 'cliff metrics' to study quantum circuit complexity. It derives a novel lower bound for the infinite-cliff diameter, showing it scales with an exponent of $4/3$. This result is significant because it disproves an existing conjecture (Brown's exponent-one conjecture) and provides a concrete lower bound for quantum circuit depth.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a significant theoretical lower bound on the complexity of quantum circuits, directly impacting the understanding of quantum computational limits by improving upon previous conjectures.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to establish lower bounds for the diameter of the unitary group PU(D) under various metrics, specifically comparing the 'infinite-cliff' distance to other bounds.

**Main result.** It proves that the infinite-cliff diameter has a lower bound scaling of order $D^{4/3}M^{2/3}$, which disproves Brown's exponent-one conjecture and yields a corresponding lower bound for fixed-error quantum circuits.

**Method.** The analysis employs advanced geometric techniques, including small-ball probability estimates on the Haar measure, Jacobi field comparisons, and Gaussian transfer techniques.

**Model / system.** The system is the unitary group PU(D), representing quantum evolution, analyzed using right-invariant cliff metrics ($d_Q$) and the infinite-cliff distance ($d_\infty$). The complexity is related to $n$ qubits, where $D=2n$.

**Key observables.** The normalized Haar measure of small balls ($\mu_D(B_{Q_D}([I],x\sqrt{Q_D}))$) and the diameter of the space ($	ext{diam}(	ext{PU}(D), d_\infty)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The penalty parameter $Q$, the dimension $D=2n$, and the subspace dimension $M=3n+9inom{n}{2}$. The analysis is restricted to the window $M \ll Q \ll D^{8/3}M^{-4/3}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The results hold outside an $e^{-\Omega(D^2)}$ exceptional set, and the derivation relies on specific asymptotic conditions on the parameters $Q$ and $M$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing small-ball estimates for the finite-cliff metric, then transferring these estimates to the infinite-cliff distance using comparison techniques, culminating in the derivation of the lower bound for the diameter that contradicts a known conjecture.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Let $D=2^n$ and $M=3n+9\binom n2$. We study the right-invariant one-step-cliff metric $d_Q$ on $\operatorname{PU}(D)$, with unit penalty on Pauli weights one and two and penalty $Q$ on all higher weights. If $M/Q_D\to0$ and $MQ_D^{3/4}/D^2\to0$, then, for every fixed $0<x<π/\sqrt3$, \[   μ_D\bigl(B_{Q_D}([I],x\sqrt{Q_D})\bigr)\le e^{-c_xD^2}. \] Here $μ_D$ is normalized Haar measure. Thus the Haar-typical distance from the identity and the diameter are both asymptotic to $(π/\sqrt3)\sqrt{Q_D}$ throughout the window $M\ll Q_D\ll D^{8/3}M^{-4/3}$. Choosing $Q_D=κD^{8/3}M^{-4/3}$ with sufficiently small fixed $κ>0$ yields a Haar-typical lower bound of order $D^{4/3}M^{-2/3}$ for the corresponding infinite-cliff Carnot--Carathéodory distance, outside an $e^{-Ω(D^2)}$ exceptional set. The infinite-cliff diameter therefore has exponential lower rate at least $4/3$, disproving Brown's exponent-one conjecture. The same estimate gives a fixed-error no-ancilla two-qubit circuit lower bound of the same order.

</details>

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops the concept of the Projected Ensemble (PE) to probe topological order in FQH states beyond standard entanglement spectra. By analyzing states conditioned on measurement outcomes, the authors reveal a hidden, measurement-dependent hierarchy in the entanglement structure. This provides a sharp, new diagnostic tool confirming topological order even in realistic Coulomb-interacting systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of 'unravelling' entanglement via measurement outcomes provides a powerful, non-equilibrium probe into the topological order of strongly correlated electron systems, offering diagnostics beyond standard ground-state entanglement measures.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To uncover a finer universal structure within the entanglement spectra of fractional quantum Hall (FQH) states, going beyond what the standard entanglement spectrum can detect.

**Main result.** The projected ensemble (PE) exhibits a hidden hierarchy, leading to a measurement-resolved Li-Haldane conjecture where conditional subspaces are governed by CFT counting.

**Method.** The study analyzes the Projected Ensemble (PE), defined by conditioning quantum states on measurement outcomes of a subsystem's complement, which unravels the standard Reduced Density Matrix (RDM).

**Model / system.** The focus is on Fractional Quantum Hall (FQH) states, specifically the non-Abelian Moore-Read state and the $
u=5/2$ Coulomb ground state, analyzed on a sphere bipartitioned into two subsystems.

**Key observables.** Entanglement spectra, Projected Ensemble (PE), conditional reduced density matrices (CRDM), and the rank of the conditional subspaces.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Filling factor ($
u$), measurement outcomes ($n_B$), and the topological sector (e.g., $\Delta_{LA}=2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the assumption that the relevant information for the rank is encoded in a subset of measured degrees of freedom, and that the structure persists under coherent deformations (rank rigidity).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the PE structure, showing rank collapse upon measurement conditioning, and compare the geometric organization of conditional subspaces between the idealized Moore-Read model and the realistic Coulomb ground state.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the limitations of the standard entanglement spectrum to introducing the PE, formulating the measurement-resolved conjecture, demonstrating rank collapse, and finally showing that this structure persists for realistic Coulomb interactions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The entanglement spectra of fractional quantum Hall states contain universal fingerprints of their underlying topological order, as posited by the Li-Haldane conjecture. In this work, we uncover a finer universal structure within the entanglement spectra unravelled by projective measurements. Concretely, we study the projected ensemble of fractional quantum Hall states, defined as the collection of quantum states on a subsystem conditioned on measurement outcomes of its complement. We find that this ensemble exhibits a hidden hierarchy inside the Li-Haldane edge manifold: by conditioning on measurement outcomes, the entanglement spectrum's support is split into measurement-dependent sectors whose ranks we demonstrate are fixed by conformal field theory counting, an observation we dub the measurement-resolved Li-Haldane conjecture. For the non-Abelian Moore-Read state, this hierarchy is particularly rich: each parity-resolved edge manifold contains internal subspaces whose dimensions reproduce the conformal field theory counting of the opposite-parity sector. This structure persists even in realistic Coulomb-interacting ground states, establishing the projected ensemble as a sharp new probe of topological order beyond what the entanglement spectrum alone can detect.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports on the coherent coupling of excitons from $	ext{WS}_2$ and quasi-2D perovskites mediated by a cavity photon mode across a micrometer gap. By combining advanced optical measurements with coupled oscillator theory, the authors confirm the formation of hybrid polaritons. This work establishes a platform for engineering novel light-matter functionalities in 2D materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to AMO physics and quantum optics as it explores engineered light-matter interactions (polaritons) in solid-state systems, demonstrating coherent coupling over distances that typically suppress such interactions.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to demonstrate and characterize the coherent coupling between excitons from two distinct, spatially separated 2D materials ($	ext{WS}_2$ and quasi-2D perovskites) mediated by a single cavity photon mode.

**Main result.** The study successfully demonstrated the formation of three polariton branches, confirming coherent, photon-mediated interaction between the two excitonic systems over a micrometer separation distance.

**Method.** The work combines experimental measurements (white-light reflectivity and photoluminescence) with theoretical modeling using coupled harmonic oscillator and Wannier-Hopfield frameworks.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a hybrid heterostructure comprising a $	ext{WS}_2$ monolayer and a quasi-2D halide perovskite ($	ext{HaP}$) placed within an open optical microcavity. The system dynamics are modeled using coupled oscillator Hamiltonians.

**Key observables.** Polariton energy branches, coupling strengths ($g$), Hopfield coefficients, white-light reflectivity spectra, and photoluminescence (PL) intensity.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Separation distance ($\sim 1.5 \ \mu	ext{m}$), coupling strengths ($g_{	ext{WS}_2} \approx 16-20 	ext{ meV}$, $g_{	ext{HaP}} \approx 36 	ext{ meV}$), and cavity detuning.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on treating the system as a coupled set of harmonic oscillators and utilizes approximations like the Born-Markov approximation for scattering rates.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the physical setup involving DBR mirrors and transferred 2D materials, showing cavity tuning series data for the hybridized polariton and PL spectra for pure and mixed systems.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical setup and initial single-material coupling confirmations to analyzing the coupled three-oscillator Hamiltonian, and finally detailing the measurement of tunable polariton characteristics and scattering rates.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The coherent coupling of cavity-confined photons and excitonic matter resonances leads to the formation of cavity polaritons, hybrid light-matter quasi-particles. If multiple exciton resonances couple to the same photonic mode, the resulting polariton constitutes a coherent interaction between matter resonances that can be spatially separated without any direct electronic coupling. In this work, we demonstrate the formation of such a coherent coupling at room temperature using an open optical cavity containing two distinct van der Waals materials - monolayer WS2 and layered quasi-2D halide perovskites (HaPs) - separated by $1.5 μ\rm m$. The system forms three polariton branches, with the middle branch possessing nearly equal fractions of both excitons and the photonic mode. White-light reflectivity and luminescence measurements are in good agreement with simulations using a coupled harmonic oscillator and a microscopic Wannier-Hopfield framework. Our results lay the foundation to combine highly complementary degrees of freedom in 2D materials in an in-situ tunable fashion to enable new polaritonic functionalities.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper demonstrates that using structured light with Orbital Angular Momentum (OAM) allows for the direct, real-space writing of topological features in quantum materials like topological insulators. The topology forms a controllable annulus whose existence and geometry are independently tuned by the light's OAM number and helicity. This establishes OAM as a powerful control parameter for programmable topological landscapes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, optically controllable mechanism for engineering non-equilibrium topology in condensed matter systems, which is highly relevant for understanding light-matter interactions in topological materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To demonstrate that structured light carrying Orbital Angular Momentum (OAM) can write topological properties directly into real space within a material, moving beyond global control mechanisms.

**Main result.** The study shows that OAM enables the formation of a topological annulus in ultrathin topological insulators, with the topology being controllable by the light's helicity and OAM number.

**Method.** The analysis uses the leading Floquet–Magnus term to derive an effective Hamiltonian for a Dirac system driven by a circularly polarized Laguerre–Gaussian beam.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using quasi-two-dimensional Dirac systems realized in ultrathin Bi2Se3 films, driven by a time- and space-dependent optical field characterized by OAM ($\ell$) and circular polarization ($\hat{\epsilon}$).

**Key observables.** Topological annulus (defined by two concentric chiral ring modes), radial Floquet mass sign changes, and vortex-core zero modes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Orbital Angular Momentum quantum number ($\ell$), light helicity ($\hat{\epsilon}$), and the ratio of peak optical amplitude to critical amplitude ($A_{	ext{peak}}/A_c$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the off-resonant regime approximation ($\omega \gg \Delta_0$) and the use of the leading Floquet–Magnus term.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the formation and expansion of the topological annulus for different OAM values ($\ell$), show the dependence of the inner and outer ring radii on the driving field strength, and summarize the helicity-selective crossover frequencies.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical framework using the Floquet-Magnus expansion, analyzes the role of helicity in driving mass inversion versus gap opening, and then demonstrates how OAM independently controls the geometry of the resulting topological annulus.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Floquet engineering usually treats light as a uniform control field that changes the topology of an entire driven material. Here we show that structured light carrying orbital angular momentum (OAM) enables a different regime, in which topology is written directly in real space. For ultrathin topological insulator films, circularly polarized Laguerre--Gaussian beams generate a radial Floquet mass whose sign changes define a topological annulus bounded by two concentric chiral ring modes. The transition is helicity selective: below a thickness-dependent critical frequency, left-circularly polarized light drives mass inversion, whereas right-circularly polarized light increases the gap and leaves the film trivial. Independently, the OAM quantum number shifts and reshapes the annulus without changing the frequency, intensity, or helicity. In the decoupled-surfaces limit, the same mechanism produces a purely Floquet-induced topological mass and a vortex-core zero mode. These results identify photon OAM as a control parameter for nonequilibrium topology and provide a route to programmable topological landscapes in quantum materials.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a powerful 'edge chain' method to calculate the topologically protected edge and corner states in 2D topological insulators. By treating the boundary layer independently, the authors demonstrate that these states can be found using advanced mathematical tools applied to the analytically continued Bloch Hamiltonian. This provides a systematic approach for analyzing boundary physics in complex quantum materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous mathematical framework developed for finding boundary states in non-Hermitian, quasi-periodic systems is highly relevant to understanding topological phases in open quantum systems or engineered photonic/acoustic lattices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to determine the adiabatically protected edge and corner states in semi-infinite, two-dimensional topological insulators.

**Main result.** A novel 'edge chain' method is proposed, which successfully computes these boundary states by analyzing only the first layer of lattice sites, and the protection of these states is confirmed using Shemesh's theorem.

**Method.** The method involves analytically continuing the Bloch Hamiltonian to the complex plane, treating the boundary as an independent 'edge chain,' and applying conditions derived from the bulk structure.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on semi-infinite 2D topological insulators, using extensions of the Su-Schrieffer-Heeger (SSH) model, the Haldane model, and the Breathing Kagome Lattice as explicit examples.

**Key observables.** Edge and corner state wave functions, energy eigenvalues, and the dimension of the kernel space defining the boundary states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Chiral symmetry, the complex wave number $z$, and the hopping parameters ($t_1, t_2, t_3$) defining the lattice structure.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that edge states can be found by considering only the first layer ('edge chain') independently of the bulk, and that the connecting sites can be set to zero amplitude.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the 2D SSH model, labeling the 'edge chain' sites, and show visualizations of the model with alternative unit cells used to find different types of boundary states.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing the edge chain ansatz, using Shemesh's theorem to prove protection, and then applying this framework to specific models (SSH3, Haldane, Kagome) using advanced techniques like para-Hermiticity and analyzing exceptional points.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that it is possible to compute the adiabatically protected edge and corner states of semi-infinite, two-dimensional, topological insulators by considering the first layer of lattice sites, what we call the "edge chain," independently from the bulk. We start by accepting this claim as an ansatz, then, using the Shemesh theorem, we show that the edge states one finds using our procedure are adiabatically protected, provided we restrict ourselves to adiabatic evolutions that do not break chiral symmetry. We show explicit examples of our method in a 2D extension of the SSH model, the SSH3 model, the Haldane model and the Breathing Kagome Lattice.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a non-perturbative theory to calculate magnetic exchange couplings in half-filled bipartite magnetic lattices. By utilizing the Magnetic Force Theorem and circumventing issues related to zero-energy modes, the authors provide a universal analytical tool. This tool offers key design principles for engineering stable magnetic order in advanced 2D spintronic materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The development of a non-perturbative, universal framework for calculating magnetic exchange interactions in complex lattices is highly relevant for designing next-generation spintronic devices where precise control over long-range magnetic order is required.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To establish a non-perturbative generalization of magnetic exchange rules in half-filled bipartite networks, overcoming the limitations of traditional perturbative frameworks.

**Main result.** The derived non-perturbative formula for magnetic coupling is universal, valid for any bipartite graph, and successfully bypasses the problematic zero-energy mode (ZEM) calculations.

**Method.** The magnetic coupling is calculated using the Magnetic Force Theorem (MFT) and Green's functions, leading to a generalized, non-perturbative expression involving frequency integrals.

**Model / system.** The system is a magnetic bipartite graph (MBG) modeled by a Hamiltonian incorporating hopping terms ($t_{AB}$) and local Heisenberg exchange interactions ($J$). The analysis focuses on half-filled systems with localized magnetic moments on two sublattices (A and B).

**Key observables.** Magnetic couplings $J_{\lambda\lambda'}(R)$, generalized susceptibility $\chi_{\lambda\lambda'}^{ij}(\omega)$, and the existence/weight of Zero-Energy Modes (ZEMs).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Half-filling ($\Lambda$), the coupling strength $J_S$, and the lattice geometry (bipartite structure).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a ferrimagnetic ground state spin texture ($\mathbf{S}_A = +S e_z$, $\mathbf{S}_B = -S e_z$) and relies on the fact that ZEMs have zero weight at magnetic impurity sites.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the magnetic bipartite graph structure, the energy spectrum showing ZEMs, and the calculated magnetic couplings as a function of distance R, distinguishing between intra-sublattice (ferro) and inter-sublattice (antiferro) interactions.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the Hamiltonian and identifying the failure points of perturbative methods related to ZEMs. It then derives the non-perturbative coupling formula using MFT and Green's functions, culminating in the general proof for arbitrary bipartite graphs.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We break from perturbative frameworks to establish a non-perturbative generalization of magnetic exchange rules in half-filled bipartite networks. While historical models remain strictly tied to the debatable perturbation approach in the dilute limit and require the absence of zero-energy modes, our universal mechanism governs exchange interactions across arbitrary bipartite graphs. By bypassing traditional expansion bottlenecks, this framework provides an analytical predictive tool for magnetism in complex lattices, offering key design principles to engineer stable, long-range order in next-generation 2D spintronic devices

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a weak-coupling mean-field theory to map out the complex phase diagram of doped and biased Bernal bilayer graphene. By treating the system's interactions and external fields rigorously, the authors predict the existence of multiple fractional metallic states. The results provide quantitative agreement with recent experiments, advancing the understanding of correlated electronic behavior in 2D materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study of correlated phases in graphene systems, especially those exhibiting fractional metallic states, touches upon strongly correlated electron physics, which shares theoretical tools with many-body physics relevant to AMO systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper theoretically investigates the many-body electronic phases, particularly fractional metallic states, that emerge in doped and electrically biased Bernal-stacked (AB) bilayer graphene.

**Main result.** The study predicts a rich phase diagram hosting several fractional-metal states, reproducing key experimental features, including the energy scale for the loss of fractional metallic order.

**Method.** A variational mean-field theory is employed, utilizing a parameter-free Random-Phase Approximation for the short-range electron-electron interaction.

**Model / system.** The physical system is AB bilayer graphene subjected to doping and an external bias field. The model incorporates kinetic energy, a screened short-range Coulomb repulsion, and a long-range capacitive potential dictated by the displacement field D.

**Key observables.** Single-particle gap, nature of electronic phases (e.g., quarter-metal, half-metal, fractional metal), and the free energy.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Doping level, applied bias voltage, and the experimentally controlled displacement field D.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory uses a variational mean-field approach, and the long-range interaction is treated as a parallel-plate capacitor potential. The analysis also assumes the order parameter is Hermitian in some derivations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the phase diagram in the (doping, bias) plane, showing transitions between various metallic states, and show the variation of gap parameters with doping at fixed voltage.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the Hamiltonian, derives self-consistency equations for the order parameters using mean-field theory, calculates the free energy, and maps out the resulting phase diagram in terms of doping and bias.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The paper presents a theoretical study of many-body electronic phases in doped and electrically biased Bernal-stacked (AB) bilayer graphene. We develop a variational mean-field theory with no fitted parameters. For the electron-electron interaction, we employ a parameter-free random-phase approximation to model the short-range screened Coulomb repulsion. The remaining long-range Coulomb potential energy is dictated by the geometry of the sample, behaving as a parallel-plate capacitor. We formulate the theory directly in terms of the experimentally controlled displacement field $\frak D$ rather than the interaction-renormalized interlayer potential difference. It makes our theory better suited for direct comparison with experiment. The resulting phase diagram is quite rich. It hosts several fractional-metal states connected by first- and second-order transitions. At higher bias and doping, three distinct fractional metallic states emerge. We classify these phases by the number of doped sectors and the symmetry of the order parameters. Our results - obtained without any parameter fitting - reproduce key qualitative and quantitative features of recent experiments. This includes the energy scale associated with the loss of fractional metallic order. At lower bias and doping, the model stabilizes a broad spectrum of fractional-metal phases with more exotic symmetry-breaking patterns. This suggests that further experimental exploration of the latter regime is warranted.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study proposes a method to create and control a hierarchy of topological superconducting phases (FOTSC and SOTSC) in a heterostructure combining a QSHI and a p-wave magnet. By analyzing the BdG Hamiltonian and using topological invariants, the authors show that these distinct phases can be tuned by magnetic coupling, leading to the emergence of Majorana corner modes and unique edge states.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed theoretical roadmap for realizing and controlling multiple distinct topological superconducting states using engineered material heterostructures, which is highly relevant for topological quantum computation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to theoretically engineer and characterize the hierarchy of topological superconducting (TSC) phases, specifically first-order (FOTSC) and second-order (SOTSC), in a novel heterostructure.

**Main result.** The model successfully demonstrates the tunability of both FOTSC and SOTSC phases by adjusting the magnetic coupling parameter, leading to distinct topological signatures like Majorana corner modes (MCMs) and different edge mode behaviors.

**Method.** The analysis employs the Bogoliubov-de Gennes (BdG) Hamiltonian, utilizing symmetry invariants (winding numbers) and deriving low-energy edge theories to classify the topological phases.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a 2D heterostructure composed of a Quantum Spin Hall Insulator (QSHI), an unconventional p-wave magnet (pWM), and proximity-induced s-wave superconductivity. The system is modeled using the BdG Hamiltonian, which incorporates contributions from the QSHI and pWM.

**Key observables.** Topological phases (Trivial, FOTSC, SOTSC), 1D winding number, quadrupolar winding number, Majorana corner modes (MCMs), and Majorana flat/dispersive edge modes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The coupling parameter $J_{sd}$ (from the pWM) and the superconducting gap $\Delta_0$ are key tunability parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on deriving effective low-energy Hamiltonians by approximating trigonometric functions near the $\Gamma$ point and applying boundary conditions (OBC) for edge/corner mode analysis.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the eigenvalue spectra showing transitions between phases based on $J_{sd}$, and depict the localized MCMs in the SOTSC phase.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the full BdG model, deriving effective low-energy edge and bulk Hamiltonians, characterizing the topological invariants (winding numbers), and finally mapping out the phase diagram showing the emergence of FOTSC and SOTSC regimes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A theoretical framework is proposed to engineer both first and second-order topological superconducting phases in a two-dimensional (2D) heterostructure, consisting of a quantum spin Hall insulator (QSHI) and an unconventional $p$-wave magnet in presence of proximity-induced $s$-wave superconducting pairing. Our analysis establishes that the transitions between the trivial and topological superconducting (TSC) phases can be regulated though the parameters of $p$-wave magnet. Presence of chiral symmetry leads to the characterization of both types of TSC phases by the respective invariants, one-dimensional winding number and quadrupolar winding number. These results are supplemented by an analytical effective low-energy edge theory that yields a deeper insight into the emergence of the different topological phases of the system. Bulk pairing analysis reveals the competition between the effective $(p_x+p_y)$ and $(p_x+ip_y)$ type pairings that are governed by the intrinsic spin-orbit coupling inherited to the QSHI and spin-split bands of the $p$-wave magnet, respectively.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a comprehensive theory for magnetotransport in 2D electron systems by combining memory effects from localized disorder scattering with hydrodynamic viscosity. The model successfully explains the observed giant negative magnetoresistance in high-quality quantum wells, distinguishing between narrow and wide sample regimes based on the relative dominance of these two physical mechanisms.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed modeling of non-Markovian transport and the explicit separation of memory and hydrodynamic contributions provide a rich framework for understanding transport in disordered quantum systems, which is relevant to open quantum systems dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the puzzle of observing hydrodynamic-like transport signatures, such as giant negative magnetoresistance, in 2D electron systems even when electron-electron scattering is too weak to cause them conventionally.

**Main result.** The developed theory successfully explains the observed giant negative magnetoresistance by modeling the mixed transport regime involving both memory effects from localized defects and unconventional viscosity from bulk/edge scattering.

**Method.** The authors developed a theoretical model using phenomenological balance transport equations for two types of electrons (circling and traveling) and solved them in the stationary regime.

**Model / system.** The system is two-dimensional electron gas in high-quality nanostructures, such as GaAs quantum wells, subjected to weak disorder. The model incorporates memory effects and hydrodynamic viscosity arising from scattering mechanisms.

**Key observables.** Magnetoresistance ($ho_{xx}(B)$), exhibiting characteristic sharp peaks (narrow samples) or blunt maxima (wide samples) at zero magnetic field.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Sample width ($W$), magnetic field ($B$), temperature ($T$), and characteristic scattering/transition lengths ($l_{tc}, l_G$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model assumes the existence of two electron populations (c- and t-electrons) and uses approximations like the gradual channel approximation and stationary flow conditions.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the model's predictions for $ho_{xx}$ vs. $B$ (showing characteristic shapes) against experimental measurements from GaAs quantum wells, demonstrating agreement in both narrow and wide sample limits.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by formulating balance equations for electron densities and flows, deriving analytical expressions for $ho_{xx}$ in different sample width limits, and finally comparing these theoretical predictions across various regimes (low/high B, narrow/wide W) against experimental data.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The hydrodynamic regime of electron transport, induced by fast inter-electron collisions, was discovered in high-quality nanostructures in recent ten years. However, signs of hydrodynamic transport, primarily, the giant negative magnetoresistance, were observed even at very low temperatures, when electron-electron scattering is too weak to affect the transport. To address this puzzle, here we develop a theory of mixed, hydrodynamic and non-Markovian, magnetotransport of two-dimensional electrons at zero temperature in samples with weak but still important disorder. Namely, we account for both the memory effects at electron scattering by localized defects in magnetic field and an unconventional viscosity effect due to electron scattering by defects in bulk and by rough sample edges. Solution of the model yields a strong negative magnetoresistance, which exhibits at zero magnetic field a sharp maximum in narrower samples or a blunt maximum in wider samples. This and other our results explain various properties of the giant negative magnetoresistance observed on ultra-high-quality GaAs quantum wells, thereby we apparently reveal the nature of low-temperature magnetotransport in these systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work derives the conductivity matrix for 2D semi-metallic electron systems undergoing a topological phase transition. While the general prediction is non-quantized conductivity, the authors show that imposing an emergent rotational symmetry restores quantization, particularly for the anti-symmetric part of the matrix. This sheds light on the conditions under which transport properties become robust topological invariants.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous derivation of transport coefficients like the conductivity matrix at a critical point, especially linking it to topological invariants under specific symmetry assumptions, is highly relevant to understanding quantum transport in novel materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to derive an explicit formula for the entire conductivity matrix at a topological phase transition (TPT) in 2D non-interacting semi-metallic electron systems.

**Main result.** Generally, the conductivity matrix is not quantized, but universality is restored under the assumption of emergent rotational symmetry, leading to quantized values for the anti-symmetric part of the matrix.

**Method.** The analysis uses the Euclidean many-body formalism and the Kubo formula, calculating the critical conductivity by averaging results from both sides of the transition.

**Model / system.** The system is a general class of 2D non-interacting electron systems on a lattice, modeled by a Hamiltonian that exhibits semi-metallic behavior when two Bloch bands touch at conical intersections.

**Key observables.** The full conductivity matrix $\sigma$, specifically the longitudinal ($\sigma_{ii}$) and transverse ($\sigma_{12}$) conductivities.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The mass parameter $m$ controlling the TPT, the Fermi energy, and the external strain parameter.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the system is non-interacting and disorder-free, and for quantization results, it crucially assumes the existence of emergent discrete rotational invariance.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by first establishing the general formula for $\sigma(m)$ using the Euclidean formalism, then analyzing the limits at the TPT ($m=0$), and finally discussing specific physical models and the conditions required for quantization.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

For a general class of two-dimensional non-interacting semi-metallic electron systems on a lattice, we derive an explicit formula for the whole conductivity matrix, by using Euclidean many-body formalism. The semi-metallic phase takes place whenever two Bloch bands touch at the Fermi energy with conical intersections and generally occurs at the transition between distinct integer quantum Hall phases. Unlike the longitudinal conductivity (which depends solely on the shape of the cones at the Fermi level), the transverse conductivity is remarkably determined both by the conical structures of the bands and by the nature of the two nearby topological phases. Generically, in the semi-metallic state, neither the longitudinal nor the transverse conductivities are quantized in integer multiples of a universal conductivity quantum; however, universality of the conductivity matrix is restored under the assumption of emergent rotational symmetry of the linearized Hamiltonian at the Fermi points. Using our formula, we compute the conductivity matrix for several physically relevant models of quantum Hall fluids at the topological phase transition, exhibiting cases where the transverse conductivity is quantized in half-integer multiples of $e^2/h$ and cases where it depends continuously on an external strain parameter, thus shedding light on its universality or non-universality features.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work develops a detailed semiclassical theory to analyze the Orbital Edelstein Effect (OEE) in a Rashba 2DEG, specifically addressing the role of disorder. The key finding is that disorder-induced asymmetric scattering channels (side-jump and skew-scattering) can dominate the orbital magnetization, highlighting the crucial role of orbital degrees of freedom over spin degrees of freedom in this effect.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter transport, the rigorous symmetry analysis (T-odd vs. T-even contributions) and the explicit separation of scattering mechanisms provide a powerful theoretical template for analyzing non-equilibrium phenomena in quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the previously unclear contribution of disorder-induced asymmetric scattering mechanisms to the Orbital Edelstein Effect (OEE). It aims to provide a comprehensive theoretical decomposition of the total OEE.

**Main result.** The disorder-induced side-jump and skew-scattering mechanisms can significantly enhance the orbital magnetization, potentially exceeding the intrinsic contribution by an order of magnitude in a magnetized Rashba 2DEG.

**Method.** The authors developed a semiclassical Boltzmann theory, decomposing the total OAM polarization into five distinct channels corresponding to different scattering mechanisms (intrinsic, Drude, side-jump, and two skew-scattering orders).

**Model / system.** The minimal model used is a magnetized Rashba two-dimensional electron gas (2DEG) subjected to an external electric field and impurity disorder potential. The analysis is performed using the semiclassical Boltzmann equation.

**Key observables.** Orbital magnetization ($\delta L_
u$), Orbital Angular Momentum (OAM) polarization, and the susceptibility tensors quantifying the response in each scattering channel.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Rashba coupling strength (SOC), magnetic field strength, and the scattering rates derived from the impurity potential.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory operates in the weak-disorder limit and utilizes semiclassical approximations, including the small-momentum-transfer approximation for scattering calculations.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the four disorder-induced contributions to the OEE, while Figure 2 decomposes the total orbital magnetization into five distinct components based on scattering mechanisms.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from a general semiclassical Boltzmann framework, decomposing the collision integral into multiple scattering channels. It then analyzes the symmetry properties of these channels, culminating in quantitative results for the orbital magnetization in the Rashba 2DEG.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The generation and manipulation of orbital angular momentum (OAM) by an external electric field constitute one of the central themes of orbitronics. In particular, the electrically induced nonequilibrium OAM polarization, known as the orbital Edelstein effect (OEE), has attracted considerable attention in recent years. While the intrinsic band-geometric mechanism and the role of conventional symmetric impurity scattering in the OEE are well understood, the contribution from disorder-induced asymmetric scattering remains unclear. Here, we develop a semiclassical theory that separates the OEE into intrinsic, Drude, side-jump, and third- and fourth-order skew-scattering channels. Unlike the Drude channel, the intrinsic, side-jump, and skew-scattering responses survive only in systems with broken time-reversal symmetry. We find that in a magnetized Rashba two-dimensional electron gas (2DEG), these disorder-induced mechanisms can substantially exceed the intrinsic contribution. Remarkably, we find that for a system with Rashba coupling of $1$ $eV Å$, the orbital magnetization is about one order of magnitude larger than the spin magnetization for the chosen parameters, highlighting the crucial role of orbital degrees of freedom in the Edelstein effect.

</details>

**Summary.** This work systematically studies how Gaussian wave packets reshape when propagating in deep water, governed by nonlinear dispersive effects. By comparing lab experiments with numerical solutions of the NLSE and MNLSE, the authors map out regimes ranging from dispersive broadening to strong nonlinear focusing. The findings provide crucial guidance for controlling localized wave energy in various nonlinear physical systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical water waves, the underlying mathematical framework—the nonlinear Schrödinger equation and its higher-order corrections—is central to nonlinear wave physics relevant to quantum optics and nonlinear wave propagation in various media.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the nonlinear reshaping and evolution of Gaussian wave packets propagating in deep water.

**Main result.** The wave packet evolution depends critically on the balance between dispersion and nonlinearity, showing steady propagation when parameters match the NLSE soliton, but requiring higher-order corrections (MNLSE) in strongly focusing regimes.

**Method.** The study combines laboratory experiments measuring wave profiles with numerical simulations solving the Nonlinear Schrödinger Equation (NLSE) and its modified version (MNLSE).

**Model / system.** The physical system is deep-water surface waves, modeled by the wave envelope $A(x, t)$ governed by the NLSE or MNLSE, derived from the Euler equations.

**Key observables.** Wave packet evolution (broadening, focusing, steady propagation), envelope amplitude, and width.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionless amplitude factor $N$ (varied from 0.36 to 1.8) and the ratio of characteristic lengths $L_D/L_{NL}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The NLSE is an approximation valid for small steepness; the MNLSE is used when higher-order effects become significant during strong focusing.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the spatial evolution of Gaussian envelopes across different regimes (dispersion-dominated, quasi-stationary, focusing), comparing experimental data with NLSE and MNLSE predictions.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical problem, derives the governing equations (NLSE/MNLSE), details the methodology (experimental setup and numerical solvers), presents results across varying nonlinearity regimes, and concludes with acknowledgements.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The propagation of narrowband wave packets is a classical problem with broad relevance across nonlinear wave physics. Within the framework of the nonlinear Schrödinger equation, solitons are known to propagate without changing shape, whereas Gaussian envelopes may undergo substantial reshaping depending on the balance between dispersion and nonlinearity. Here, we combine laboratory experiments with numerical simulations to systematically investigate the evolution of hydrodynamic Gaussian wave packets over a wide range of amplitudes and spectral bandwidths. We show that when the envelope amplitude and width match those of the stationary NLSE soliton solution, the wave packet propagates steadily in the wave flume. More generally, by varying the amplitude of the Gaussian envelope, the wave packet may undergo either dispersive broadening or nonlinear focusing, resembling the dynamics of Satsuma-Yajima breathers. These findings may facilitate the controlled generation and manipulation of localized wave packets in a variety of nonlinear dispersive systems, including optics, plasmas, and Bose-Einstein condensates.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper explores vortex solitons in quasicrystals by introducing controlled topological defects called disclinations. By modifying the underlying symmetry of the quasicrystal structure, the authors demonstrate the creation of new, stable vortex soliton families. This expands the understanding of wave localization in complex, aperiodically structured photonic media.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges nonlinear optics, topological physics, and quasiperiodicity, showing how engineered defects can fundamentally alter the light-matter interaction and support robust, topologically protected wave states.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how introducing global topological defects, specifically disclinations, into quasicrystals affects the stable propagation of vortex-carrying light beams and vortex solitons.

**Main result.** The introduction of disclinations enriches the linear spectrum, enabling the existence of new, stable, thresholdless vortex solitons whose properties reflect the quasicrystal's discrete rotational symmetry.

**Method.** The analysis uses the nonlinear Schrödinger equation (NLSE) governing the light field, analyzing the linear spectrum and solving the nonlinear equation numerically.

**Model / system.** The system models light propagation in photonic quasicrystals, constructed by deforming Penrose tilings with controlled topological defects (disclinations) to achieve various discrete rotational symmetries ($	ext{C}
u$).

**Key observables.** Stable vortex soliton existence, soliton intensity/phase distributions, and the dependence of soliton stability intervals on the discrete rotational symmetry $	ext{C}
u$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Discrete rotational symmetry order ($
u$), the strength of the nonlinearity, and the topological defect structure.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the quasicrystals are large enough to exhibit the desired symmetries, and the study focuses on the evolution of localized vortex inputs in a pre-fabricated potential.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the construction of quasicrystals via disclinations for different symmetries ($	ext{C}_5, 	ext{C}_4, 	ext{C}_6, 	ext{C}_7$), show the linear spectra, and display the profiles and phase distributions of the resulting vortex states.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical model using the NLSE, details the method of generating defect-laden quasicrystals, analyzes the linear spectrum, and finally presents the findings regarding the bifurcation and stability of vortex solitons.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Being structures characterized by discrete rotational symmetry $\mathcal{C}_ν$ of order $ν$, photonic quasicrystals are capable to support stable propagation of linear vortex-carrying light beams and vortex solitons. However, the impact of discrete rotational symmetry $ν$ of quasicrystals on the properties of vortex light states was not investigated so far, as only the systems constructed using the simplest Penrose tiling or corresponding optically induced Penrose lattices were considered in this context. Here we propose a broad class of quasicrystals with global topological defects -- disclinations -- introduced into their structure that allows to produce new quasicrystalline structures with any desired order of discrete rotational symmetry from basic Penrose structure. Such global topological deformation substantially enriches linear spectrum of quasicrystals, allowing them to support new types of linear vortex states and bifurcating from them families of stable thresholdless vortex solitons with unusual intensity and phase distributions. We found two different classes of stable vortex solitons consisting of in-phase or out-of-phase pairs of closely located bright spots, with total intensity distribution reflecting particular discrete rotational symmetry of the quasicrystal with disclination. Remarkably, even low-charge vortex solitons can be stable in quasicrystals with disclinations, while stability intervals for them broaden with decrease of the discrete rotational symmetry $\mathcal{C}_ν$ of quasicrystal. Our results expand the theory of localization in quasicrystals to structures with global topological deformation, highlighting new prospects for robust transmission of power or information arising in these systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper provides a comprehensive theoretical framework to understand how magnetic flux noise couples to superconducting qubits by incorporating the full effects of Faraday's law. It demonstrates that this coupling imposes a fundamental, universal limit on the qubit quality factor, even in devices without explicit flux loops. The findings also establish geometric constraints, such as a 'safety distance,' that must be respected when scaling up quantum processors.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous, fundamental theoretical constraint on qubit performance based on electromagnetic coupling principles, which is crucial for designing next-generation quantum hardware.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Flux noise is a leading dephasing mechanism in superconducting qubits, but the microscopic understanding of its coupling to the device geometry is incomplete.

**Main result.** The work derives a general coupling framework showing that flux noise imposes a fundamental upper limit on the qubit quality factor, even for nominally non-flux-tunable qubits.

**Method.** The authors use a quantum geometric description of the Faraday effect to derive the coupling between generic magnetic sources and superconducting thin films, treating electric and magnetic fields together.

**Model / system.** The system involves superconducting qubits (like transmons) modeled as thin film structures interacting with time-varying magnetic dipoles or flux lines. The analysis distinguishes between the standard flux mode and a novel electromotive field (EMF) mode.

**Key observables.** Qubit quality factor (Q), dephasing/relaxation rates ($\Gamma_\phi, \Gamma_1$), and the flux power noise spectrum ($S_\Phi(\omega)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The predicted fundamental limit for Q is estimated between $10^8$ and $10^{10}$; the 'minimal safety distance' for flux lines is also derived.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes surface spins are the microscopic origin of $1/f$ flux noise and extends the formalism to include finite size effects from capacitor wings.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the coupling mechanism via Faraday's law, showing how induced EMF affects different qubit geometries (single vs. double junction) and distinguishing between flux and EMF modes.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the general coupling derivation based on the Faraday effect to specific applications in qubit loss mechanisms, culminating in quantitative predictions for the Q-factor limit and spatial constraints for flux lines.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Flux noise is unanimously recognised as a leading dephasing mechanism for flux-tunable superconducting qubits. However, our microscopic understanding remains incomplete, and basic effects like Faraday's law of induction have only very recently come into focus. Based on a quantum geometric description of the Faraday effect, we provide an in-depth derivation of the coupling of generic magnetic sources to thin film superconducting structures, under appropriate consideration of the device geometry. We apply the resulting framework to time-varying magnetic dipoles, describing surface or substrate spins, as well as current-carrying flux lines. We show that flux noise not only affects dephasing, but also provides a fundamental limit for the qubit quality factor - notably, even when the qubit contains no loops and is thus nominally not flux-tunable. Assuming surface spins as the origin for universal flux noise, we expect that this quality factor limit might be reached in the near term. For flux lines, we formulate a minimal safety distance to conserve the qubit performance, potentially constraining the scale-up of quantum hardware. This distance is boosted in the presence of large capacitor wings typical for transmons, due to a lensing of the electromotive field which is largely independent of Meissner screening.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a comprehensive spectral theory for quasiperiodic systems, overcoming the limitations of conventional band theory. It proposes an efficient spectral function method and defines 'Janus quantum numbers' to label eigenstates consistently across localization transitions. This framework allows for the calculation and understanding of energy spectra in complex, non-periodic quantum materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The development of a rigorous 'band theory' for quasiperiodic systems provides powerful tools for analyzing complex, non-periodic quantum materials, which is highly relevant for understanding engineered quantum matter.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The absence of translational symmetry in quasiperiodic systems invalidates conventional band theory, posing a central challenge in understanding their energy spectra.

**Main result.** The authors establish a unified spectral theory using a spectral function method and Janus quantum numbers, allowing for state definition and computation analogous to periodic systems.

**Method.** They develop an efficient spectral function method that calculates the spectral function A(k,ω) using a small truncated Hamiltonian matrix, bypassing full diagonalization.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on quasiperiodic systems, exemplified by the Aubry-André-Harper (AAH) model, which lacks translational symmetry.

**Key observables.** Spectral Function A(k,ω), Incommensurate Energy Bands (IEB) dispersion E(k), and Janus quantum numbers.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio of potential strength to hopping amplitude (e.g., V/t) determines the localization regime, and the system is analyzed in the thermodynamic limit.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The spectral function method converges via a successive locking of energy moments, allowing for exact thermodynamic-limit results without finite-size scaling.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the spectral function calculation, the convergence of energy moments via moment-locking ratios, and the relationship between k-space and real-space descriptions.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from identifying the failure of conventional band theory to proposing the IEB concept, introducing the spectral function method to calculate A(k,ω), and finally unifying the framework with Janus quantum numbers to create a complete spectral theory.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The absence of translational symmetry in quasiperiodic systems invalidates conventional band theory, posing the central challenge in the field. Building upon the incommensurate energy band (IEB) concept, we establish a unified spectral theory for quasiperiodic systems by introducing two key advances. First, we develop an efficient spectral function method that calculates $A(k,ω)$ using a small truncated Hamiltonian matrix, bypassing full diagonalization. It converges via a distinctive successive locking of energy moments, yielding exact thermodynamic-limit results without finite-size scaling. Second, we introduce that quasiperiodic eigenstates possess Janus quantum numbers: a single eigenstate, continuously tracked across localization transitions, carries dual labels in momentum and real space, which naturally reduce to the familiar Bloch momentum and band index in the commensurate limit. Together with IEB, these advances constitute a ``band theory'' for quasiperiodic systems, enabling us to define, compute, and label states with the same facility as in periodic ones.

</details>

**Summary.** This work provides a rigorous mathematical derivation showing that the BCS variational principle is exact for the ground-state energy density of a fermionic system with pairing interactions in the thermodynamic limit. By comparing the exact ground state energy to the BCS functional minimum, the authors establish a quantitative link between the Hamiltonian and its variational approximation. This result solidifies the theoretical foundation of BCS theory for condensed matter and quantum gas applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous mathematical proof of the BCS variational principle's exactness in the thermodynamic limit provides a powerful theoretical tool for analyzing pairing phenomena in fermionic systems, which is central to quantum gases research.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to rigorously derive the zero-temperature BCS functional from the reduced BCS Hamiltonian and prove that the BCS variational principle yields the exact ground-state energy density in the thermodynamic limit.

**Main result.** The authors prove that the finite-volume ground-state energy approaches the minimum of the BCS functional in the thermodynamic limit, establishing the exactness of the BCS approximation for the energy density.

**Method.** The derivation uses advanced techniques involving spectral localization, functional calculus, and bounding the difference between the exact ground state energy and the BCS variational energy using commutator identities.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled by spin-1/2 fermions in a periodic box with an attractive pairing interaction, governed by the reduced BCS Hamiltonian. The analysis compares the exact ground state energy $E_0$ to the BCS variational energy $E_{	ext{BCS}}$.

**Key observables.** Ground-state energy density ($e_0(\mu)$), BCS functional minimum ($e_{	ext{BCS}}(\mu)$), and the free Fermi sea energy density ($e_{	ext{FS}}(\mu)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Chemical potential ($\mu$), system volume ($L^3$), and the pairing coupling constant ($g$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The pairing interaction is assumed to be separable and supported in an energy shell $\Omega_\mu$. The proof relies on controlling error terms arising from spectral localization techniques.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops by first defining the finite-volume and infinite-volume BCS functionals, establishing an upper bound using a product state ansatz, and then deriving a rigorous lower bound by controlling fluctuation terms via spectral localization techniques.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We consider a grand-canonical reduced BCS Hamiltonian on the full fermionic Fock space with an attractive pairing interaction supported in an energy shell around the Fermi surface. For fixed chemical potential, we prove that the exact finite-volume ground-state energy differs from the minimum of the corresponding finite-volume BCS functional by a volume-independent error. As a consequence, the BCS variational principle is exact for the ground-state energy density in the thermodynamic limit.

</details>
