**Summary.** This work uses muon spin rotation ($\mu$SR) to investigate the magnetic state of infinite layer nickelates across varying levels of hole doping. The researchers found that local magnetic moments undergo spin freezing into a glassy state regardless of doping, suggesting that magnetism and superconductivity are largely independent phenomena. These findings help refine theoretical models describing correlated electron behavior in these novel superconducting materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The observation of glassy magnetic behavior persisting across the superconducting dome, suggesting decoupling from superconductivity, provides crucial input for models of strongly correlated electron systems, particularly those involving orbital degrees of freedom.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To characterize the doping-dependent magnetic behavior in infinite layer nickelates and uncover the relationship between local moments and superconductivity.

**Main result.** Local moments exhibit spin freezing into a glassy state at temperatures around tens of Kelvin, suggesting that magnetism and superconductivity are largely decoupled phenomena.

**Method.** Muon Spin Rotation (muSR) spectroscopy was used across a doping series to probe the magnetic ground state and temperature-dependent magnetic dynamics.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on infinite layer nickelates, specifically the (La,Sr)NiO2 doping series, which are contrasted with the parent compounds of superconducting cuprates.

**Key observables.** Zero-field (ZF) asymmetry spectra, the magnetic volume fraction (wTF), and the temperature-dependent depolarization rate ($\lambda_{TF}(T)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Doping level (Sr substitution $x=0\%$ to $25\%$), temperature (down to a few Kelvin), and the spin freezing temperature (tens of Kelvin).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the magnetic signal can be modeled by a stretched exponential function, and that the local moments persist independently of superconductivity.

**Figures summary.** Figures show ZF asymmetry spectra across doping levels and temperatures, and plots detail the temperature dependence of the magnetic volume fraction and the depolarization rate.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically measures magnetic properties (muSR) across the entire doping range, correlating the observed glassy magnetic behavior with the superconducting dome and the undoped parent compound.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The infinite layer nickelates are notable for their lack of long-range antiferromagnetic ordering, in contrast to the parent compounds of the superconducting cuprates. Instead, the nickelates show evidence of short-range glassy behavior in both the undoped and optimally-doped regimes, implying that local electronic moments exist independent of superconductivity. However, the systematic doping-dependent magnetic behavior is not yet fully resolved, and characterizing it could uncover the relationship between local moments and the superconducting dome. In this work, we use muon spin rotation ($μ$SR) on a (La,Sr)NiO$_2$ doping series from the undoped parent compound, through the superconducting dome, to the over-doped normal state (Sr substitution 0% $\leq$ x $\leq$ 25%) to probe the magnetic ground state and the temperature-dependent static and dynamic behavior. We find that local moments experience spin freezing into a glassy state at temperatures on the order of a few tens of kelvin regardless of the doping level. We also observe a subtle destabilization of the glassy state with increased hole doping. These observations suggest that magnetism and superconductivity are largely decoupled phenomena with indirect interactions described in a multi-orbital framework.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically analyzes Andreev reflection and conductance in semiconductor nanowires coupled to a superconductor. By controlling the relative direction of the spin-orbit field across the junction, the authors demonstrate a strong, electrically tunable control mechanism over electron transport. This suggests a pathway for realizing highly controllable superconducting quantum devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** The ability to use spin-orbit coupling direction as an electrical knob to control superconducting proximity effects and transport is highly relevant for building tunable quantum devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how the spin-orbit field direction can be used to tune Andreev reflection (AR) and nonlinear conductance in a hybrid normal-superconductor nanowire junction.

**Main result.** The AR and conductance are strongly tunable by misaligning the spin-orbit field between the normal and superconducting sides, offering an electrically controllable device.

**Method.** The analysis employs the Bogoliubov–de Gennes (BdG) equations and the scattering matrix method to calculate transport properties.

**Model / system.** The system is a normal-superconductor (N-S) junction realized in a proximitized semiconductor nanowire exposed to a magnetic field. The physics is modeled using a four-component Nambu spinor field governed by the BdG Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Andreev reflection (AR) coefficient ($\mathcal{R}_e$) and nonlinear conductance ($G$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The SO field misalignment angle ($\phi_{SO}$), the applied magnetic field ($b$), and the energy level ($E$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The SO field inhomogeneity is assumed to arise from differences in material deposition or screening between the N-side and S-side, and the model assumes a single-channel nanowire.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the physical setup, showing the N-S junction geometry and the relative orientation of the SO field vectors ($\alpha_N, \alpha_S$) and the magnetic field $b$.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the Hamiltonian in the BdG form, solves the resulting differential equations using the scattering matrix formalism, and analyzes the resulting transport coefficients based on the relative SO field angle.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the Andreev reflection (AR) and the nonlinear conductance in a normal-superconductor junction realized with a proximitized semiconductor nanowire exposed to a magnetic field along its axis. We show that, while at zero energy the AR coefficient is determined by the topological phase of the superconducting side of the junction, at finite energy the AR is strongly affected by the directions of the Rashba spin-orbit field characterizing the normal and the proximitized sides. The tunability of the AR and the nonlinear conductance as a function of the spin-orbit misalignment angle is particularly pronounced when the proximitized side is in the topologically trivial phase. The spin-orbit field direction is revealed to be an efficient knob to control the electron transport through the hybrid junction, realizing an electrically widely tunable device. The implementation in realistic setups is discussed.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study uses DFT to model B-N doped ZGNRs, showing that doping induces an internal electric field that controls the magnetic ground state between AF and FM. By combining doping design with an external electric field, the authors propose a robust, switchable prototype device capable of generating pure thermal spin current for spintronic applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** While primarily condensed matter physics, the concept of using electric fields to switch magnetic states (magnetoelectric coupling) and generate pure spin currents is highly relevant to open quantum systems and spintronics research.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how built-in electric fields, induced by B-N doping, modulate the spontaneous magnetism and resulting thermospin transport in Zigzag Graphene Nanoribbons (ZGNRs). The goal is to create a switchable device for pure thermal spin current generation.

**Main result.** By engineering the doping configuration and applying an external electric field, the system can switch between Antiferromagnetic (AF) and Ferromagnetic (FM) states, enabling the generation and switching of a pure thermal spin current at room temperature.

**Method.** The research relies on first-principle Density Functional Theory (DFT) calculations, using supercell models to simulate the electronic and magnetic properties.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a Zigzag Graphene Nanoribbon (ZGNR) periodically doped with Boron and Nitrogen atoms. The model explores the magnetic ground state (AF vs. FM) and the resulting spin-split band structure under applied electric fields.

**Key observables.** Total energy difference ($\Delta E$) between AF and FM states, band gaps, spin polarization distribution, and pure thermal spin current.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Doping configuration ($k_N, k_B$), external transversal static electric field strength, and temperature (room temperature operation).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The doping system maintains overall electrical neutrality, and the calculations use DFT with GGA-PBE functional approximations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the doped ZGNR structure, the dependence of energy differences ($\Delta E$) on doping geometry, and the resulting band structures for different magnetic states.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the magnetic ground state based on doping patterns, then examines how external electric fields further modulate the band structure and achieve the desired pure spin current switching functionality.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Zigzag-edged graphene nanoribbons (ZGNRs) host large magnetic moments at two zigzag edges due to spontaneous magnetism, whose ground state is antiferromagnetically coupled spin-order edge state (AF state). In this paper, the spontaneous magnetism and thermoelectricity of ZGNRs with periodic substituted doping of boron and nitrogen atoms along the axial direction are investigated by first principle calculation. The doping induces an internal electric field, which modulates the magnetic moments at the two zigzag edges and the band structure with spin splitting. For varying doping configuration, the ground state could be AF state, ferromagnetically coupled spin-order edge state (FM state), or degenerated pair of AF and FM states. External transversal static electric field can further modulate the magnetic moments and the band structure. By designing the doping configuration and the external field, pure thermal spin current with the absence of thermal charge current can be generated at room temperature, which can be switched on and off by flipping the magnetic configuration between AF and FM states. Thus, the doped ZGNRs can be applied as prototype devices for spin-caloritronic.

</details>

**Summary.** This work thoroughly characterizes a copper-based MOF that forms a glass upon heating. By using multiple advanced techniques, the researchers demonstrated that the resulting glass possesses exceptional mechanical robustness, achieving high stiffness and record-high fracture toughness for MOF glasses. This advances the field of processing and shaping MOF-derived materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the focus is on materials science, the study of glass formation and mechanical failure in complex, porous frameworks touches upon concepts relevant to disordered systems and amorphous materials physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To conduct a multimodal and multiscale interrogation of a mechanically tough, glass-forming copper-based Metal-Organic Framework (MOF).

**Main result.** The resulting MOF glasses exhibit an appreciably high elastic modulus (~10 GPa) and the highest fracture toughness (K_Ic ~ 0.5 MPa m^1/2) reported for MOF glasses.

**Method.** The study employed a combination of structural, thermal, and mechanical characterization techniques, including XRD, AFM, nanoindentation, and synchrotron pair distribution function analysis.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a copper-based MOF, Cu(Im)2, which is synthesized and subsequently melt-quenched into a glass state upon heating above 240 °C.

**Key observables.** Elastic modulus (E), fracture toughness (K_Ic), BET surface areas, and structural motifs observed via various microscopy and spectroscopy techniques.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Melting temperatures (e.g., 240 °C to 310 °C), quenching times, and mechanical stress/strain regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The mechanical properties are compared against literature examples, and the study focuses on the structural integrity of the framework upon glass formation.

**Figures summary.** Figures show images of as-synthesized crystals and melted glass samples, and supplementary figures detail R-STEM micrographs, THz spectroscopy, and nanoindentation results.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a comprehensive characterization approach, detailing structural analysis (XRD, microscopy, synchrotron methods), thermal analysis (TGA/DSC), and culminating in mechanical testing (nanoindentation, K_Ic measurement).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A copper-based metal-organic framework, Cu(Im)2, was synthesized using a sol-gel process and subsequently melt-quenched into glass upon heating above 240 °C. In this paper, we present a multimodal, multiscale interrogation of the MOF nanocrystals and the resulting glasses. Structural characterization using X-ray diffraction, atomic force microscopy, and electron microscopy was performed to understand the morphology and size of the synthesized nanocrystals and glasses. Thermogravimetric analysis and differential scanning calorimetry were employed to understand the melting process of the crystals to form the glass. Synchrotron pair distribution function analysis was performed to verify that the framework structure is maintained upon melting, and nearfield infrared nanospectroscopy provided insight into the local chemical structure of the materials. The mechanical characterization via nanoindentation revealed that the resulting glasses exhibit an appreciably high elastic modulus (~10 GPa) and the highest fracture toughness (K_1c ~ 0.5 MPa m^1/2) yet reported for MOF glasses. This development is central to the emerging field of MOF-based materials processing and shaping, while upholding mechanical robustness and resistance to cracking.

</details>
